## Volume 1, Chapter 1

57 items; 51 with a Lean name; 25 proved proved here; 26 in Mathlib; 6 not a result.


#### Section 1.1

- **1:§1.1 Definition (function) p16**, p.16 [CA-001]: A function from a set X to a set Y assigns to each input in X exactly one output in Y; the inputs form the domain and the outputs attained form the range. — *not a result* — –
- **1:§1.1 Zeros and intercepts p22**, p.22 [CA-002]: A zero of f is an input x with f(x)=0; the x-intercepts of the graph are the points (x,0) at the zeros, and the y-intercept is (0,f(0)). — *not a result* — –
- **1:§1.1 Rule: Vertical Line Test p22**, p.22 [CA-003]: A curve in the plane is the graph of a function exactly when every vertical line meets it at most once. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.vertical_line_test_book`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.vertical_line_test`
- **1:§1.1 Definition (increasing/decreasing) p25**, p.25 [CA-004]: Definition of increasing, strictly increasing, decreasing and strictly decreasing on an interval via comparing f(x1) and f(x2) for x1<x2. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.increasing_decreasing_defs`, `Monotone`, `StrictMono`, `Antitone`
- **1:§1.1 Combining Functions Using Mathematical Operations p26**, p.26 [CA-005]: Sum, difference, product and quotient of functions are defined pointwise (quotient where the denominator is nonzero). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.pointwise_operations`, `Pi.mul_apply`, `Pi.div_apply`
- **1:§1.1 Definition (composite function) p27**, p.27 [CA-006]: The composite (f∘g)(x)=f(g(x)) is defined on those x in the domain of g whose value lies in the domain of f. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.comp_apply_eq`
- **1:§1.1 Definition (even and odd functions) p30**, p.30 [CA-007]: A function is even if f(-x)=f(x) and odd if f(-x)=-f(x) for all x in the domain; even graphs are symmetric about the y-axis, odd graphs about the origin. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.even_odd_iff`, `Function.Even`, `Function.Odd`, `Function.Even.eq`
- **1:§1.1 Absolute value function p31**, p.31 [CA-008]: The absolute value \|x\| equals x for x≥0 and -x for x<0. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.abs_piecewise`, `abs_of_nonneg`, `abs_of_neg`
- **1:Example 1.11 p32**, p.32 [CA-008]: The absolute value \|x\| equals x for x≥0 and -x for x<0. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.abs_piecewise`, `abs_of_nonneg`, `abs_of_neg`

#### Section 1.2

- **1:§1.2 Definition (slope) p39**, p.39 [CA-009]: The slope of the line through (x1,y1) and (x2,y2) is the ratio of the change in y to the change in x, (y2-y1)/(x2-x1), and a linear function f(x)=mx+b has slope m. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.slope_ratio_and_linear`, `slope`
- **1:§1.2 Definition (equations of a line) p40**, p.40 [CA-010]: Point-slope form y-y0=m(x-x0), slope-intercept form y=mx+b, and standard form ax+by=c (a,b not both zero) describe a line. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.line_equation_forms`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.pointSlope_iff_slopeIntercept`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.standardForm_iff_slopeIntercept`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.standardForm_vertical`
- **1:§1.2 Polynomial function (definition in text) p42**, p.42 [CA-011]: A polynomial function is a finite sum of coefficient times power of x; its degree is the top exponent with nonzero coefficient, called leading coefficient; degree 2 and 3 are quadratic and cubic. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.polynomial_eval_as_sum`, `Polynomial`, `Polynomial.degree`, `Polynomial.leadingCoeff`
- **1:§1.2 end behavior of polynomial p43**, p.43 [CA-012]: For a polynomial of positive degree, behavior as x tends to infinity and negative infinity is governed by the sign of the leading coefficient and parity of the degree. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.polynomial_end_behavior`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.polynomial_tendsto_atBot_atTop`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.polynomial_tendsto_atBot_atBot`, `Polynomial.tendsto_atTop_of_leadingCoeff_nonneg`
- **1:§1.2 Rule: The Quadratic Formula p44**, p.44 [CA-013]: The solutions of ax²+bx+c=0 (a≠0) are x=(-b±√(b²-4ac))/(2a). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.quadratic_formula`, `quadratic_eq_zero_iff`
- **1:§1.2 Algebraic and transcendental functions, piecewise-defined functions p48-51**, p.48 [CA-014]: Rational functions are quotients of polynomials; algebraic functions come from polynomials by algebraic operations and roots; other functions are transcendental; a piecewise-defined function uses different formulas on different parts of its domain. — *not a result* — –
- **1:Example 1.19 p51**, p.51 [CA-014]: Rational functions are quotients of polynomials; algebraic functions come from polynomials by algebraic operations and roots; other functions are transcendental; a piecewise-defined function uses different formulas on different parts of its domain. — *not a result* — –
- **1:§1.2 Transformations of Functions p53-57**, p.53 [CA-015]: Replacing f(x) by f(x)+c, f(x+c), a·f(x), f(bx), -f(x), f(-x) shifts, stretches, compresses or reflects the graph in the standard ways. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.graph_transformations`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.fnGraph_add_const`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.fnGraph_comp_add_const`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.fnGraph_const_mul`
- **1:Example 1.21 p57**, p.57 [CA-015]: Replacing f(x) by f(x)+c, f(x+c), a·f(x), f(bx), -f(x), f(-x) shifts, stretches, compresses or reflects the graph in the standard ways. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.graph_transformations`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.fnGraph_add_const`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.fnGraph_comp_add_const`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.fnGraph_const_mul`

#### Section 1.3

- **1:§1.3 Radian measure p61-62**, p.61 [CA-016]: Radian measure of an angle is the length of the arc it cuts on the unit circle; arc length on radius r is rθ; 180 degrees equals π radians. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.radian_measure`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.circleArcLength`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.radiansOfDegrees`
- **1:§1.3 Definition (trigonometric functions) p63**, p.63 [CA-017]: For a point (x,y) on the unit circle at angle θ, sinθ=y, cosθ=x and tanθ=y/x, with cscθ, secθ, cotθ the reciprocals of sin, cos, tan (reciprocal identities). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.trig_reciprocal_identities`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.reciprocal_identities`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.sec_mul_cos`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.csc_mul_sin`
- **1:§1.3 Trigonometric functions properties p64-65**, p.64 [CA-018]: Sine and cosine have period 2π, tangent has period π; sine is odd and cosine is even. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.trig_periodicity_parity`, `Real.sin_periodic`, `Real.cos_periodic`, `Real.tan_periodic`
- **1:§1.3 Rule: Trigonometric Identities (reciprocal identities) p66**, p.66 [CA-017]: For a point (x,y) on the unit circle at angle θ, sinθ=y, cosθ=x and tanθ=y/x, with cscθ, secθ, cotθ the reciprocals of sin, cos, tan (reciprocal identities). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.trig_reciprocal_identities`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.reciprocal_identities`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.sec_mul_cos`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.csc_mul_sin`
- **1:§1.3 Rule: Trigonometric Identities (Pythagorean) p66**, p.66 [CA-019]: sin²θ+cos²θ=1, 1+tan²θ=sec²θ, 1+cot²θ=csc²θ. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.pythagorean_identities`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.sin_sq_add_cos_sq'`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.one_add_tan_sq`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.one_add_cot_sq`
- **1:§1.3 Rule: Trigonometric Identities (addition and subtraction) p66**, p.66 [CA-020]: sin(α±β)=sinα cosβ ± cosα sinβ and cos(α±β)=cosα cosβ ∓ sinα sinβ. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.trig_addition_subtraction`, `Real.sin_add`, `Real.sin_sub`, `Real.cos_add`
- **1:§1.3 Rule: Trigonometric Identities (double-angle) p66**, p.66 [CA-021]: sin2θ=2 sinθ cosθ and cos2θ=cos²θ-sin²θ=2cos²θ-1=1-2sin²θ. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.trig_double_angle`, `Real.sin_two_mul`, `Real.cos_two_mul`, `Real.cos_two_mul'`
- **1:Example 1.27 p69**, p.69 [CA-022]: The function A sin(B(x-α))+d has amplitude \|A\|, period 2π/\|B\|, phase shift α and vertical shift d. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.generalized_sine_amplitude_period`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.generalized_sin_periodic`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.generalized_sin_range`, `Real.sin_periodic`

#### Section 1.4

- **1:§1.4 Existence of an inverse function p73**, p.73 [CA-025]: A function has an inverse exactly when it is one-to-one (onto its range). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.injective_has_inverse_on_range`, `Function.Injective.hasLeftInverse`, `Function.bijective_iff_has_inverse`, `Function.leftInverse_invFun`
- **1:§1.4 Definition (inverse function) p74**, p.74 [CA-023]: The inverse f⁻¹ of f satisfies f⁻¹(f(x))=x on the domain of f and f(f⁻¹(y))=y on the range of f, with domain and range exchanged. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.inverse_function_iff_bijective`, `Function.bijective_iff_has_inverse`
- **1:§1.4 Definition (one-to-one) p74**, p.74 [CA-024]: A function is one-to-one if distinct inputs give distinct outputs. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.injective_iff_distinct_outputs`, `Set.InjOn`
- **1:§1.4 Rule: Horizontal Line Test p74**, p.74 [CA-026]: A function is one-to-one exactly when every horizontal line meets its graph at most once. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.horizontal_line_test_book`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.horizontal_line_test`
- **1:§1.4 Problem-Solving Strategy (finding an inverse) p77**, p.77 [CA-023]: The inverse f⁻¹ of f satisfies f⁻¹(f(x))=x on the domain of f and f(f⁻¹(y))=y on the range of f, with domain and range exchanged. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.inverse_function_iff_bijective`, `Function.bijective_iff_has_inverse`
- **1:§1.4 Graphing Inverse Functions p77**, p.77 [CA-027]: The graph of f⁻¹ is the reflection of the graph of f across the line y=x. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.graph_of_inverse_is_reflection`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.graph_inverse_eq_swap`
- **1:§1.4 Restricting the domain p78**, p.78 [CA-028]: A function that is not one-to-one can be made invertible by restricting it to a subset of its domain on which it is one-to-one, for example where it is monotone. — *not a result* — –
- **1:Example 1.30 p78**, p.78 [CA-027]: The graph of f⁻¹ is the reflection of the graph of f across the line y=x. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.graph_of_inverse_is_reflection`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.graph_inverse_eq_swap`
- **1:Example 1.31 p79**, p.79 [CA-028]: A function that is not one-to-one can be made invertible by restricting it to a subset of its domain on which it is one-to-one, for example where it is monotone. — *not a result* — –
- **1:§1.4 Definition (inverse trigonometric functions) p81**, p.81 [CA-029]: arcsin, arccos, arctan, arccot, arcsec, arccsc are the inverses of sin, cos, tan, cot, sec, csc restricted to principal domains. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.inverse_trig_principal_ranges`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.arccot_mem_Ioo`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.arcsec_mem`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.arccsc_mem`
- **1:§1.4 Inverse trigonometric functions p82**, p.82 [CA-030]: sin(arcsin x)=x on [-1,1], arcsin(sin x)=x on [-π/2,π/2], and similarly for cos, tan and the other inverse trigonometric functions. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.inverse_trig_cancellation`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.sin_arcsin'`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.arcsin_sin'`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.cos_arccos'`

#### Section 1.5

- **1:§1.5 Exponential functions p88-91**, p.88 [CA-031]: For b>0, b≠1, the exponential b^x is increasing when b>1 and decreasing when 0<b<1. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.exponential_monotone`, `Real.rpow_lt_rpow_of_exponent_lt`, `Real.rpow_lt_rpow_of_exponent_gt`, `Real.strictMono_rpow_of_base_gt_one`
- **1:§1.5 Rule: Laws of Exponents p90**, p.90 [CA-032]: Laws of exponents: b^x b^y=b^(x+y), b^x/b^y=b^(x-y), (b^x)^y=b^(xy), (ab)^x=a^x b^x, (a/b)^x=a^x/b^x. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.laws_of_exponents`, `Real.rpow_add`, `Real.rpow_sub`, `Real.rpow_mul`
- **1:Example 1.34 p90**, p.90 [CA-032]: Laws of exponents: b^x b^y=b^(x+y), b^x/b^y=b^(x-y), (b^x)^y=b^(xy), (ab)^x=a^x b^x, (a/b)^x=a^x/b^x. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.laws_of_exponents`, `Real.rpow_add`, `Real.rpow_sub`, `Real.rpow_mul`
- **1:§1.5 The number e, natural exponential function p91-92**, p.91 [CA-033]: The number e is the limit of (1+1/m)^m, and the natural exponential function is e^x. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.number_e_and_natural_exponential`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.tendsto_one_add_inv_pow_exp`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.exp_one_rpow_eq`, `Real.tendsto_one_add_div_pow_exp`
- **1:§1.5 Logarithmic functions p92-93**, p.92 [CA-036]: log_b is the inverse of b^x: log_b(b^x)=x, b^(log_b y)=y for y>0, and y=ln x exactly when e^y=x. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.logarithm_inverse`, `Real.logb_rpow`, `Real.rpow_logb`, `Real.logb_eq_iff_rpow_eq`
- **1:§1.5 Rule: Properties of Logarithms p94**, p.94 [CA-040]: log_b(xy)=log_b x+log_b y, log_b(x/y)=log_b x-log_b y, log_b(x^r)=r log_b x. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.logarithm_properties`, `Real.logb_mul`, `Real.logb_div`, `Real.logb_pow`
- **1:§1.5 Rule: Change-of-Base Formulas p95**, p.95 [CA-041]: Change of base: log_b x=ln x/ln b, log_a x=log_b x / log_b a, and b^x=e^(x ln b). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.change_of_base_formulas`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.logb_eq_log_div_log'`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.logb_change_base`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.rpow_eq_exp_mul_log`
- **1:Example 1.37 p95**, p.95 [CA-040]: log_b(xy)=log_b x+log_b y, log_b(x/y)=log_b x-log_b y, log_b(x^r)=r log_b x. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.logarithm_properties`, `Real.logb_mul`, `Real.logb_div`, `Real.logb_pow`
- **1:Example 1.38 p96**, p.96 [CA-041]: Change of base: log_b x=ln x/ln b, log_a x=log_b x / log_b a, and b^x=e^(x ln b). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.change_of_base_formulas`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.logb_eq_log_div_log'`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.logb_change_base`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.rpow_eq_exp_mul_log`
- **1:§1.5 Definition (hyperbolic functions) p98**, p.98 [CA-042]: sinh x=(e^x-e^(-x))/2, cosh x=(e^x+e^(-x))/2, and tanh, sech, csch, coth are the corresponding ratios. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.hyperbolic_functions_def`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.sinh_eq_exp`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.cosh_eq_exp`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.tanh_eq_exp`
- **1:§1.5 Rule: Identities Involving Hyperbolic Functions p100**, p.100 [CA-043]: Hyperbolic identities: cosh+sinh=e^x, cosh-sinh=e^(-x), cosh²-sinh²=1, 1-tanh²=sech², coth²-1=csch², and addition and double-angle formulas for sinh and cosh. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.hyperbolic_identities`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.cosh_add_sinh`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.cosh_sub_sinh_eq`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.cosh_sq_sub_sinh_sq_eq`
- **1:§1.5 Definition (inverse hyperbolic functions) p101**, p.101 [CA-044]: arsinh x=ln(x+√(x²+1)), arcosh x=ln(x+√(x²-1)) for x≥1, artanh x=½ln((1+x)/(1-x)) for \|x\|<1, and similar formulas for the reciprocal ones. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.inverse_hyperbolic_functions_def`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.arsinh_eq_log`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.arcosh_eq_log`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.artanh_eq_log`

## Volume 1, Chapter 2

58 items; 54 with a Lean name; 11 proved proved here; 43 in Mathlib; 4 not a result.


#### Section 2.1

- **1:§2.1 Definition (secant line) p115**, p.115 [CA-046]: The secant line to f through (a,f(a)) and (x,f(x)) has slope (f(x)-f(a))/(x-a). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C2.secant_slope`, `slope`
- **1:§2.1 tangent line (definition in text) p116**, p.116 [CA-047]: The tangent line to f at a is the line that the secant lines through (a,f(a)) approach, and its slope is the limit of the secant slopes (the derivative). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C2.tangent_slope_is_limit_of_secants`, `hasDerivAt_iff_tendsto_slope`
- **1:§2.1 Definition (instantaneous velocity) p118**, p.118 [CA-048]: The instantaneous velocity at time a is the value the average velocities over shrinking intervals around a approach, if it exists. — *not a result* — –

#### Section 2.2

- **1:§2.2 Definition (intuitive definition of a limit) p125**, p.125 [CA-049]: Informally, lim f(x) = L as x→a when f(x) is as close to L as desired for x sufficiently close to a (x≠a), f defined near a. — *not a result* — –
- **1:Theorem 2.1 p130**, p.130 [CA-050]: lim x = a and lim c = c as x→a, for constants a and c. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C2.limit_id_const`, `Filter.tendsto_id`, `tendsto_const_nhds`
- **1:§2.2 Definition (one-sided limits) p132**, p.132 [CA-051]: The limit from the left (right) of f at a is L if f(x) approaches L for x<a (x>a) approaching a. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C2.one_sided_limit_def`, `Filter.Tendsto`, `nhdsWithin`
- **1:Theorem 2.2 p133**, p.133 [CA-052]: lim f(x)=L as x→a exactly when both one-sided limits exist and equal L. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C2.limit_iff_one_sided_limits`, `nhdsLT_sup_nhdsGT`, `Filter.tendsto_sup`

- **1:§3.1 Definition (difference quotient, with increment) p.197**, p.197 [CA-084]: The slope of the secant line through $(a,f(a))$ and $(x,f(x))$ is the difference quotient $\frac{f(x)-f(a)}{x-a}$, or with increment $h$, $\frac{f(a+h)-f(a)}{h}$. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C3.difference_quotient_increment`, `slope`

## Volume 1, Chapter 4

#### Section 4.1

- **1:§4.1 Definition (related rates) p.304**, p.304 [CA-132]: Two quantities changing in time that are linked by an equation have related rates: differentiating the equation (chain rule) links their time derivatives. — *not a result* — –

#### Section 4.2

- **1:§4.2 Linear approximation (linearization, tangent line approximation) p.316**, p.316 [CA-133]: If f is differentiable at a, then f(x) is approximated near a by the tangent-line (linearization) L(x)=f(a)+f'(a)(x-a), with error o(x-a). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.linearization_error_isLittleO`, `hasDerivAt_iff_isLittleO`
- **1:Example 4.7 p.319**, p.319 [CA-134]: Special case of the linearization: (1+x)^n is approximately 1+nx for small x, used to estimate roots and powers of numbers near 1. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.linearization_one_add_rpow`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_one_add_rpow`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.one_add_rpow_isLittleO`, `Real.hasDerivAt_rpow_const`
- **1:§4.2 Definition (differentials) p.320**, p.320 [CA-135]: For y=f(x), the differential dx is an independent variable and dy is defined as f'(x) dx. — *not a result* — –
- **1:§4.2 Differentials and approximating change p.321**, p.321 [CA-136]: For small dx, the actual change Δy=f(x+dx)-f(x) is approximately dy=f'(x)dx, so a measurement error dx propagates to an error about f'(x)dx in the computed quantity. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.change_sub_differential_isLittleO`, `hasDerivAt_iff_isLittleO`
- **1:§4.2 Propagated error p.322**, p.322 [CA-136]: For small dx, the actual change Δy=f(x+dx)-f(x) is approximately dy=f'(x)dx, so a measurement error dx propagates to an error about f'(x)dx in the computed quantity. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.change_sub_differential_isLittleO`, `hasDerivAt_iff_isLittleO`
- **1:§4.2 Definition (relative and percentage error) p.323**, p.323 [CA-137]: The relative error of a quantity with absolute error Δq is Δq divided by the true value q; percentage error is this ratio times 100. — *not a result* — –

#### Section 4.3

- **1:§4.3 Definition (absolute extrema) p.326**, p.326 [CA-138]: f has an absolute maximum (minimum) at c in its domain D if f(c) >= f(x) (f(c) <= f(x)) for all x in D. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.absolute_max_iff`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.absolute_min_iff`, `IsMaxOn`, `IsMinOn`
- **1:Theorem 4.1 p.327**, p.327 [CA-139]: A continuous function on a closed bounded interval [a,b] attains an absolute maximum and an absolute minimum there. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.extreme_value_theorem`, `IsCompact.exists_isMaxOn`, `IsCompact.exists_isMinOn`
- **1:§4.3 Definition (local extrema) p.328**, p.328 [CA-140]: f has a local maximum (minimum) at c if f(c) >= f(x) (f(c) <= f(x)) for all x in some open interval around c in the domain; local extremum means either. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.local_max_iff`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.local_min_iff`, `IsLocalMax`, `IsLocalMin`
- **1:Theorem 4.2 p.329**, p.329 [CA-141]: If f has a local extremum at an interior point c and is differentiable at c, then f'(c)=0. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.fermat_local_extremum`, `IsLocalExtr.hasDerivAt_eq_zero`, `IsLocalMax.hasDerivAt_eq_zero`, `IsLocalMin.hasDerivAt_eq_zero`
- **1:§4.3 Definition (critical point) p.329**, p.329 [CA-142]: A critical point of f is an interior point c of the domain where f'(c)=0 or f'(c) does not exist. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.critical_point_def`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsCriticalPoint1`
- **1:Theorem 4.3 p.332**, p.332 [CA-143]: For continuous f on [a,b], each absolute extremum occurs at an endpoint or at a critical point in (a,b). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.extrema_at_endpoint_or_critical_point`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.isMaxOn_Icc_endpoint_or_critical`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.isMinOn_Icc_endpoint_or_critical`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.exists_extrema_endpoint_or_critical`
- **1:§4.3 Problem-Solving Strategy p.332**, p.332 [CA-143]: For continuous f on [a,b], each absolute extremum occurs at an endpoint or at a critical point in (a,b). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.extrema_at_endpoint_or_critical_point`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.isMaxOn_Icc_endpoint_or_critical`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.isMinOn_Icc_endpoint_or_critical`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.exists_extrema_endpoint_or_critical`
- **1:Theorem 4.4 p.338**, p.338 [CA-144]: If f is continuous on [a,b], differentiable on (a,b) and f(a)=f(b), then f'(c)=0 for some c in (a,b). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.rolle`, `exists_deriv_eq_zero`, `exists_hasDerivAt_eq_zero`
- **1:Theorem 4.5 p.341**, p.341 [CA-145]: If f is continuous on [a,b] and differentiable on (a,b), then f'(c)=(f(b)-f(a))/(b-a) for some c in (a,b). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.mean_value_theorem`, `exists_deriv_eq_slope`, `exists_hasDerivAt_eq_slope`
- **1:Theorem 4.6 p.344**, p.344 [CA-146]: If f'(x)=0 for all x in an interval, then f is constant on the interval. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.constant_of_deriv_zero`, `is_const_of_deriv_eq_zero`, `IsOpen.is_const_of_deriv_eq_zero`, `constant_of_derivWithin_zero`
- **1:Theorem 4.7 p.344**, p.344 [CA-147]: If f'=g' on an interval then f-g is constant there (and so any two antiderivatives of a function on an interval differ by a constant). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.eq_add_const_of_deriv_eq`, `IsOpen.exists_eq_add_of_deriv_eq`, `IsOpen.eqOn_of_deriv_eq`
- **1:Corollary 1 p.344**, p.344 [CA-146]: If f'(x)=0 for all x in an interval, then f is constant on the interval. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.constant_of_deriv_zero`, `is_const_of_deriv_eq_zero`, `IsOpen.is_const_of_deriv_eq_zero`, `constant_of_derivWithin_zero`
- **1:Corollary 2 p.344**, p.344 [CA-147]: If f'=g' on an interval then f-g is constant there (and so any two antiderivatives of a function on an interval differ by a constant). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.eq_add_const_of_deriv_eq`, `IsOpen.exists_eq_add_of_deriv_eq`, `IsOpen.eqOn_of_deriv_eq`
- **1:Theorem 4.8 p.345**, p.345 [CA-148]: If f'>0 on an interval then f is increasing there; if f'<0 then f is decreasing there. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.strictMonoOn_of_deriv_pos_Ioo`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.strictAntiOn_of_deriv_neg_Ioo`, `strictMonoOn_of_deriv_pos`, `strictAntiOn_of_deriv_neg`
- **1:Corollary 3 p.345**, p.345 [CA-148]: If f'>0 on an interval then f is increasing there; if f'<0 then f is decreasing there. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.strictMonoOn_of_deriv_pos_Ioo`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.strictAntiOn_of_deriv_neg_Ioo`, `strictMonoOn_of_deriv_pos`, `strictAntiOn_of_deriv_neg`

#### Section 4.5

- **1:§4.5 Problem-Solving Strategy p.349**, p.349 [CA-149]: For continuous f with critical point c, if f' changes from + to - at c then f has a local maximum there, from - to + a local minimum, and with no sign change no local extremum. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.first_derivative_test`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.first_derivative_test_max`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.first_derivative_test_min`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.first_derivative_test_no_change_pos`
- **1:Theorem 4.9 p.349**, p.349 [CA-149]: For continuous f with critical point c, if f' changes from + to - at c then f has a local maximum there, from - to + a local minimum, and with no sign change no local extremum. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.first_derivative_test`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.first_derivative_test_max`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.first_derivative_test_min`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.first_derivative_test_no_change_pos`
- **1:§4.5 Definition (concave up, concave down) p.352**, p.352 [CA-150]: A differentiable f is concave up (down) on an interval if f' is increasing (decreasing) there. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.concave_up_down_def`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ConcaveUpOn`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ConcaveDownOn`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ConcaveUpOn.strictConvexOn`
- **1:§4.5 Definition (inflection point) p.353**, p.353 [CA-152]: A point (c,f(c)) is an inflection point of f if f is continuous at c and the concavity of f changes at c. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.inflection_point_def`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsInflectionPoint`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.isInflectionPoint_of_deriv2_sign_change`
- **1:Theorem 4.10 p.353**, p.353 [CA-151]: If f''>0 on an interval then f is concave up there; if f''<0 then concave down. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.concave_up_of_second_deriv_pos`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.concave_down_of_second_deriv_neg`, `strictConvexOn_of_deriv2_pos`, `strictConcaveOn_of_deriv2_neg`
- **1:Theorem 4.11 p.356**, p.356 [CA-153]: If f'(c)=0 and f'' is continuous near c: f''(c)>0 gives a local minimum, f''(c)<0 a local maximum, f''(c)=0 is inconclusive. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.second_derivative_test_min`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.second_derivative_test_max`, `isLocalMin_of_deriv_deriv_pos`, `isLocalMax_of_deriv_deriv_neg`

#### Section 4.6

- **1:§4.6 Definition (informal limit at infinity) p.363**, p.363 [CA-154]: lim_{x->infinity} f(x)=L (and at minus infinity) means f(x) gets within any epsilon of L once x is large enough (resp. negative enough): for all eps there is N with \|f(x)-L\|<eps for all x>N. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.tendsto_atTop_nhds_iff_eps_N`, `Filter.Tendsto`, `Metric.tendsto_nhds`, `Filter.eventually_atTop`
- **1:§4.6 Definition (horizontal asymptote) p.363**, p.363 [CA-155]: The line y=L is a horizontal asymptote of f if the limit of f at plus or minus infinity equals L. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.horizontal_asymptote_def`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsHorizontalAsymptote`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.isHorizontalAsymptote_inv`
- **1:§4.6 Limit laws remark p.364**, p.364 [CA-157]: The algebraic limit laws and the squeeze theorem also hold for limits at plus or minus infinity. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.tendsto_add_atTop`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.tendsto_mul_atTop`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.squeeze_atTop`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.squeeze_atBot`
- **1:§4.6 Definition (formal limit at infinity) p.367**, p.367 [CA-154]: lim_{x->infinity} f(x)=L (and at minus infinity) means f(x) gets within any epsilon of L once x is large enough (resp. negative enough): for all eps there is N with \|f(x)-L\|<eps for all x>N. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.tendsto_atTop_nhds_iff_eps_N`, `Filter.Tendsto`, `Metric.tendsto_nhds`, `Filter.eventually_atTop`
- **1:§4.6 Definition (informal infinite limit at infinity) p.367**, p.367 [CA-156]: lim_{x->infinity} f(x)=infinity means f(x) exceeds any bound M once x is large enough (similarly for -infinity and x->-infinity). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.tendsto_atTop_atTop_iff_bound`, `Filter.tendsto_atTop_atTop`, `Filter.Tendsto`
- **1:§4.6 Definition (formal infinite limit at infinity) p.368**, p.368 [CA-156]: lim_{x->infinity} f(x)=infinity means f(x) exceeds any bound M once x is large enough (similarly for -infinity and x->-infinity). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.tendsto_atTop_atTop_iff_bound`, `Filter.tendsto_atTop_atTop`, `Filter.Tendsto`
- **1:Example 4.22 p.368**, p.368 [CA-154]: lim_{x->infinity} f(x)=L (and at minus infinity) means f(x) gets within any epsilon of L once x is large enough (resp. negative enough): for all eps there is N with \|f(x)-L\|<eps for all x>N. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.tendsto_atTop_nhds_iff_eps_N`, `Filter.Tendsto`, `Metric.tendsto_nhds`, `Filter.eventually_atTop`
- **1:Example 4.23 p.369**, p.369 [CA-156]: lim_{x->infinity} f(x)=infinity means f(x) exceeds any bound M once x is large enough (similarly for -infinity and x->-infinity). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.tendsto_atTop_atTop_iff_bound`, `Filter.tendsto_atTop_atTop`, `Filter.Tendsto`
- **1:§4.6 End behavior of power functions p.370**, p.370 [CA-158]: For a power x^n (n a positive integer) the limit at +infinity is +infinity; at -infinity it is +infinity for even n and -infinity for odd n; for c x^n the signs follow the sign of c. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.end_behavior_power_functions`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.tendsto_pow_atTop_kb`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.tendsto_pow_atBot_even`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.tendsto_pow_atBot_odd`
- **1:§4.6 End behavior for polynomial functions p.371**, p.371 [CA-159]: A polynomial of degree n with leading coefficient a_n!=0 behaves like its leading term a_n x^n as x->+-infinity, so it tends to +-infinity according to degree and sign of a_n. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.polynomial_asymptotic_leading_term`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.polynomial_tendsto_atTop`, `Polynomial.isEquivalent_atTop_lead`, `Polynomial.isEquivalent_atBot_lead`
- **1:Example 4.24 p.371**, p.371 [CA-158]: For a power x^n (n a positive integer) the limit at +infinity is +infinity; at -infinity it is +infinity for even n and -infinity for odd n; for c x^n the signs follow the sign of c. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.end_behavior_power_functions`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.tendsto_pow_atTop_kb`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.tendsto_pow_atBot_even`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.tendsto_pow_atBot_odd`
- **1:§4.6 End behavior for rational functions p.372**, p.372 [CA-160]: For f=p/q with deg p<deg q, f tends to 0 (horizontal asymptote y=0); with equal degrees f tends to the ratio of leading coefficients; with deg p>deg q f tends to plus or minus infinity. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.rational_tendsto_zero`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.rational_tendsto_leading_ratio`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.rational_tendsto_atTop_of_degree_gt`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.rational_tendsto_atBot_of_degree_gt`
- **1:Example 4.25 p.372**, p.372 [CA-160]: For f=p/q with deg p<deg q, f tends to 0 (horizontal asymptote y=0); with equal degrees f tends to the ratio of leading coefficients; with deg p>deg q f tends to plus or minus infinity. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.rational_tendsto_zero`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.rational_tendsto_leading_ratio`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.rational_tendsto_atTop_of_degree_gt`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.rational_tendsto_atBot_of_degree_gt`
- **1:§4.6 Summary of rational end behavior p.375**, p.375 [CA-160]: For f=p/q with deg p<deg q, f tends to 0 (horizontal asymptote y=0); with equal degrees f tends to the ratio of leading coefficients; with deg p>deg q f tends to plus or minus infinity. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.rational_tendsto_zero`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.rational_tendsto_leading_ratio`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.rational_tendsto_atTop_of_degree_gt`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.rational_tendsto_atBot_of_degree_gt`
- **1:§4.6 Definition (oblique asymptote) p.375**, p.375 [CA-161]: When deg p = deg q + 1, the quotient part of p/q by long division is a line y=mx+b and f approaches it at +-infinity; this line is an oblique (slant) asymptote. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.oblique_asymptote_def`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsObliqueAsymptote`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.rational_oblique_asymptote`
- **1:Table 4.5 p.377**, p.377 [CA-162]: e^x tends to infinity as x->infinity and to 0 as x->-infinity; ln x tends to infinity as x->infinity and to -infinity as x->0 from the right. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.exp_tendsto_atTop`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.exp_tendsto_zero_atBot`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.log_tendsto_atTop`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.log_tendsto_atBot_zero_right`
- **1:§4.6 End behavior of exponential and logarithm p.377**, p.377 [CA-162]: e^x tends to infinity as x->infinity and to 0 as x->-infinity; ln x tends to infinity as x->infinity and to -infinity as x->0 from the right. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.exp_tendsto_atTop`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.exp_tendsto_zero_atBot`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.log_tendsto_atTop`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.log_tendsto_atBot_zero_right`
- **1:Table 4.6 p.377**, p.377 [CA-162]: e^x tends to infinity as x->infinity and to 0 as x->-infinity; ln x tends to infinity as x->infinity and to -infinity as x->0 from the right. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.exp_tendsto_atTop`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.exp_tendsto_zero_atBot`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.log_tendsto_atTop`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.log_tendsto_atBot_zero_right`
- **1:Theorem 4.12 p.404**, p.404 [CA-163]: If f,g are differentiable near a (g' nonzero), f(x)->0 and g(x)->0 as x->a, and f'/g' has a limit (finite or infinite) L, then f/g has the same limit L. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.lhopital_zero_over_zero`, `HasDerivAt.lhopital_zero_nhdsNE`, `deriv.lhopital_zero_nhdsNE`, `HasDerivAt.lhopital_zero_nhds`
- **1:Theorem 4.13 p.406**, p.406 [CA-164]: If f(x) and g(x) both tend to infinity (in absolute value) as x->a (or +-infinity) and f'/g' has a limit L, then f/g has limit L. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.lhopital_infinity_over_infinity`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.lhopital_infty_nhdsGT`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.lhopital_infty_nhdsLT`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.lhopital_infty_nhdsNE`

#### Section 4.8

- **1:§4.8 Indeterminate forms p.408**, p.408 [CA-165]: Limits that formally look like 0/0, infinity/infinity, 0 times infinity, infinity minus infinity, 0^0, infinity^0, 1^infinity are called indeterminate forms; they must be rewritten (quotient, or take logs) to apply L'Hopital. — *not a result* — –
- **1:Example 4.41 p.408**, p.408 [CA-166]: x ln x tends to 0 as x->0 from the right (a 0 times infinity form). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.x_mul_log_tendsto_zero`, `tendsto_log_mul_rpow_nhdsGT_zero`, `Real.continuous_mul_log`
- **1:§4.8 Exponent forms via logarithm p.410**, p.410 [CA-165]: Limits that formally look like 0/0, infinity/infinity, 0 times infinity, infinity minus infinity, 0^0, infinity^0, 1^infinity are called indeterminate forms; they must be rewritten (quotient, or take logs) to apply L'Hopital. — *not a result* — –
- **1:Example 4.43 p.410**, p.410 [CA-167]: x^(1/x) tends to 1 as x->infinity (an infinity^0 form). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.rpow_one_div_tendsto_one`, `tendsto_rpow_div`
- **1:Example 4.44 p.411**, p.411 [CA-168]: x^x tends to 1 as x->0 from the right (a 0^0 form). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.limit_x_pow_x_zero_right`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.tendsto_rpow_self_zero_right`, `Real.continuous_mul_log`, `tendsto_log_mul_rpow_nhdsGT_zero`
- **1:§4.8 Growth rates definition p.412**, p.412 [CA-169]: For f,g tending to infinity, f grows faster than g if f/g tends to infinity, and they grow at the same rate if f/g tends to a nonzero constant. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.growth_rates_def`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.GrowsFasterThan`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.SameGrowthRate`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.exp_growsFasterThan_pow`
- **1:Example 4.45 p.413**, p.413 [CA-170]: e^x grows faster than any power x^p as x->infinity (limit of e^x/x^p is infinity). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.exp_div_rpow_tendsto_atTop`, `tendsto_exp_div_rpow_atTop`, `Real.tendsto_exp_div_pow_atTop`, `isLittleO_rpow_exp_atTop`
- **1:Example 4.45 p.414**, p.414 [CA-171]: ln x grows more slowly than any positive power x^p (p>0): ln x / x^p tends to 0 as x->infinity. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.log_div_rpow_tendsto_zero`, `isLittleO_log_rpow_atTop`, `Real.isLittleO_log_id_atTop`

#### Section 4.9

- **1:§4.9 Newton's method iteration formula (Equation 4.8) p.418**, p.418 [CA-172]: Newton's method: starting from x_0, the iterates x_{n+1}=x_n-f(x_n)/f'(x_n) (the x-intercept of the tangent line at x_n) often converge to a root of f; each step applies the same function, an iterative process. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.newtons_method`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.newtonSeq`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.newton_local_convergence`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.newton_local_convergence_of_contDiff`
- **1:§4.9 Iterative processes p.420**, p.420 [CA-172]: Newton's method: starting from x_0, the iterates x_{n+1}=x_n-f(x_n)/f'(x_n) (the x-intercept of the tangent line at x_n) often converge to a root of f; each step applies the same function, an iterative process. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.newtons_method`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.newtonSeq`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.newton_local_convergence`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.newton_local_convergence_of_contDiff`

#### Section 4.10

- **1:§4.10 Definition (antiderivative) p.428**, p.428 [CA-173]: F is an antiderivative of f on an interval if F'(x)=f(x) for all x in the interval. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.antiderivative_def`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsAntiderivOn`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsAntiderivOn.eq_add_const`
- **1:Theorem 4.14 p.428**, p.428 [CA-147]: If f'=g' on an interval then f-g is constant there (and so any two antiderivatives of a function on an interval differ by a constant). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.eq_add_const_of_deriv_eq`, `IsOpen.exists_eq_add_of_deriv_eq`, `IsOpen.eqOn_of_deriv_eq`
- **1:§4.10 Definition (indefinite integral) p.429**, p.429 [CA-174]: The indefinite integral of f is its most general antiderivative F(x)+C, written with the integral sign, integrand f and variable of integration x. — *not a result* — –
- **1:Theorem 4.15 p.430**, p.430 [CA-175]: For n != -1, an antiderivative of x^n is x^(n+1)/(n+1), so the indefinite integral of x^n is x^(n+1)/(n+1)+C. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.antiderivative_power_rule`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.isAntiderivOn_rpow`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.isAntiderivOn_zpow`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.isAntiderivOn_pow`
- **1:Table 4.13 Integration Formulas p.431**, p.431 [CA-176]: Antiderivatives of basic functions: e^x, 1/x (ln\|x\|), sin, cos, sec^2, sec tan, csc^2, csc cot, 1/sqrt(1-x^2) (arcsin), 1/(1+x^2) (arctan), etc., each read off from the matching differentiation formula. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.table_of_antiderivatives`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.isAntiderivOn_exp`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.isAntiderivOn_inv`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.isAntiderivOn_sin`
- **1:Theorem 4.16 p.433**, p.433 [CA-177]: Antiderivatives respect sums, differences and constant multiples: if F,G are antiderivatives of f,g then F+-G is an antiderivative of f+-g and kF of kf. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.antiderivative_linearity`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsAntiderivOn.add`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsAntiderivOn.sub`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsAntiderivOn.const_mul`
- **1:§4.10 Initial-value problems p.435**, p.435 [CA-178]: A differential equation relates an unknown function to its derivatives; an initial-value problem adds a condition y(x_0)=y_0, which picks one antiderivative from the family. — *not a result* — –

#### Section 4.2

- **1:§4.2 Key Equations p.440**, p.440 [CA-133]: If f is differentiable at a, then f(x) is approximated near a by the tangent-line (linearization) L(x)=f(a)+f'(a)(x-a), with error o(x-a). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.linearization_error_isLittleO`, `hasDerivAt_iff_isLittleO`
- **1:§4.2 Key Equations p.440**, p.440 [CA-135]: For y=f(x), the differential dx is an independent variable and dy is defined as f'(x) dx. — *not a result* — –

- **1:§4.3 Key Concepts p.440**, p.440 [CA-141]: If f has a local extremum at an interior point c and is differentiable at c, then f'(c)=0. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.fermat_local_extremum`, `IsLocalExtr.hasDerivAt_eq_zero`, `IsLocalMax.hasDerivAt_eq_zero`, `IsLocalMin.hasDerivAt_eq_zero`

- **1:§4.6 Glossary p.440**, p.440 [CA-161]: When deg p = deg q + 1, the quotient part of p/q by long division is a line y=mx+b and f approaches it at +-infinity; this line is an oblique (slant) asymptote. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.oblique_asymptote_def`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsObliqueAsymptote`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.rational_oblique_asymptote`

#### Section 4.4

- **1:§4.4 Key Concepts p.441**, p.441 [CA-145]: If f is continuous on [a,b] and differentiable on (a,b), then f'(c)=(f(b)-f(a))/(b-a) for some c in (a,b). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.mean_value_theorem`, `exists_deriv_eq_slope`, `exists_hasDerivAt_eq_slope`
- **1:§4.4 Key Concepts p.441**, p.441 [CA-148]: If f'>0 on an interval then f is increasing there; if f'<0 then f is decreasing there. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.strictMonoOn_of_deriv_pos_Ioo`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.strictAntiOn_of_deriv_neg_Ioo`, `strictMonoOn_of_deriv_pos`, `strictAntiOn_of_deriv_neg`

#### Section 4.5

- **1:§4.5 Key Concepts p.441**, p.441 [CA-149]: For continuous f with critical point c, if f' changes from + to - at c then f has a local maximum there, from - to + a local minimum, and with no sign change no local extremum. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.first_derivative_test`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.first_derivative_test_max`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.first_derivative_test_min`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.first_derivative_test_no_change_pos`
- **1:§4.5 Key Concepts p.441**, p.441 [CA-151]: If f''>0 on an interval then f is concave up there; if f''<0 then concave down. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.concave_up_of_second_deriv_pos`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.concave_down_of_second_deriv_neg`, `strictConvexOn_of_deriv2_pos`, `strictConcaveOn_of_deriv2_neg`
- **1:§4.5 Key Concepts p.441**, p.441 [CA-153]: If f'(c)=0 and f'' is continuous near c: f''(c)>0 gives a local minimum, f''(c)<0 a local maximum, f''(c)=0 is inconclusive. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.second_derivative_test_min`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.second_derivative_test_max`, `isLocalMin_of_deriv_deriv_pos`, `isLocalMax_of_deriv_deriv_neg`

#### Section 4.6

- **1:§4.6 Key Concepts p.441**, p.441 [CA-159]: A polynomial of degree n with leading coefficient a_n!=0 behaves like its leading term a_n x^n as x->+-infinity, so it tends to +-infinity according to degree and sign of a_n. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.polynomial_asymptotic_leading_term`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.polynomial_tendsto_atTop`, `Polynomial.isEquivalent_atTop_lead`, `Polynomial.isEquivalent_atBot_lead`
- **1:§4.6 Key Concepts p.441**, p.441 [CA-160]: For f=p/q with deg p<deg q, f tends to 0 (horizontal asymptote y=0); with equal degrees f tends to the ratio of leading coefficients; with deg p>deg q f tends to plus or minus infinity. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.rational_tendsto_zero`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.rational_tendsto_leading_ratio`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.rational_tendsto_atTop_of_degree_gt`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.rational_tendsto_atBot_of_degree_gt`

#### Section 4.8

- **1:§4.8 Key Concepts p.441**, p.441 [CA-163]: If f,g are differentiable near a (g' nonzero), f(x)->0 and g(x)->0 as x->a, and f'/g' has a limit (finite or infinite) L, then f/g has the same limit L. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.lhopital_zero_over_zero`, `HasDerivAt.lhopital_zero_nhdsNE`, `deriv.lhopital_zero_nhdsNE`, `HasDerivAt.lhopital_zero_nhds`
- **1:§4.8 Key Concepts p.441**, p.441 [CA-164]: If f(x) and g(x) both tend to infinity (in absolute value) as x->a (or +-infinity) and f'/g' has a limit L, then f/g has limit L. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.lhopital_infinity_over_infinity`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.lhopital_infty_nhdsGT`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.lhopital_infty_nhdsLT`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.lhopital_infty_nhdsNE`
- **1:§4.8 Key Concepts p.441**, p.441 [CA-170]: e^x grows faster than any power x^p as x->infinity (limit of e^x/x^p is infinity). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.exp_div_rpow_tendsto_atTop`, `tendsto_exp_div_rpow_atTop`, `Real.tendsto_exp_div_pow_atTop`, `isLittleO_rpow_exp_atTop`
- **1:§4.8 Key Concepts p.441**, p.441 [CA-171]: ln x grows more slowly than any positive power x^p (p>0): ln x / x^p tends to 0 as x->infinity. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.log_div_rpow_tendsto_zero`, `isLittleO_log_rpow_atTop`, `Real.isLittleO_log_id_atTop`

#### Section 4.9

- **1:§4.9 Key Concepts p.441**, p.441 [CA-172]: Newton's method: starting from x_0, the iterates x_{n+1}=x_n-f(x_n)/f'(x_n) (the x-intercept of the tangent line at x_n) often converge to a root of f; each step applies the same function, an iterative process. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.newtons_method`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.newtonSeq`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.newton_local_convergence`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.newton_local_convergence_of_contDiff`

#### Section 5.1

- **1:§5.1 Rule (properties of sigma notation) p.447**, p.447 [CA-179]: Finite sums are linear: sum of a constant n times is nc, constants factor out, sums and differences split termwise, and a sum from m to n equals the sum from 1 to n minus the sum from 1 to m-1. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C5.sigma_const`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C5.sigma_const_mul`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C5.sigma_add`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C5.sigma_sub`
- **1:§5.1 Rule (sums and powers of integers) p.448**, p.448 [CA-180]: Closed forms for 1+...+n = n(n+1)/2, the sum of squares n(n+1)(2n+1)/6, and the sum of cubes (n(n+1)/2)^2. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C5.sums_of_powers_of_integers`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.sum_Icc_id`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.sum_Icc_sq`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.sum_Icc_cube`
- **1:§5.1 Definition (partition, regular partition) p.450**, p.450 [CA-181]: A partition of [a,b] is a finite increasing list of points from a to b cutting it into subintervals; it is regular when all subintervals have equal width. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C5.regular_partition_def`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.regularPartition`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.regularPartition_mono`, `BoxIntegral.Prepartition`
- **1:§5.1 Rule (left-endpoint approximation) p.450**, p.450 [CA-182]: Left-endpoint and right-endpoint approximations L_n and R_n of the area under a graph are the sums of f at left or right endpoints times the width Delta x. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C5.endpoint_approximations_converge`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.leftSum`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.rightSum`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.tendsto_leftSum`
- **1:§5.1 Rule (right-endpoint approximation) p.451**, p.451 [CA-182]: Left-endpoint and right-endpoint approximations L_n and R_n of the area under a graph are the sums of f at left or right endpoints times the width Delta x. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C5.endpoint_approximations_converge`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.leftSum`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.rightSum`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.tendsto_leftSum`
- **1:§5.1 Definition (Riemann sum) p.456**, p.456 [CA-183]: A Riemann sum for f on a regular partition is the sum of f(c_i) Delta x with tag points c_i chosen anywhere in the i-th subinterval. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C5.riemann_sum_def`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.taggedSum`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.tendsto_taggedSum`, `BoxIntegral.integralSum`
- **1:§5.1 Definition (area under a curve) p.456**, p.456 [CA-184]: For continuous nonnegative f the area under the graph over [a,b] is defined as the limit of Riemann sums as n tends to infinity. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C5.area_under_curve_as_limit`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.tendsto_leftSum`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.tendsto_rightSum`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.tendsto_taggedSum`
- **1:§5.1 Definition (upper sum, lower sum) p.457**, p.457 [CA-185]: Choosing the tags at maximum (or minimum) points of each subinterval gives an upper (or lower) sum, an over- (or under-) estimate; for monotone f these are the endpoint sums. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C5.upper_lower_sums_monotone`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.leftSum_le_integral_of_monotoneOn`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.integral_le_rightSum_of_monotoneOn`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.rightSum_le_integral_of_antitoneOn`
- **1:Example 5.5 p.457**, p.457 [CA-185]: Choosing the tags at maximum (or minimum) points of each subinterval gives an upper (or lower) sum, an over- (or under-) estimate; for monotone f these are the endpoint sums. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C5.upper_lower_sums_monotone`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.leftSum_le_integral_of_monotoneOn`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.integral_le_rightSum_of_monotoneOn`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.rightSum_le_integral_of_antitoneOn`

## Volume 1, Chapter 6

55 items; 50 with a Lean name; 37 proved proved here; 13 in Mathlib; 5 not a result.

- **1:Theorem 6.1 p.549**, p.549 [CA-249]: If f and g are continuous with g(x) <= f(x) on [a,b], the area of the region between their graphs over [a,b] is the integral of f - g. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.area_between_curves`, `volume_regionBetween_eq_integral`, `regionBetween`
- **1:Theorem 6.2 p.551**, p.551 [CA-250]: For continuous f,g over [a,b] whose graphs may cross, the area between them is the integral of \|f - g\|, computed by splitting at crossings. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.area_between_crossing_curves`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.area_between_crossing`, `volume_regionBetween_eq_integral`, `volume_regionBetween_eq_lintegral`
- **1:Theorem 6.3 p.554**, p.554 [CA-251]: If u and v are continuous with v(y) <= u(y) on [c,d], the area between the curves x=v(y), x=u(y) is the integral of u - v over [c,d]. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.area_between_curves_in_y_book`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.area_between_curves_in_y`, `volume_regionBetween_eq_integral`

#### Section 6.2

- **1:§6.2 Definition (cylinder) and volume formula A*h p.559**, p.559 [CA-252]: A (generalized) cylinder is a plane region translated along the perpendicular axis; its volume is base area times height. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.cylinder_volume`, `MeasureTheory.Measure.prod_prod`
- **1:§6.2 slicing method p.561**, p.561 [CA-253]: Volume of a solid with cross-sectional area A(x) over [a,b] is the integral of A(x) dx (slicing method). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.slicing_method`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.volume_eq_lintegral_slices`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.volume_solid_of_slices`, `MeasureTheory.Measure.prod_apply`
- **1:Example 6.6 p.561**, p.561 [CA-254]: A pyramid with square base of side a and height h has volume a^2 h / 3 (derived by slicing). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.pyramid_volume`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.volume_pyramid`
- **1:§6.2 Definition (solid of revolution) p.562**, p.562 [CA-255]: Revolving a plane region about a line in its plane gives a solid of revolution. — *not a result* — –
- **1:§6.2 Rule: The Disk Method (6.3) p.566**, p.566 [CA-256]: Revolving the region under y=f(x), f>=0 continuous, over [a,b] about the x-axis gives volume pi times the integral of f(x)^2 dx. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.disk_method`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.volume_disk_method`
- **1:§6.2 Rule: Disk Method around the y-axis (6.4) p.568**, p.568 [CA-257]: Revolving the region between x=g(y), g>=0, and the y-axis over [c,d] about the y-axis gives volume pi times the integral of g(y)^2 dy. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.disk_method_y_axis`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.volume_disk_method_y`
- **1:§6.2 Rule: The Washer Method (6.5) p.570**, p.570 [CA-258]: Region between 0<=g<=f over [a,b] revolved about the x-axis has volume pi times the integral of f^2 - g^2. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.washer_method`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.volume_washer_method`
- **1:§6.2 Rule: Washer Method around the y-axis p.571**, p.571 [CA-259]: Region between 0<=u<=v as functions of y over [c,d] revolved about the y-axis has volume pi times the integral of v^2 - u^2 dy. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.washer_method_y_axis`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.volume_washer_method_y`

#### Section 6.3

- **1:§6.3 Rule: The Method of Cylindrical Shells (6.6) p.580**, p.580 [CA-260]: Region under y=f(x)>=0 over [a,b] (0<=a<b) revolved about the y-axis has volume 2 pi times the integral of x f(x) dx (cylindrical shells). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.cylindrical_shells`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.volume_shell_method`
- **1:§6.3 Rule: Method of Cylindrical Shells around the x-axis p.582**, p.582 [CA-261]: Region between x=0 and x=g(y) over [c,d] revolved about the x-axis has volume 2 pi times the integral of y g(y) dy. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.cylindrical_shells_x_axis`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.volume_shell_method_x`
- **1:§6.3 shell formula for axis x=-k p.583**, p.583 [CA-262]: Revolving about the vertical line x=-k replaces the shell radius x by x+k, so volume is 2 pi times the integral of (x+k) f(x) dx. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.cylindrical_shells_shifted_axis`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.volume_shell_method_shifted`
- **1:Theorem 6.4 p.591**, p.591 [CA-263]: For f with continuous derivative on [a,b], the graph has arc length the integral of sqrt(1 + f'(x)^2) dx. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.arc_length_formula`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.arcLength_isLUB`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.arcLength_eq_sSup`
- **1:Theorem 6.5 p.593**, p.593 [CA-264]: For g with continuous derivative on [c,d], the graph x=g(y) has arc length the integral of sqrt(1 + g'(y)^2) dy. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.arc_length_in_y`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.arcLength_inverse`

#### Section 6.4

- **1:§6.4 Definition (frustum of a cone) p.595**, p.595 [CA-265]: A frustum of a cone is a cone with its tip cut off by a plane parallel to the base. — *not a result* — –
- **1:§6.4 lateral area of frustum p.596**, p.596 [CA-266]: The lateral surface area of a frustum with end radii r1, r2 and slant height s is pi (r1 + r2) s (cone lateral area pi r s recalled). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.frustum_lateral_area`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.frustum_lateralArea`
- **1:Theorem 6.6 p.597**, p.597 [CA-267]: For f>=0 with continuous derivative on [a,b], revolving its graph about the x-axis gives surface area 2 pi times the integral of f sqrt(1+f'^2) dx; similarly for x=g(y) about the y-axis. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.surface_area_of_revolution`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.surfaceArea`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.surfaceArea_cone`
- **1:Theorem 6.7 p.603**, p.603 [CA-268]: A thin rod over [a,b] with linear density rho(x) has mass the integral of rho. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.mass_of_rod`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.rodMass`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.rodMass_eq_withDensity`, `MeasureTheory.withDensity_apply`
- **1:Theorem 6.8 p.605**, p.605 [CA-269]: A disk of radius r with radial density rho(x) has mass 2 pi times the integral of x rho(x) dx over [0,r]. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.mass_of_disk_radial_density`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.lintegral_disk_radial`, `MeasureTheory.integral_fun_norm_addHaar`

#### Section 6.5

- **1:§6.5 Definition (work) (6.12) p.606**, p.606 [CA-270]: The work done by a variable force F(x) moving an object from a to b along the x-axis is the integral of F (equal to F(b-a) for constant F). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.work_as_integral`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.work`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.work_const`
- **1:§6.5 Hooke's law p.607**, p.607 [CA-271]: Hooke's law: the force to stretch or compress a spring by x from equilibrium is kx with spring constant k>0. — *not a result* — –
- **1:§6.5 hydrostatic pressure (Pascal's principle) p.611**, p.611 [CA-272]: A plate of area A horizontally submerged at depth s with weight-density gamma feels force gamma s A, i.e. pressure gamma s, the same in all directions. — *not a result* — –
- **1:§6.5 Equation 6.13 (hydrostatic force) p.613**, p.613 [CA-273]: Force on a vertical plate with depth function d(x) and width w(x) over [a,b] in a liquid of weight-density gamma is gamma times the integral of d(x) w(x) dx. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.hydrostatic_force`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hydrostaticForce`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hydrostaticForce_const`
- **1:Theorem 6.9 p.620**, p.620 [CA-274]: Point masses m_i at positions x_i on a line have moment sum m_i x_i and center of mass the moment divided by total mass. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.center_of_mass_on_line`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.centerOfMass_eq_centerMass`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.centerOfMass_add_const`, `Finset.centerMass`
- **1:Theorem 6.10 p.621**, p.621 [CA-275]: Point masses in the plane have moments M_x = sum m y, M_y = sum m x and center of mass (M_y/m, M_x/m). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.center_of_mass_in_plane`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.centerOfMass2_eq_centerMass`, `Finset.centerMass`
- **1:Theorem 6.11 p.622**, p.622 [CA-277]: If a region is symmetric about a line l, its centroid lies on l. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.centroid_on_symmetry_line`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.lamina_symmetric_centroid_x`
- **1:Theorem 6.12 p.624**, p.624 [CA-278]: For a lamina of constant density rho under y=f(x) over [a,b]: mass = rho integral f, M_x = rho integral f^2/2, M_y = rho integral x f, center = moments over mass. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.lamina_under_curve`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.lamina_under_graph`
- **1:Theorem 6.13 p.626**, p.626 [CA-279]: Same formulas for a lamina between graphs g<=f: mass rho integral (f-g), M_x = rho integral (f^2-g^2)/2, M_y = rho integral x(f-g). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.lamina_between_curves`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.lamina_mass_moments`
- **1:Theorem 6.14 p.631**, p.631 [CA-280]: Revolving a planar region about a disjoint line in its plane gives volume equal to area times distance travelled by the centroid. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.pappus_theorem`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.pappus_x_axis`

#### Section 6.7

- **1:§6.7 Definition (natural logarithm) (6.24) p.635**, p.635 [CA-281]: The natural logarithm is defined for x>0 by ln x = integral from 1 to x of dt/t. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.log_eq_integral_inv`, `integral_one_div_of_pos`, `integral_inv_of_pos`
- **1:Theorem 6.15 p.635**, p.635 [CA-282]: For x>0, d/dx ln x = 1/x. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.hasDerivAt_log_pos`, `Real.hasDerivAt_log`, `Real.deriv_log`
- **1:Theorem 6.16 p.635**, p.635 [CA-283]: ln is differentiable on (0, infinity), hence continuous. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.log_differentiableOn`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.log_continuousOn`, `Real.continuousOn_log`
- **1:Theorem 6.17 p.636**, p.636 [CA-284]: The antiderivative of 1/u is ln\|u\| (integral of du/u = ln\|u\| + C). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.hasDerivAt_log_abs`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.integral_inv_log_div`, `Real.hasDerivAt_log`, `integral_inv`
- **1:Theorem 6.18 p.637**, p.637 [CA-285]: For a,b>0 and rational r: ln 1 = 0, ln(ab)=ln a+ln b, ln(a/b)=ln a-ln b, ln(a^r)=r ln a. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.log_one_eq_zero`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.log_mul_pos`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.log_div_pos`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.log_rpow_rat`
- **1:§6.7 Definition (the number e) p.638**, p.638 [CA-286]: The number e is the unique real with ln e = 1 (area under 1/t from 1 to e equals 1). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.exp_one_unique`, `Real.log_exp`
- **1:§6.7 Definition (exponential function) (6.25-6.26) p.639**, p.639 [CA-287]: exp is the inverse function of ln: y = exp x iff ln y = x, with exp x = e^x for all real x. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.eq_exp_iff_log_eq`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.exp_eq_e_rpow`, `Real.exp_log`, `Real.log_exp`
- **1:Theorem 6.19 p.639**, p.639 [CA-288]: e^(x+y) = e^x e^y, e^(x-y) = e^x / e^y, and (e^x)^r = e^(rx). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.exp_add_eq`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.exp_sub_eq`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.exp_rpow_eq`, `Real.exp_add`
- **1:§6.7 derivative and integral of e^x p.640**, p.640 [CA-289]: d/dx e^x = e^x and integral of e^x dx = e^x + C. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.hasDerivAt_exp_self`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.integral_exp_eq`, `Real.hasDerivAt_exp`, `integral_exp`
- **1:§6.7 Definition (general exponential) p.641**, p.641 [CA-290]: For b>0 and real x, b^x is defined as e^(x ln b). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.rpow_eq_exp_mul_log`, `Real.rpow_def_of_pos`
- **1:§6.7 change of base for general logarithm p.641**, p.641 [CA-292]: For b>0, b != 1, log_b x = ln x / ln b, so all logarithms are constant multiples of ln. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.logb_eq_log_div_log`, `Real.log_div_log`, `Real.logb`
- **1:Theorem 6.20 p.641**, p.641 [CA-291]: For b>0, d/dx b^x = b^x ln b, and for b != 1 the integral of b^x dx is b^x / ln b + C. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.deriv_and_integral_general_exponential`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_rpow_self`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_rpow_div_log`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.integral_rpow_base`
- **1:Theorem 6.21 p.642**, p.642 [CA-293]: For b>0, b != 1, d/dx log_b x = 1/(x ln b). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.deriv_logb_self`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_logb_self`

#### Section 6.8

- **1:§6.8 Rule: Exponential Growth Model (6.27) p.645**, p.645 [CA-294]: Exponential growth is y = y0 e^(kt) with k>0, equivalently y' = k y (differential equation). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.exponential_growth_model`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.exp_growth_iff`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.exp_growth_ivp`, `Real.hasDerivAt_exp`
- **1:§6.8 continuous compound interest p.647**, p.647 [CA-295]: With continuous compounding at rate r, balance after t years is P e^(rt), from the limit of (1 + r/n)^(nt) as n grows. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.continuous_compound_interest`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.compound_interest_tendsto`, `Real.tendsto_one_add_div_pow_exp`
- **1:§6.8 Definition (doubling time) p.648**, p.648 [CA-296]: For exponential growth with constant k, the doubling time is ln 2 / k. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.doubling_time_formula`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.doubling_time_iff`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.doubling_time`
- **1:§6.8 Rule: Exponential Decay Model p.648**, p.648 [CA-297]: Exponential decay is y = y0 e^(-kt) with k>0, equivalently y' = -k y. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.exponential_decay_model`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.exp_decay_iff`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.exp_decay_ivp`, `Real.hasDerivAt_exp`
- **1:§6.8 Newton's law of cooling solution p.649**, p.649 [CA-298]: If T' = -k(T - T_a) then T(t) = T_a + (T_0 - T_a) e^(-kt). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.newtons_law_of_cooling_solution`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.newton_cooling_iff`
- **1:§6.8 Definition (half-life) p.650**, p.650 [CA-299]: For exponential decay with constant k, the half-life is ln 2 / k. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.half_life_formula`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.half_life_iff`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.half_life`

#### Section 6.9

- **1:§6.9 Table 6.2 p.655**, p.655 [CA-300]: Derivatives of the six hyperbolic functions: sinh' = cosh, cosh' = sinh, tanh' = sech^2, coth' = -csch^2, sech' = -sech tanh, csch' = -csch coth. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.derivatives_of_hyperbolic_functions`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_tanh`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_coth`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_sech`
- **1:§6.9 hyperbolic integral formulas p.656**, p.656 [CA-301]: Antiderivatives of the hyperbolic functions (reverse of the derivative table), e.g. integral of sinh = cosh, integral of cosh = sinh, integral of sech^2 = tanh. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.integrals_of_hyperbolic_functions`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_neg_sech`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_neg_csch`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_neg_coth`
- **1:§6.9 Table 6.4 and derivation p.658**, p.658 [CA-302]: Derivatives of inverse hyperbolic functions, e.g. arsinh' = 1/sqrt(x^2+1), arcosh' = 1/sqrt(x^2-1) for x>1, artanh' = 1/(1-x^2) on (-1,1), and others. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.derivatives_of_inverse_hyperbolic_functions`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_arsinh'`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_arcosh'`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_artanh`
- **1:§6.9 inverse hyperbolic integral formulas p.659**, p.659 [CA-303]: Integral formulas giving inverse hyperbolic functions: du/sqrt(u^2+a^2), du/sqrt(u^2-a^2), du/(a^2-u^2) and so on. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.integrals_giving_inverse_hyperbolic_functions`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_arsinh_div`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_arcosh_div`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_artanh_div`
- **1:§6.9 Applications (catenary) p.660**, p.660 [CA-304]: A uniform hanging cable forms a catenary, a curve of the form a cosh(x/a). — *not a result* — –

- **2:Theorem 2.1 p.117**, p.117 [CA-249]: If f and g are continuous with g(x) <= f(x) on [a,b], the area of the region between their graphs over [a,b] is the integral of f - g. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.area_between_curves`, `volume_regionBetween_eq_integral`, `regionBetween`

#### Section 2.1

- **2:§2.1 Key Equation (2.1) p.117**, p.117 [CA-249]: If f and g are continuous with g(x) <= f(x) on [a,b], the area of the region between their graphs over [a,b] is the integral of f - g. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.area_between_curves`, `volume_regionBetween_eq_integral`, `regionBetween`
- **2:Theorem 2.2 p.119**, p.119 [CA-250]: For continuous f,g over [a,b] whose graphs may cross, the area between them is the integral of \|f - g\|, computed by splitting at crossings. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.area_between_crossing_curves`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.area_between_crossing`, `volume_regionBetween_eq_integral`, `volume_regionBetween_eq_lintegral`
- **2:§2.1 Key Equation (2.2) p.122**, p.122 [CA-251]: If u and v are continuous with v(y) <= u(y) on [c,d], the area between the curves x=v(y), x=u(y) is the integral of u - v over [c,d]. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.area_between_curves_in_y_book`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.area_between_curves_in_y`, `volume_regionBetween_eq_integral`
- **2:Theorem 2.3 p.122**, p.122 [CA-251]: If u and v are continuous with v(y) <= u(y) on [c,d], the area between the curves x=v(y), x=u(y) is the integral of u - v over [c,d]. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.area_between_curves_in_y_book`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.area_between_curves_in_y`, `volume_regionBetween_eq_integral`

#### Section 2.2

- **2:§2.2 Definition (cylinder, cross-section) p.127**, p.127 [CA-252]: A (generalized) cylinder is a plane region translated along the perpendicular axis; its volume is base area times height. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.cylinder_volume`, `MeasureTheory.Measure.prod_prod`
- **2:§2.2 Rule: slicing method (volume as integral of cross-section area) p.129**, p.129 [CA-253]: Volume of a solid with cross-sectional area A(x) over [a,b] is the integral of A(x) dx (slicing method). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.slicing_method`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.volume_eq_lintegral_slices`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.volume_solid_of_slices`, `MeasureTheory.Measure.prod_apply`
- **2:Example 2.6 p.129**, p.129 [CA-254]: A pyramid with square base of side a and height h has volume a^2 h / 3 (derived by slicing). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.pyramid_volume`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.volume_pyramid`
- **2:§2.2 Definition (solid of revolution) p.130**, p.130 [CA-255]: Revolving a plane region about a line in its plane gives a solid of revolution. — *not a result* — –
- **2:§2.2 Rule: The Disk Method p.134**, p.134 [CA-256]: Revolving the region under y=f(x), f>=0 continuous, over [a,b] about the x-axis gives volume pi times the integral of f(x)^2 dx. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.disk_method`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.volume_disk_method`
- **2:§2.2 Rule: The Disk Method for Solids of Revolution around the y-axis p.136**, p.136 [CA-257]: Revolving the region between x=g(y), g>=0, and the y-axis over [c,d] about the y-axis gives volume pi times the integral of g(y)^2 dy. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.disk_method_y_axis`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.volume_disk_method_y`
- **2:§2.2 Rule: The Washer Method p.138**, p.138 [CA-258]: Region between 0<=g<=f over [a,b] revolved about the x-axis has volume pi times the integral of f^2 - g^2. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.washer_method`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.volume_washer_method`
- **2:§2.2 Rule: The Washer Method for Solids of Revolution around the y-axis p.139**, p.139 [CA-259]: Region between 0<=u<=v as functions of y over [c,d] revolved about the y-axis has volume pi times the integral of v^2 - u^2 dy. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.washer_method_y_axis`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.volume_washer_method_y`

#### Section 2.3

- **2:§2.3 Rule: The Method of Cylindrical Shells p.148**, p.148 [CA-260]: Region under y=f(x)>=0 over [a,b] (0<=a<b) revolved about the y-axis has volume 2 pi times the integral of x f(x) dx (cylindrical shells). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.cylindrical_shells`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.volume_shell_method`
- **2:§2.3 Rule: The Method of Cylindrical Shells for Solids of Revolution around the x-axis…**, p.150 [CA-261]: Region between x=0 and x=g(y) over [c,d] revolved about the x-axis has volume 2 pi times the integral of y g(y) dy. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.cylindrical_shells_x_axis`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.volume_shell_method_x`
- **2:Theorem 2.4 p.159**, p.159 [CA-263]: For f with continuous derivative on [a,b], the graph has arc length the integral of sqrt(1 + f'(x)^2) dx. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.arc_length_formula`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.arcLength_isLUB`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.arcLength_eq_sSup`
- **2:Theorem 2.5 p.161**, p.161 [CA-264]: For g with continuous derivative on [c,d], the graph x=g(y) has arc length the integral of sqrt(1 + g'(y)^2) dy. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.arc_length_in_y`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.arcLength_inverse`

#### Section 2.4

- **2:§2.4 Definition (frustum of a cone) p.162**, p.162 [CA-266]: The lateral surface area of a frustum with end radii r1, r2 and slant height s is pi (r1 + r2) s (cone lateral area pi r s recalled). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.frustum_lateral_area`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.frustum_lateralArea`
- **2:§2.4 Formula (lateral surface area of cone and frustum) p.163**, p.163 [CA-266]: The lateral surface area of a frustum with end radii r1, r2 and slant height s is pi (r1 + r2) s (cone lateral area pi r s recalled). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.frustum_lateral_area`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.frustum_lateralArea`
- **2:Theorem 2.6 p.165**, p.165 [CA-267]: For f>=0 with continuous derivative on [a,b], revolving its graph about the x-axis gives surface area 2 pi times the integral of f sqrt(1+f'^2) dx; similarly for x=g(y) about the y-axis. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.surface_area_of_revolution`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.surfaceArea`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.surfaceArea_cone`
- **2:Theorem 2.7 p.171**, p.171 [CA-268]: A thin rod over [a,b] with linear density rho(x) has mass the integral of rho. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.mass_of_rod`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.rodMass`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.rodMass_eq_withDensity`, `MeasureTheory.withDensity_apply`
- **2:Theorem 2.8 p.172**, p.172 [CA-269]: A disk of radius r with radial density rho(x) has mass 2 pi times the integral of x rho(x) dx over [0,r]. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.mass_of_disk_radial_density`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.lintegral_disk_radial`, `MeasureTheory.integral_fun_norm_addHaar`

#### Section 2.5

- **2:§2.5 Definition (work) p.173**, p.173 [CA-270]: The work done by a variable force F(x) moving an object from a to b along the x-axis is the integral of F (equal to F(b-a) for constant F). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.work_as_integral`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.work`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.work_const`
- **2:§2.5 Hooke's law p.174**, p.174 [CA-271]: Hooke's law: the force to stretch or compress a spring by x from equilibrium is kx with spring constant k>0. — *not a result* — –
- **2:§2.5 Hydrostatic force and pressure (definition) p.178**, p.178 [CA-272]: A plate of area A horizontally submerged at depth s with weight-density gamma feels force gamma s A, i.e. pressure gamma s, the same in all directions. — *not a result* — –
- **2:§2.5 Equation (2.13) hydrostatic force p.180**, p.180 [CA-273]: Force on a vertical plate with depth function d(x) and width w(x) over [a,b] in a liquid of weight-density gamma is gamma times the integral of d(x) w(x) dx. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.hydrostatic_force`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hydrostaticForce`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hydrostaticForce_const`
