- **2:Theorem 2.9 p.187**, p.187 [CA-274]: Point masses m_i at positions x_i on a line have moment sum m_i x_i and center of mass the moment divided by total mass. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.center_of_mass_on_line`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.centerOfMass_eq_centerMass`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.centerOfMass_add_const`, `Finset.centerMass`
- **2:Theorem 2.10 p.188**, p.188 [CA-275]: Point masses in the plane have moments M_x = sum m y, M_y = sum m x and center of mass (M_y/m, M_x/m). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.center_of_mass_in_plane`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.centerOfMass2_eq_centerMass`, `Finset.centerMass`
- **2:§2.6 Definition (centroid) p.189**, p.189 [CA-276]: The centroid of a plane region is its geometric centre, the centre of mass of a lamina of constant density. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C2.centroid_definition`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.laminaMass`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.laminaCenter`
- **2:Theorem 2.11 p.189**, p.189 [CA-277]: If a region is symmetric about a line l, its centroid lies on l. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.centroid_on_symmetry_line`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.lamina_symmetric_centroid_x`
- **2:Theorem 2.12 p.191**, p.191 [CA-278]: For a lamina of constant density rho under y=f(x) over [a,b]: mass = rho integral f, M_x = rho integral f^2/2, M_y = rho integral x f, center = moments over mass. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.lamina_under_curve`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.lamina_under_graph`
- **2:Theorem 2.13 p.193**, p.193 [CA-279]: Same formulas for a lamina between graphs g<=f: mass rho integral (f-g), M_x = rho integral (f^2-g^2)/2, M_y = rho integral x(f-g). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.lamina_between_curves`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.lamina_mass_moments`
- **2:Theorem 2.14 p.198**, p.198 [CA-280]: Revolving a planar region about a disjoint line in its plane gives volume equal to area times distance travelled by the centroid. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.pappus_theorem`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.pappus_x_axis`

#### Section 2.7

- **2:§2.7 Definition (natural logarithm) p.202**, p.202 [CA-037]: For x > 0, ln x equals the integral of 1/t from 1 to x. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C2.log_eq_integral_inv`, `integral_inv_of_pos`
- **2:Theorem 2.15 p.202**, p.202 [CA-125]: For $x>0$, $\frac{d}{dx}\ln x=\frac1x$, and $\frac{d}{dx}\ln g(x)=\frac{g'(x)}{g(x)}$ where $g>0$. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C3.deriv_log_eq`, `Real.hasDerivAt_log`, `Real.deriv_log`, `HasDerivAt.log`
- **2:Theorem 2.16 p.202**, p.202 [CA-083]: ln is differentiable and therefore continuous on its domain. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C2.log_differentiable_continuous`, `Real.differentiableOn_log`, `Real.continuousOn_log`
- **2:Theorem 2.17 p.203**, p.203 [CA-206]: The integral of du/u is ln\|u\| + C, i.e. the integral over [a,b] avoiding 0 equals ln(b/a). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C5.integral_inv_eq_log_div`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C5.hasDerivAt_log_abs`, `integral_inv`, `integral_one_div`
- **2:Theorem 2.18 p.204**, p.204 [CA-040]: log_b(xy)=log_b x+log_b y, log_b(x/y)=log_b x-log_b y, log_b(x^r)=r log_b x. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.logarithm_properties`, `Real.logb_mul`, `Real.logb_div`, `Real.logb_pow`
- **2:§2.7 Definition (the number e) p.205**, p.205 [CA-035]: The number e is the unique real number with the integral of 1/t from 1 to e equal to 1. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C2.integral_inv_one_to_e`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C2.e_unique_by_integral`, `Real.log_exp`, `integral_inv_of_pos`
- **2:§2.7 Definition (exponential function) p.206**, p.206 [CA-038]: exp is defined as the inverse of ln: exp x = y exactly when ln y = x; so exp (ln x) = x and ln (exp x) = x. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C2.exp_log_eq`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C2.log_exp_eq`, `Real.exp_log`, `Real.log_exp`
- **2:Theorem 2.19 p.206**, p.206 [CA-032]: Laws of exponents: b^x b^y=b^(x+y), b^x/b^y=b^(x-y), (b^x)^y=b^(xy), (ab)^x=a^x b^x, (a/b)^x=a^x/b^x. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.laws_of_exponents`, `Real.rpow_add`, `Real.rpow_sub`, `Real.rpow_mul`
- **2:§2.7 unnumbered statement (derivative and integral of e^x) p.207**, p.207 [CA-124]: d/dx e^x = e^x, d/dx e^g(x) = e^g(x) g'(x), and the integral of e^x is e^x + C — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C3.deriv_and_integral_exp`, `Real.hasDerivAt_exp`, `Real.deriv_exp`, `HasDerivAt.exp`
- **2:§2.7 Definition (b^x) p.208**, p.208 [CA-039]: For b > 0, b^x is defined as e^(x ln b) for all real x. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C2.rpow_eq_exp_mul_log`, `Real.rpow_def_of_pos`
- **2:Theorem 2.20 p.208**, p.208 [CA-126]: For b > 0, d/dx b^g(x) = b^g(x) (ln b) g'(x), and for b != 1 the integral of b^x is b^x/ln b + C — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C3.deriv_and_integral_rpow_base`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_rpow_comp`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_rpow_div_log`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.integral_rpow_base`
- **2:Theorem 2.21 p.209**, p.209 [CA-127]: For $b>0$, $b\ne1$, $\frac{d}{dx}\log_b g(x)=\frac{g'(x)}{g(x)\ln b}$. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C3.deriv_logb_comp`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_logb_comp`, `Real.hasDerivAt_log`, `HasDerivAt.log`

#### Section 2.8

- **2:§2.8 Rule: Exponential Growth Model p.212**, p.212 [CA-305]: A quantity with y' = k y (k > 0) grows as y = y0 e^(kt), y0 the initial value. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C2.exponential_growth_model`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.exp_growth_iff`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.exp_growth_ivp`, `Real.hasDerivAt_exp`
- **2:§2.8 Equation (2.27) p.212**, p.212 [CA-305]: A quantity with y' = k y (k > 0) grows as y = y0 e^(kt), y0 the initial value. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C2.exponential_growth_model`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.exp_growth_iff`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.exp_growth_ivp`, `Real.hasDerivAt_exp`
- **2:§2.8 Derivation of continuous compounding p.214**, p.214 [CA-075]: (1 + r/n)^(nt) tends to e^(rt) as n tends to infinity, so continuously compounded interest gives P e^(rt). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C2.compound_interest_limit`, `Real.tendsto_one_add_div_pow_exp`
- **2:§2.8 Definition (doubling time) p.215**, p.215 [CA-306]: A quantity growing as y0 e^(kt) doubles in time t = ln 2 / k. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C2.doubling_time_formula`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.doubling_time`
- **2:§2.8 Rule: Exponential Decay Model p.215**, p.215 [CA-307]: A quantity with y' = -k y (k > 0) decays as y = y0 e^(-kt). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C2.exponential_decay_model`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.exp_decay_iff`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.exp_decay_ivp`, `Real.hasDerivAt_exp`
- **2:§2.8 Newton's law of cooling p.216**, p.216 [CA-308]: Newton's law of cooling: if T' = -k(T - Ta), then T(t) = Ta + (T0 - Ta) e^(-kt). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C2.newtons_cooling_law_solution`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.newton_cooling_iff`
- **2:§2.8 Definition (half-life) p.217**, p.217 [CA-309]: A quantity decaying as y0 e^(-kt) halves in time t = ln 2 / k. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C2.half_life_formula`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.half_life`

#### Section 2.9

- **2:§2.9 Definition (hyperbolic functions) p.221**, p.221 [CA-042]: sinh x=(e^x-e^(-x))/2, cosh x=(e^x+e^(-x))/2, and tanh, sech, csch, coth are the corresponding ratios. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.hyperbolic_functions_def`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.sinh_eq_exp`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.cosh_eq_exp`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.tanh_eq_exp`

- **2:§2.9 Example 2.47 (use of table) p.223**, p.223 [CA-130]: d/dx sinh = cosh, d/dx cosh = sinh, and the table of derivatives of tanh, coth, sech, csch. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C2.hyperbolic_derivatives`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_sinh'`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_cosh'`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_tanh`

- **2:§2.9 Table 2.3 (inverse hyperbolic function domains and ranges) p.224**, p.224 [CA-045]: Domains and ranges of the inverse hyperbolic functions arsinh, arcosh, artanh, and the others. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C2.inverse_hyperbolic_domains_ranges`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.arsinh_bijective`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.arcosh_bijOn'`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.artanh_bijOn'`

- **2:§2.9 Example 2.49 (use of table) p.226**, p.226 [CA-131]: Derivatives of arsinh, arcosh, artanh, arcoth, arsech, arcsch (e.g. d/dx arsinh x = 1/sqrt(1+x^2)). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C2.inverse_hyperbolic_derivatives`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_arsinh'`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_arcosh'`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_artanh`
- **2:§2.9 Integration formulas with inverse hyperbolic functions p.226**, p.226 [CA-212]: Integrals of 1/sqrt(a^2+u^2), 1/sqrt(u^2-a^2), 1/(a^2-u^2), 1/(u sqrt(a^2-u^2)), 1/(u sqrt(a^2+u^2)) in terms of inverse hyperbolic functions. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C2.inverse_hyperbolic_integration_formulas`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_arsinh_div`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_arcosh_div`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_artanh_div`
- **2:§2.9 Example 2.50 (use of formulas) p.226**, p.226 [CA-212]: Integrals of 1/sqrt(a^2+u^2), 1/sqrt(u^2-a^2), 1/(a^2-u^2), 1/(u sqrt(a^2-u^2)), 1/(u sqrt(a^2+u^2)) in terms of inverse hyperbolic functions. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C2.inverse_hyperbolic_integration_formulas`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_arsinh_div`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_arcosh_div`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_artanh_div`

#### Section 2.2

- **2:§2.2 Key Equation (disk method along x-axis) p.233**, p.233 [CA-256]: Revolving the region under y=f(x), f>=0 continuous, over [a,b] about the x-axis gives volume pi times the integral of f(x)^2 dx. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.disk_method`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.volume_disk_method`
- **2:§2.2 Key Equation (disk method along y-axis) p.233**, p.233 [CA-257]: Revolving the region between x=g(y), g>=0, and the y-axis over [c,d] about the y-axis gives volume pi times the integral of g(y)^2 dy. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.disk_method_y_axis`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.volume_disk_method_y`
- **2:§2.2 Key Equation (washer method) p.233**, p.233 [CA-258]: Region between 0<=g<=f over [a,b] revolved about the x-axis has volume pi times the integral of f^2 - g^2. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.washer_method`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.volume_washer_method`

#### Section 2.3

- **2:§2.3 Key Equation (cylindrical shells) p.233**, p.233 [CA-260]: Region under y=f(x)>=0 over [a,b] (0<=a<b) revolved about the y-axis has volume 2 pi times the integral of x f(x) dx (cylindrical shells). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.cylindrical_shells`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.volume_shell_method`

#### Section 2.4

- **2:§2.4 Key Equation (arc length of a function of x) p.233**, p.233 [CA-263]: For f with continuous derivative on [a,b], the graph has arc length the integral of sqrt(1 + f'(x)^2) dx. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.arc_length_formula`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.arcLength_isLUB`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.arcLength_eq_sSup`
- **2:§2.4 Key Equation (arc length of a function of y) p.233**, p.233 [CA-264]: For g with continuous derivative on [c,d], the graph x=g(y) has arc length the integral of sqrt(1 + g'(y)^2) dy. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.arc_length_in_y`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.arcLength_inverse`
- **2:§2.4 Key Equation (surface area of a function of x) p.233**, p.233 [CA-267]: For f>=0 with continuous derivative on [a,b], revolving its graph about the x-axis gives surface area 2 pi times the integral of f sqrt(1+f'^2) dx; similarly for x=g(y) about the y-axis. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.surface_area_of_revolution`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.surfaceArea`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.surfaceArea_cone`

#### Section 2.5

- **2:§2.5 Key Equation (mass of a one-dimensional object) p.233**, p.233 [CA-268]: A thin rod over [a,b] with linear density rho(x) has mass the integral of rho. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.mass_of_rod`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.rodMass`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.rodMass_eq_withDensity`, `MeasureTheory.withDensity_apply`
- **2:§2.5 Key Equation (mass of a circular object) p.233**, p.233 [CA-269]: A disk of radius r with radial density rho(x) has mass 2 pi times the integral of x rho(x) dx over [0,r]. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.mass_of_disk_radial_density`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.lintegral_disk_radial`, `MeasureTheory.integral_fun_norm_addHaar`
- **2:§2.5 Key Equation (work done on an object) p.233**, p.233 [CA-270]: The work done by a variable force F(x) moving an object from a to b along the x-axis is the integral of F (equal to F(b-a) for constant F). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.work_as_integral`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.work`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.work_const`
- **2:§2.5 Key Equation (hydrostatic force on a plate) p.233**, p.233 [CA-273]: Force on a vertical plate with depth function d(x) and width w(x) over [a,b] in a liquid of weight-density gamma is gamma times the integral of d(x) w(x) dx. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.hydrostatic_force`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hydrostaticForce`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hydrostaticForce_const`

- **2:§3.2 Strategy (powers of tan and sec) p.253**, p.253 [CA-219]: Strategy for integrating tan^j x sec^k x: use sec^2 = 1 + tan^2 with u = tan x when k even, u = sec x when j odd, otherwise reduce. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.tangent_secant_powers_strategy`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.sec_pow_even`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.tan_pow_odd_mul_sec_pow`

- **2:Example 3.16 p.254**, p.254 [CA-219]: Strategy for integrating tan^j x sec^k x: use sec^2 = 1 + tan^2 with u = tan x when k even, u = sec x when j odd, otherwise reduce. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.tangent_secant_powers_strategy`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.sec_pow_even`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.tan_pow_odd_mul_sec_pow`
- **2:Example 3.17 p.255**, p.255 [CA-219]: Strategy for integrating tan^j x sec^k x: use sec^2 = 1 + tan^2 with u = tan x when k even, u = sec x when j odd, otherwise reduce. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.tangent_secant_powers_strategy`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.sec_pow_even`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.tan_pow_odd_mul_sec_pow`
- **2:Example 3.18 p.255**, p.255 [CA-220]: The antiderivative of sec^3 x is (sec x tan x + ln\|sec x + tan x\|)/2 plus a constant, found by parts. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.integral_sec_cubed_example`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_sec_cube`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.integral_sec_cube`

- **2:§3.3 Strategy (sqrt(a^2 + x^2)) p.263**, p.263 [CA-224]: For integrands with sqrt(a^2 + x^2), substitute x = a tan(theta) (theta in (-pi/2, pi/2)) so the root becomes a sec(theta). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.trig_substitution_tangent`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.sqrt_sq_add_mul_tan`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_mul_tan`, `intervalIntegral.integral_comp_mul_deriv`
- **2:Example 3.24 p.264**, p.264 [CA-226]: The antiderivative of 1/sqrt(1+x^2) is ln(x + sqrt(1+x^2)) (equivalently arcsinh x), obtained via x = tan(theta) or x = sinh(theta). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.hasDerivAt_log_x_add_sqrt`, `Real.arsinh`, `Real.hasDerivAt_arsinh`

- **2:§3.3 Strategy (sqrt(x^2 - a^2)) p.266**, p.266 [CA-225]: For integrands with sqrt(x^2 - a^2), substitute x = a sec(theta) (theta in [0,pi/2) for x >= a, in (pi/2, pi] for x <= -a) so the root becomes a tan(theta) up to sign. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.trig_substitution_secant`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.sqrt_sq_sub_mul_sec`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.sqrt_sq_sub_mul_sec_of_first`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.sqrt_sq_sub_mul_sec_of_second`

#### Section 3.4

- **2:§3.4 Requirement: degree of numerator less than denominator p.271**, p.271 [CA-227]: A rational function P/Q with deg P >= deg Q is first divided: P/Q = polynomial + remainder/Q with deg remainder < deg Q, then integrated termwise. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.proper_fraction_by_long_division`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.polynomial_long_division`, `Polynomial.modByMonic_add_div`
- **2:§3.4 Nonrepeated linear factors p.271**, p.271 [CA-229]: If Q has distinct linear factors, P/Q (deg P < deg Q) is a sum of constants over each linear factor. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.partial_fractions_distinct_linear`, `Polynomial.div_prod_eq_quo_add_sum_rem_div`
- **2:Example 3.28 p.271**, p.271 [CA-227]: A rational function P/Q with deg P >= deg Q is first divided: P/Q = polynomial + remainder/Q with deg remainder < deg Q, then integrated termwise. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.proper_fraction_by_long_division`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.polynomial_long_division`, `Polynomial.modByMonic_add_div`
- **2:Example 3.29 p.271**, p.271 [CA-229]: If Q has distinct linear factors, P/Q (deg P < deg Q) is a sum of constants over each linear factor. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.partial_fractions_distinct_linear`, `Polynomial.div_prod_eq_quo_add_sum_rem_div`
- **2:§3.4 Rule (Method of Equating Coefficients) p.272**, p.272 [CA-233]: To find decomposition constants, equate coefficients of an identity between polynomials, or substitute chosen values of x, since polynomials equal at all reals are identical. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.equating_coefficients`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.strategic_substitution`, `Polynomial.funext`, `Polynomial.ext`
- **2:§3.4 Rule (Method of Strategic Substitution) p.272**, p.272 [CA-233]: To find decomposition constants, equate coefficients of an identity between polynomials, or substitute chosen values of x, since polynomials equal at all reals are identical. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.equating_coefficients`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.strategic_substitution`, `Polynomial.funext`, `Polynomial.ext`
- **2:Example 3.30 p.273**, p.273 [CA-227]: A rational function P/Q with deg P >= deg Q is first divided: P/Q = polynomial + remainder/Q with deg remainder < deg Q, then integrated termwise. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.proper_fraction_by_long_division`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.polynomial_long_division`, `Polynomial.modByMonic_add_div`
- **2:§3.4 Repeated linear factors p.274**, p.274 [CA-230]: A repeated linear factor (x-a)^n in Q contributes terms A_1/(x-a) + ... + A_n/(x-a)^n to the decomposition. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.partial_fractions_repeated_linear`, `Polynomial.eq_quo_mul_pow_add_sum_rem_mul_pow`
- **2:Example 3.32 p.274**, p.274 [CA-230]: A repeated linear factor (x-a)^n in Q contributes terms A_1/(x-a) + ... + A_n/(x-a)^n to the decomposition. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.partial_fractions_repeated_linear`, `Polynomial.eq_quo_mul_pow_add_sum_rem_mul_pow`
- **2:§3.4 Problem-Solving Strategy (Partial Fraction Decomposition), step 2 p.275**, p.275 [CA-228]: Every real polynomial denominator factors into linear factors and irreducible quadratic factors, which determines the shape of the decomposition. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.real_polynomial_linear_quadratic_factors`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.real_polynomial_factorization`, `Irreducible.degree_le_two`
- **2:§3.4 Strategy step 3c p.275**, p.275 [CA-231]: Each irreducible quadratic factor ax^2+bx+c of Q contributes a term (Ax+B)/(ax^2+bx+c). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.partial_fractions_quadratic_factors`, `Polynomial.div_prod_eq_quo_add_sum_rem_div`
- **2:§3.4 Strategy step 3d p.275**, p.275 [CA-232]: A repeated irreducible quadratic factor (ax^2+bx+c)^n contributes terms (A_k x+B_k)/(ax^2+bx+c)^k for k = 1..n. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.partial_fractions_repeated_quadratic`, `Polynomial.eq_quo_mul_pow_add_sum_rem_mul_pow`
- **2:Example 3.33 p.275**, p.275 [CA-231]: Each irreducible quadratic factor ax^2+bx+c of Q contributes a term (Ax+B)/(ax^2+bx+c). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.partial_fractions_quadratic_factors`, `Polynomial.div_prod_eq_quo_add_sum_rem_div`
- **2:Example 3.34 p.276**, p.276 [CA-232]: A repeated irreducible quadratic factor (ax^2+bx+c)^n contributes terms (A_k x+B_k)/(ax^2+bx+c)^k for k = 1..n. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.partial_fractions_repeated_quadratic`, `Polynomial.eq_quo_mul_pow_add_sum_rem_mul_pow`

#### Section 3.6

- **2:§3.6 Definition (absolute and relative error) p.288**, p.288 [CA-236]: Absolute error is \|estimate - true value\|; relative error is the absolute error divided by \|true value\|, as a percentage. — *not a result* — –
- **2:Example 3.41 p.288**, p.288 [CA-235]: The trapezoidal sum T_n (half the endpoint values plus interior values, times the step) converges to the integral as n tends to infinity. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.trapezoidal_sum_converges`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.trapezoidal_integral_eq_avg`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.tendsto_trapezoid`, `trapezoidal_integral`

- **2:Theorem 3.5 p.289**, p.289 [CA-237]: If \|f''\| <= M on [a,b], the midpoint error is at most M(b-a)^3/(24n^2) and the trapezoidal error is at most M(b-a)^3/(12n^2). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.midpoint_trapezoid_error_bounds`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.midpoint_error_le`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.trapezoid_error_le`, `trapezoidal_error_le`
- **2:Example 3.43 p.289**, p.289 [CA-236]: Absolute error is \|estimate - true value\|; relative error is the absolute error divided by \|true value\|, as a percentage. — *not a result* — –

- **2:Theorem 3.6 p.291**, p.291 [CA-238]: Simpson's rule S_n (n even) with weights 1,4,2,4,...,4,1 times Δx/3 approximates the integral and converges to it. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.simpsons_rule`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.simpson`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.simpson_weights`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.tendsto_simpson`
- **2:§3.6 Remark (Simpson as weighted average) p.292**, p.292 [CA-239]: Simpson's rule S_2n is a weighted average of the midpoint and trapezoidal sums: (2 M_n + T_n)/3. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.simpson_weighted_average`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.simpson_two_mul`
- **2:§3.6 Rule (Error Bound for Simpson's Rule) p.292**, p.292 [CA-240]: If \|f''''\| <= M on [a,b], Simpson's error is at most M(b-a)^5/(180 n^4). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.simpson_error_bound`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.simpson_error_le`

- **2:§3.7 Definition (infinite intervals, Eq. 3.16-3.18) p.296**, p.296 [CA-241]: Improper integrals over [a,inf), (-inf,b], (-inf,inf) are defined as limits of proper integrals (the last by splitting at a point); convergent iff the limits exist. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.improper_integral_infinite_interval`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.tendsto_intervalIntegral_of_integrableOn_Ioi`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.converges_Ioi_iff_integrableOn`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.converges_of_le_of_converges`

- **2:§3.7 Student Project (transform of a derivative) p.307**, p.307 [CA-247]: The Laplace transform of f' equals s times the transform of f minus f(0). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.laplace_transform_of_derivative`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.laplace_deriv`
- **2:§3.7 Student Project (transform of an integral) p.307**, p.307 [CA-248]: The Laplace transform of an antiderivative of f (vanishing at 0) is the transform of f divided by s. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.laplace_transform_of_integral`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.laplace_integral`

## Volume 2, Chapter 4

27 items; 16 with a Lean name; 16 proved proved here; 11 not a result.


#### Section 4.1

- **2:§4.1 Definition (differential equation, order, solution) p.316**, p.316 [CA-310]: A differential equation relates an unknown function to its derivatives; its order is the highest derivative order present, and a solution is a function satisfying it identically. — *not a result* — –
- **2:§4.1 Definition (general solution, particular solution, initial-value problem) p.317**, p.317 [CA-311]: The general solution is the whole family of solutions; a particular solution picks one member by an initial condition, and an equation plus initial data is an initial-value problem. — *not a result* — –

#### Section 4.2

- **2:§4.2 Definition (direction field) p.327**, p.327 [CA-312]: A direction (slope) field draws at each point a short segment whose slope is the right-hand side y'=f(x,y), and solution curves follow the segments. — *not a result* — –
- **2:§4.2 Definition (equilibrium solution) p.329**, p.329 [CA-313]: For an autonomous equation y'=f(y), constant solutions y=c exist exactly at the zeros c of f. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C4.equilibrium_solution_iff`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.equilibrium_iff`
- **2:§4.2 Definition (asymptotically stable / unstable / semi-stable solution) p.329**, p.329 [CA-314]: An equilibrium is asymptotically stable, unstable or semi-stable according to whether nearby solutions tend to it, move away, or do both on different sides. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C4.equilibrium_asymptotically_stable`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.AsympStable`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Unstable`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.SemiStable`
- **2:Example 4.8 p.330**, p.330 [CA-312]: A direction (slope) field draws at each point a short segment whose slope is the right-hand side y'=f(x,y), and solution curves follow the segments. — *not a result* — –
- **2:Theorem 4.1 p.333**, p.333 [CA-315]: Euler's method approximates the solution of y'=f(x,y), y(x0)=y0 by iterating x_{n}=x_{n-1}+h, y_{n}=y_{n-1}+h f(x_{n-1},y_{n-1}). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C4.euler_method_error_bound`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.eulerIter`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.euler_error_bound`
- **2:Example 4.9 p.334**, p.334 [CA-315]: Euler's method approximates the solution of y'=f(x,y), y(x0)=y0 by iterating x_{n}=x_{n-1}+h, y_{n}=y_{n-1}+h f(x_{n-1},y_{n-1}). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C4.euler_method_error_bound`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.eulerIter`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.euler_error_bound`

#### Section 4.3

- **2:§4.3 Definition (separable differential equation) p.342**, p.342 [CA-316]: A separable equation is one of the form y'=g(x)h(y), a product of a function of x and a function of y. — *not a result* — –
- **2:§4.3 Problem-Solving Strategy (separation of variables) p.342**, p.342 [CA-317]: To solve y'=g(x)h(y), note constant solutions where h(y)=0, otherwise write dy/h(y)=g(x)dx, integrate both sides and solve for y. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C4.separation_of_variables`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.separable_const_solution`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.separable_iff`
- **2:Example 4.10 p.342**, p.342 [CA-317]: To solve y'=g(x)h(y), note constant solutions where h(y)=0, otherwise write dy/h(y)=g(x)dx, integrate both sides and solve for y. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C4.separation_of_variables`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.separable_const_solution`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.separable_iff`
- **2:Example 4.11 p.343**, p.343 [CA-317]: To solve y'=g(x)h(y), note constant solutions where h(y)=0, otherwise write dy/h(y)=g(x)dx, integrate both sides and solve for y. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C4.separation_of_variables`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.separable_const_solution`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.separable_iff`
- **2:Example 4.13 p.347**, p.347 [CA-319]: Newton's law of cooling T'=k(T-Ta) has solution T(t)=Ta+(T0-Ta)e^{kt}. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C4.newtons_law_of_cooling`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.newton_cooling_pos_iff`
- **2:§4.3 Equation (exponential growth/decay) p.352**, p.352 [CA-318]: The equation y'=ky has solutions y=y0 e^{kt}; k>0 gives growth, k<0 decay. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C4.exponential_growth_decay_equation`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.exp_model_iff`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.exp_model_ivp`, `Real.hasDerivAt_exp`

#### Section 4.4

- **2:§4.4 Definition (carrying capacity) p.353**, p.353 [CA-320]: The carrying capacity K is the largest population an environment can sustain indefinitely. — *not a result* — –
- **2:§4.4 Definition (logistic differential equation) p.353**, p.353 [CA-321]: The logistic equation is P'=rP(1-P/K) with growth rate r and carrying capacity K; its equilibria are 0 and K. — *not a result* — –
- **2:Example 4.14 p.354**, p.354 [CA-322]: The initial-value problem P'=rP(1-P/K), P(0)=P0 has solution P(t)=P0 K e^{rt}/(K+P0(e^{rt}-1)). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C4.logistic_initial_value_problem`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.logisticSol`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.logisticSol_hasDerivAt`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.logistic_unique`
- **2:Theorem 4.2 p.359**, p.359 [CA-322]: The initial-value problem P'=rP(1-P/K), P(0)=P0 has solution P(t)=P0 K e^{rt}/(K+P0(e^{rt}-1)). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C4.logistic_initial_value_problem`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.logisticSol`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.logisticSol_hasDerivAt`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.logistic_unique`
- **2:§4.4 text on point of inflection p.359**, p.359 [CA-323]: The logistic solution has an inflection point where P=K/2 (when P0<K/2), where growth switches from accelerating to slowing. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C4.logistic_point_of_inflection`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.logistic_inflection`
- **2:§4.4 Student Project equation 4.12 p.360**, p.360 [CA-324]: With threshold population T, the model P'=rP(1-P/K)(1-T/P) has equilibria at T and K. — *not a result* — –

#### Section 4.5

- **2:§4.5 Definition (first-order linear, standard form) p.364**, p.364 [CA-325]: A first-order linear equation can be written in standard form y'+p(x)y=q(x). — *not a result* — –
- **2:Example 4.15 p.366**, p.366 [CA-325]: A first-order linear equation can be written in standard form y'+p(x)y=q(x). — *not a result* — –
- **2:§4.5 Integrating factor and Problem-Solving Strategy p.367**, p.367 [CA-326]: For y'+p y=q, with mu=e^{∫p dx}, one has (mu y)'=mu q, so y=(1/mu)(∫mu q dx + C). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C4.linear_first_order_integrating_factor`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.linear_first_order_iff`
- **2:Example 4.16 p.368**, p.368 [CA-326]: For y'+p y=q, with mu=e^{∫p dx}, one has (mu y)'=mu q, so y=(1/mu)(∫mu q dx + C). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C4.linear_first_order_integrating_factor`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.linear_first_order_iff`
- **2:Example 4.17 p.369**, p.369 [CA-326]: For y'+p y=q, with mu=e^{∫p dx}, one has (mu y)'=mu q, so y=(1/mu)(∫mu q dx + C). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C4.linear_first_order_integrating_factor`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.linear_first_order_iff`

#### Section 4.3

- **2:§4.3 Key Equation (Newton's law of cooling) p.377**, p.377 [CA-319]: Newton's law of cooling T'=k(T-Ta) has solution T(t)=Ta+(T0-Ta)e^{kt}. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C4.newtons_law_of_cooling`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.newton_cooling_pos_iff`

#### Section 4.4

- **2:§4.4 Key Equation (threshold population model) p.378**, p.378 [CA-324]: With threshold population T, the model P'=rP(1-P/K)(1-T/P) has equilibria at T and K. — *not a result* — –

- **2:Example 5.19 p.447**, p.447 [CA-351]: If b_n is decreasing with limit 0 then sum (-1)^{n+1} b_n converges. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C5.alternating_series_test`, `Antitone.tendsto_alternating_series_of_tendsto_zero`

## Volume 2, Chapter 6

39 items; 38 with a Lean name; 13 proved proved here; 25 in Mathlib; 1 not a result.


#### Section 6.1

- **2:§6.1 Definition (power series centered at a) p.478**, p.478 [CA-360]: A power series centered at a is a series of the form $\sum_{n\ge0} c_n (x-a)^n$ (centered at 0 when a=0); the convention is that the n=0 term is $c_0$ even at x=a. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.power_series_centered_at`, `FormalMultilinearSeries.ofScalars`, `FormalMultilinearSeries.ofScalarsSum`
- **2:Theorem 6.1 p.479**, p.479 [CA-361]: For a power series exactly one holds: it converges only at the center, or converges for all real x, or there is R>0 with convergence for \|x-a\|<R and divergence for \|x-a\|>R (endpoints undetermined). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.power_series_convergence_trichotomy`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.power_series_convergence_cases`, `FormalMultilinearSeries.radius`, `FormalMultilinearSeries.summable_norm_apply`
- **2:§6.1 Definition (interval and radius of convergence) p.480**, p.480 [CA-362]: Interval of convergence is the set of x where the series converges; the radius of convergence R is the number such that convergence holds for \|x-a\|<R and divergence for \|x-a\|>R, with R=0 if only at the center and R=infinity if everywhere. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.radius_of_convergence`, `FormalMultilinearSeries.radius`
- **2:Example 6.1 p.481**, p.481 [CA-363]: Radius of convergence is found with the ratio test: if $\|c_n/c_{n+1}\|$ tends to L then R=L (R=0 or infinity for the extreme limits). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.radius_eq_of_ratio_tendsto`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.radius_eq_top_of_ratio_tendsto_zero`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.radius_eq_zero_of_ratio_tendsto_atTop`, `FormalMultilinearSeries.ofScalars_radius_eq_of_tendsto`
- **2:§6.1 Geometric series as power series (6.3) p.483**, p.483 [CA-364]: $1/(1-x)=\sum_{n\ge0}x^n$ for \|x\|<1, and the series diverges for \|x\|>=1; this represents a function by a power series. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.geometric_power_series`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.geometric_power_series_diverges`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.one_div_one_sub_hasFPowerSeriesOnBall`, `Real.one_div_one_sub_hasFPowerSeriesOnBall_zero`
- **2:Theorem 6.2 p.489**, p.489 [CA-365]: If two power series converge to f and g on a common interval I, then their sum converges to f+g, $\sum c_n b x^{n+m}$ converges to $b x^m f(x)$, and the series with x replaced by $b x^m$ converges to $f(bx^m)$ whenever $bx^m$ is in I. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.power_series_sum_and_substitution`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.power_series_algebra`, `HasFPowerSeriesOnBall.add`, `HasFPowerSeriesOnBall.const_smul`
- **2:Theorem 6.3 p.495**, p.495 [CA-366]: If $\sum a_nx^n$ and $\sum b_nx^n$ converge to f and g on a common interval, then the Cauchy product with coefficients $\sum_{k\le n}a_kb_{n-k}$ converges to $fg$ there. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.power_series_cauchy_product`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.power_series_product`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.power_series_product_of_lt_radius`, `tsum_mul_tsum_eq_tsum_sum_antidiagonal_of_summable_norm`
- **2:Example 6.8 p.496**, p.496 [CA-367]: $1/(1-x)^2=\sum_{n\ge0}(n+1)x^n$ for \|x\|<1 (diverging at both endpoints), hence $\sum n x^n=x/(1-x)^2$ and for instance $\sum n/2^n=2$. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.one_div_one_sub_sq_power_series`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.sum_n_mul_geometric`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.sum_n_div_two_pow`, `Real.one_div_one_sub_sq_hasFPowerSeriesOnBall_zero`

#### Section 6.2

- **2:§6.2 Term-by-term differentiation/integration (terminology) p.497**, p.497 [CA-368]: A power series with radius R can be differentiated and integrated term by term on \|x-a\|<R, with the same radius; the sums are f' and the antiderivative of f (endpoints behave separately). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.power_series_term_by_term`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.power_series_hasDerivAt`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.power_series_integral_hasDerivAt`, `HasFPowerSeriesOnBall.hasFDerivAt`
- **2:Theorem 6.4 p.497**, p.497 [CA-368]: A power series with radius R can be differentiated and integrated term by term on \|x-a\|<R, with the same radius; the sums are f' and the antiderivative of f (endpoints behave separately). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.power_series_term_by_term`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.power_series_hasDerivAt`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.power_series_integral_hasDerivAt`, `HasFPowerSeriesOnBall.hasFDerivAt`
- **2:Example 6.9 p.498**, p.498 [CA-367]: $1/(1-x)^2=\sum_{n\ge0}(n+1)x^n$ for \|x\|<1 (diverging at both endpoints), hence $\sum n x^n=x/(1-x)^2$ and for instance $\sum n/2^n=2$. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.one_div_one_sub_sq_power_series`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.sum_n_mul_geometric`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.sum_n_div_two_pow`, `Real.one_div_one_sub_sq_hasFPowerSeriesOnBall_zero`
- **2:Example 6.10 (part a) p.499**, p.499 [CA-369]: $\ln(1+x)=\sum_{n\ge1}(-1)^{n+1}x^n/n$ for -1<x<=1 (interval (-1,1] since the series converges at 1 but diverges at -1). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.log_one_add_power_series`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.alternating_harmonic_sum_eq_log_two`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.log_one_add_series`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.log_one_add_series_diverges_at_neg_one`
- **2:Example 6.10 (part b) p.499**, p.499 [CA-370]: $\arctan x=\sum_{n\ge0}(-1)^n x^{2n+1}/(2n+1)$ for \|x\|<=1 (endpoints by the alternating series test and Abel's theorem). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.arctan_power_series_interval`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.arctan_series`, `Complex.hasSum_arctan`, `Real.tendsto_sum_pi_div_four`
- **2:§6.2 Abel's theorem (mentioned) p.500**, p.500 [CA-371]: Abel's theorem (mentioned, not stated or proved): if a power series converges at an endpoint of its interval, the function it defines is continuous up to that endpoint, so the series sums to the limit of the function there. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.abel_limit_theorem`, `Real.tendsto_tsum_powerSeries_nhdsWithin_lt`, `Complex.tendsto_tsum_powerSeries_nhdsWithin_stolzSet`
- **2:Theorem 6.5 p.500**, p.500 [CA-372]: If two power series centered at a agree on an open interval around a, then all their coefficients are equal. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.power_series_coefficients_unique`, `HasFPowerSeriesAt.eq_formalMultilinearSeries`, `HasFPowerSeriesAt.eq_formalMultilinearSeries_of_eventually`, `FormalMultilinearSeries.ofScalars_series_injective`

#### Section 6.3

- **2:§6.3 Definition (Taylor and Maclaurin series) p.506**, p.506 [CA-373]: For f with derivatives of all orders at a, the Taylor series at a is $\sum f^{(n)}(a)(x-a)^n/n!$, and the Maclaurin series is the case a=0. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.taylor_series_definition`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.taylorSeriesTerm`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.taylorWithinEval_univ_eq_sum_taylorSeriesTerm`, `FormalMultilinearSeries.ofScalars`
- **2:Theorem 6.6 p.506**, p.506 [CA-374]: If f equals a power series at a on an open interval containing a, that power series is the Taylor series of f at a. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.power_series_is_taylor_series`, `HasFPowerSeriesAt.eq_formalMultilinearSeries`, `AnalyticAt.hasFPowerSeriesAt`, `HasFPowerSeriesAt.analyticAt`
- **2:§6.3 Definition (Taylor and Maclaurin polynomials) p.507**, p.507 [CA-375]: The nth Taylor polynomial of f at a is $\sum_{k\le n}f^{(k)}(a)(x-a)^k/k!$, the nth partial sum of the Taylor series; at a=0 it is the Maclaurin polynomial. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.taylor_polynomial_formula`, `taylorWithinEval`, `taylorWithin`
- **2:Example 6.12 (part a) p.508**, p.508 [CA-379]: $e^x=\sum_{n\ge0}x^n/n!$ for all real x (the Maclaurin polynomials are the partial sums); proved via the remainder bound. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.exp_power_series`, `NormedSpace.expSeries_div_hasSum_exp`, `Real.exp_eq_exp_ℝ`
- **2:Example 6.12 (part b) p.508**, p.508 [CA-380]: $\sin x=\sum_{n\ge0}(-1)^n x^{2n+1}/(2n+1)!$ for all real x. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.sin_power_series`, `Real.hasSum_sin`, `Real.sin_eq_tsum`
- **2:Example 6.12 (part c) p.508**, p.508 [CA-381]: $\cos x=\sum_{n\ge0}(-1)^n x^{2n}/(2n)!$ for all real x. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.cos_power_series`, `Real.hasSum_cos`, `Real.cos_eq_tsum`
- **2:§6.3 Definition (remainder R_n) p.510**, p.510 [CA-376]: Taylor's theorem with remainder: if f is n+1 times differentiable on an interval containing a and x, then $f(x)-p_n(x)=f^{(n+1)}(c)(x-a)^{n+1}/(n+1)!$ for some c between a and x, hence $\|R_n(x)\|\le M\|x-a\|^{n+1}/(n+1)!$ when $\|f^{(n+1)}\|\le M$. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.taylor_theorem_lagrange_remainder`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.taylor_remainder_bound`, `taylor_mean_remainder_lagrange`, `taylor_mean_remainder_bound`
- **2:Theorem 6.7 p.511**, p.511 [CA-376]: Taylor's theorem with remainder: if f is n+1 times differentiable on an interval containing a and x, then $f(x)-p_n(x)=f^{(n+1)}(c)(x-a)^{n+1}/(n+1)!$ for some c between a and x, hence $\|R_n(x)\|\le M\|x-a\|^{n+1}/(n+1)!$ when $\|f^{(n+1)}\|\le M$. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.taylor_theorem_lagrange_remainder`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.taylor_remainder_bound`, `taylor_mean_remainder_lagrange`, `taylor_mean_remainder_bound`
- **2:Example 6.15 p.515**, p.515 [CA-377]: The Taylor series of 1/x at a=1 is $\sum(-1)^n(x-1)^n$, convergent exactly on (0,2) and converging to 1/x there (geometric series). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.taylor_series_of_reciprocal_at_one`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.taylorSeriesTerm_inv_at_one`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.inv_taylor_series_at_one`, `Real.one_div_sub_hasFPowerSeriesOnBall_zero`
- **2:Theorem 6.8 p.516**, p.516 [CA-378]: For f smooth on an interval I containing a, its Taylor series converges to f(x) at x in I exactly when the remainder $R_n(x)\to0$. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.taylor_series_convergence_criterion`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.taylor_series_converges_iff_remainder_tendsto_zero`
- **2:Example 6.16 (part a) p.516**, p.516 [CA-379]: $e^x=\sum_{n\ge0}x^n/n!$ for all real x (the Maclaurin polynomials are the partial sums); proved via the remainder bound. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.exp_power_series`, `NormedSpace.expSeries_div_hasSum_exp`, `Real.exp_eq_exp_ℝ`

- **2:§6.4 Binomial expansion and binomial coefficients (6.6)-(6.7) p.521**, p.521 [CA-383]: For a nonnegative integer r, $(1+x)^r=\sum_{n\le r}\binom rn x^n$ with $\binom rn=r!/(n!(r-n)!)$ (finite binomial expansion). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.binomial_expansion`, `add_pow`, `Commute.add_pow`, `Nat.choose`
- **2:§6.4 Binomial coefficient for real exponent (6.8) p.522**, p.522 [CA-384]: For real r the derivatives of $(1+x)^r$ at 0 give coefficients $r(r-1)\cdots(r-n+1)/n!=\binom rn$, which agree with the usual binomial coefficient when r is a nonnegative integer (and the series then terminates). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.binomial_coefficient_real_formula`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.binomial_coefficient_natCast`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.binomial_coefficient_eq_taylor_coefficient`, `Ring.choose`

## Volume 2, Chapter 7

44 items; 43 with a Lean name; 41 proved proved here; 2 in Mathlib; 1 not a result.


#### Section 7.1

- **2:§7.1 Definition (parametric equations, parametric curve) p.545**, p.545 [CA-389]: Parametric equations x=x(t), y=y(t) with x,y continuous on an interval I define a plane curve C (the set of points as t ranges over I), with t called the parameter; the orientation is the direction of motion as t increases. — *not a result* — –
- **2:§7.1 Cycloid equations p.551**, p.551 [CA-390]: The cycloid traced by a point on a wheel of radius a rolling along a line is $x=a(t-\sin t)$, $y=a(1-\cos t)$. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.cycloid_equations`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.cycloid_point`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.cycloid_velocity`
- **2:§7.1 Hypocycloid equations p.551**, p.551 [CA-391]: A circle of radius b rolling inside a circle of radius a traces the hypocycloid $x=(a-b)\cos t+b\cos\frac{a-b}{b}t$, $y=(a-b)\sin t-b\sin\frac{a-b}{b}t$; the ratio a/b governs the number of cusps (rational gives a closed curve with finitely many cusps). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.hypocycloid_equations`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hypocycloid_point`
- **2:Theorem 7.1 p.560**, p.560 [CA-392]: If x'(t) and y'(t) exist and x'(t) is not zero, then $dy/dx=(dy/dt)/(dx/dt)$; this gives slopes of tangents and critical points of a parametric curve. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.parametric_slope`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.parametric_deriv`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.parametric_deriv_exists_inverse`, `HasDerivAt.of_local_left_inverse`
- **2:Definition (second derivative as derivative of the first) p.563**, p.563 [CA-393]: The second derivative of a parametric curve is $d^2y/dx^2=\dfrac{\frac{d}{dt}(dy/dx)}{dx/dt}$. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.parametric_second_derivative`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.parametric_second_deriv`, `HasDerivAt.of_local_left_inverse`, `HasDerivAt.comp`

#### Section 7.2

- **2:§7.2 Equation (7.2) p.564**, p.564 [CA-393]: The second derivative of a parametric curve is $d^2y/dx^2=\dfrac{\frac{d}{dt}(dy/dx)}{dx/dt}$. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.parametric_second_derivative`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.parametric_second_deriv`, `HasDerivAt.of_local_left_inverse`, `HasDerivAt.comp`
- **2:Theorem 7.2 p.565**, p.565 [CA-394]: For a non-self-intersecting curve x=x(t), y=y(t) on [t1,t2] with x' continuous, the area under the curve is $\int_{t_1}^{t_2}y(t)x'(t)\,dt$. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.parametric_area_under_curve`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.parametric_area_integral`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.parametric_area`, `intervalIntegral.integral_comp_mul_deriv`
- **2:Example 7.7 p.566**, p.566 [CA-395]: The area under one arch of the cycloid $x=t-\sin t$, $y=1-\cos t$ (0<=t<=2*pi) is $3\pi$. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.cycloid_arch_area_example`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.cycloid_arch_integral`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.cycloid_arch_area`
- **2:Theorem 7.3 p.567**, p.567 [CA-396]: The arc length of the curve x=x(t), y=y(t), a<=t<=b, with x', y' continuous is $\int_a^b\sqrt{x'(t)^2+y'(t)^2}\,dt$ (reducing to $\int\sqrt{1+f'(x)^2}dx$ for y=f(x)). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.parametric_arc_length`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.parametricArcLength`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.parametricArcLength_graph`
- **2:§7.2 Equation (7.6) p.571**, p.571 [CA-397]: Revolving the curve x=x(t), y=y(t) (y>=0) about the x-axis gives surface area $2\pi\int y(t)\sqrt{x'(t)^2+y'(t)^2}\,dt$, and about the y-axis $2\pi\int x(t)\sqrt{x'(t)^2+y'(t)^2}\,dt$ (x>=0). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.parametric_surface_area_of_revolution`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.surfaceAreaAboutXAxis`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.surfaceAreaAboutYAxis`

#### Section 7.3

- **2:§7.3 Definition (polar coordinate system) p.575**, p.575 [CA-399]: A point is located in polar coordinates (r,theta) by its radial coordinate r (directed distance from the pole) and angular coordinate theta (angle from the polar axis, counterclockwise positive); the pole is the origin and the polar axis is the positive x-axis. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.polar_to_rectangular_point`, `polarCoord`, `Complex.polarCoord`, `Complex.arg`
- **2:Theorem 7.4 p.575**, p.575 [CA-400]: Polar and rectangular coordinates are related by $x=r\cos\theta$, $y=r\sin\theta$ and $r^2=x^2+y^2$, $\tan\theta=y/x$ (for x nonzero). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.rectangular_x_eq_r_cos`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.rectangular_y_eq_r_sin`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.polar_radius_sq`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.tan_polar_angle`
- **2:§7.3 Non-uniqueness of polar coordinates p.577**, p.577 [CA-401]: A point has infinitely many polar representations: $(r,\theta)$, $(r,\theta+2\pi n)$ and $(-r,\theta+\pi)$ all give the same point, whereas rectangular coordinates are unique. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.polar_coordinates_non_unique`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.polarPoint_add_int_mul_two_pi`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.polarPoint_neg_add_pi`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.polarPoint_eq_iff`
- **2:Example 7.13 p.580**, p.580 [CA-402]: In polar coordinates the equation $\theta=\alpha$ is a line through the pole with slope $\tan\alpha$, and $r=k$ (k>0) is the circle of radius k centred at the origin. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.polar_line_and_circle`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_theta_eq_line`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_r_eq_circle`
- **2:§7.3 Remark on petals of roses p.584**, p.584 [CA-403]: The rose $r=a\cos n\theta$ (or $a\sin n\theta$) has n petals when n is odd and 2n petals when n is even; non-integer n gives closed curves iff n is rational. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.rose_petals`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.rose_petal_count`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.rose_periodic_iff_rational`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.rose_period`
- **2:Example 7.14 p.584**, p.584 [CA-404]: An Archimedean spiral is the polar curve $r=a+b\theta$ (distance proportional to the angle, $r=k\theta$ from the origin), and a logarithmic spiral is a curve with r exponential in theta, the shape of a nautilus shell. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.spirals_example`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.archimedeanSpiral`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.logSpiral`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.spiral_per_turn`
- **2:§7.3 Polar curve as parametric curve p.586**, p.586 [CA-405]: A polar curve $r=f(\theta)$ is parametrised in rectangular coordinates by $x=f(\theta)\cos\theta$, $y=f(\theta)\sin\theta$ with theta as parameter. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.polar_curve_as_parametric`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.polarCurve_range`, `polarCoord`
- **2:Theorem 7.5 p.586**, p.586 [CA-406]: Symmetry tests for polar curves: about the polar axis if (r,theta) in graph implies (r,-theta) (or (-r,pi-theta)); about the pole if (r,theta) implies (-r,theta) (or (r,theta+pi)); about the vertical line theta=pi/2 if (r,theta) implies (r,pi-theta) (or (-r,-theta)). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.polar_symmetry_tests`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_symm_axis`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_symm_pole`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_symm_vertical`
- **2:Theorem 7.6 p.593**, p.593 [CA-407]: If f is continuous and nonnegative on [alpha,beta] (beta-alpha<=2*pi), the region between the origin and $r=f(\theta)$ has area $\tfrac12\int_\alpha^\beta f(\theta)^2d\theta$; the area between two polar curves is the difference of such integrals over the common angular range (check the pole as an extra intersection point). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.polar_region_area_formula`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_region_area`, `integral_comp_polarCoord_symm`, `volume_regionBetween_eq_integral`
- **2:Example 7.16 p.593**, p.593 [CA-407]: If f is continuous and nonnegative on [alpha,beta] (beta-alpha<=2*pi), the region between the origin and $r=f(\theta)$ has area $\tfrac12\int_\alpha^\beta f(\theta)^2d\theta$; the area between two polar curves is the difference of such integrals over the common angular range (check the pole as an extra intersection point). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.polar_region_area_formula`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_region_area`, `integral_comp_polarCoord_symm`, `volume_regionBetween_eq_integral`
- **2:Example 7.17 p.594**, p.594 [CA-407]: If f is continuous and nonnegative on [alpha,beta] (beta-alpha<=2*pi), the region between the origin and $r=f(\theta)$ has area $\tfrac12\int_\alpha^\beta f(\theta)^2d\theta$; the area between two polar curves is the difference of such integrals over the common angular range (check the pole as an extra intersection point). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.polar_region_area_formula`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_region_area`, `integral_comp_polarCoord_symm`, `volume_regionBetween_eq_integral`

#### Section 7.4

- **2:§7.4 Intersection at the pole when comparing polar curves p.595**, p.595 [CA-407]: If f is continuous and nonnegative on [alpha,beta] (beta-alpha<=2*pi), the region between the origin and $r=f(\theta)$ has area $\tfrac12\int_\alpha^\beta f(\theta)^2d\theta$; the area between two polar curves is the difference of such integrals over the common angular range (check the pole as an extra intersection point). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.polar_region_area_formula`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_region_area`, `integral_comp_polarCoord_symm`, `volume_regionBetween_eq_integral`
- **2:Theorem 7.7 p.596**, p.596 [CA-408]: For a polar curve $r=f(\theta)$ with f' continuous on [alpha,beta], the arc length is $\int_\alpha^\beta\sqrt{f(\theta)^2+f'(\theta)^2}\,d\theta$. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.polar_arc_length_formula`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_arc_length_integrand`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_arc_length`

#### Section 7.5

- **2:§7.5 Conic sections as plane sections of a cone p.600**, p.600 [CA-409]: Plane sections of a double cone: a plane meeting both nappes gives a hyperbola, a plane parallel to a generating line gives a parabola, a plane perpendicular to the axis gives a circle, and any other plane meeting one nappe gives an ellipse. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.conic_sections_of_cone`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ConeSections.cone_section_projection`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ConeSections.cone_section_ellipse`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ConeSections.cone_section_parabola`
- **2:§7.5 Definition (parabola) p.600**, p.600 [CA-410]: A parabola is the set of points equidistant from a fixed point (the focus) and a fixed line (the directrix); the vertex is the point halfway between them. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.parabola_vertex_halfway`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.parabola_vertex_midpoint`
- **2:Theorem 7.8 p.601**, p.601 [CA-411]: A parabola is the set of points equidistant from a focus and directrix; with vertex (h,k) and focal distance p its standard equation is (y-k)^2=4p(x-h) or (x-h)^2=4p(y-k). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C1.parabola_standard_equation`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.parabola_horizontal`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.parabola_vertical`
- **2:§7.5 Reflective properties of parabola, ellipse, hyperbola p.603**, p.603 [CA-412]: Reflective properties: rays parallel to the axis of a parabola reflect through the focus; rays from one focus of an ellipse reflect through the other; rays aimed at one focus of a hyperbola reflect toward the other. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.reflective_property_parabola`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.reflective_property_ellipse`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.reflective_property_hyperbola`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.parabola_reflective`
- **2:§7.5 Definition (ellipse) p.604**, p.604 [CA-413]: An ellipse is the set of points whose distances to two fixed points (the foci) have a constant sum; the vertices are the ends of the major axis, and the minor axis is perpendicular to it. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.ellipse_standard_equation`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ellipse_standard`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ellipse_standard_vertical`
- **2:§7.5 Ellipse vertices and axes p.605**, p.605 [CA-413]: An ellipse is the set of points whose distances to two fixed points (the foci) have a constant sum; the vertices are the ends of the major axis, and the minor axis is perpendicular to it. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.ellipse_standard_equation`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ellipse_standard`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ellipse_standard_vertical`
- **2:Theorem 7.9 p.606**, p.606 [CA-414]: An ellipse (constant sum of distances to two foci) has standard form (x-h)^2/a^2+(y-k)^2/b^2=1 with foci at distance c, c^2=a^2-b^2. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C1.ellipse_standard_equation`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ellipse_standard`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ellipse_standard_vertical`
- **2:§7.5 Reflective property of ellipse p.607**, p.607 [CA-412]: Reflective properties: rays parallel to the axis of a parabola reflect through the focus; rays from one focus of an ellipse reflect through the other; rays aimed at one focus of a hyperbola reflect toward the other. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.reflective_property_parabola`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.reflective_property_ellipse`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.reflective_property_hyperbola`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.parabola_reflective`
- **2:§7.5 Definition (hyperbola) p.608**, p.608 [CA-415]: A hyperbola is the set of points whose distances to two fixed points (the foci) differ by a constant (in absolute value). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.hyperbola_standard_equation`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hyperbola_standard`
- **2:Theorem 7.10 p.609**, p.609 [CA-416]: A hyperbola (constant difference of distances to two foci) has standard form (x-h)^2/a^2-(y-k)^2/b^2=1 with c^2=a^2+b^2, asymptotes y-k=±(b/a)(x-h). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C1.hyperbola_standard_equation`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hyperbola_standard`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hyperbola_asymptote`
- **2:§7.5 Reflective property of hyperbola p.611**, p.611 [CA-412]: Reflective properties: rays parallel to the axis of a parabola reflect through the focus; rays from one focus of an ellipse reflect through the other; rays aimed at one focus of a hyperbola reflect toward the other. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.reflective_property_parabola`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.reflective_property_ellipse`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.reflective_property_hyperbola`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.parabola_reflective`
- **2:§7.5 Definition (eccentricity) p.611**, p.611 [CA-417]: Eccentricity e is the ratio of the distance to a focus to the distance to the nearest directrix (e<1 ellipse, e=1 parabola, e>1 hyperbola); focal parameter p is the focus-directrix distance. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C1.eccentricity_focus_directrix`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.parabola_eccentricity_one`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.focus_directrix_quadratic`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.eccentricity_discriminant`
- **2:§7.5 Definition (focal parameter) p.612**, p.612 [CA-417]: Eccentricity e is the ratio of the distance to a focus to the distance to the nearest directrix (e<1 ellipse, e=1 parabola, e>1 hyperbola); focal parameter p is the focus-directrix distance. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C1.eccentricity_focus_directrix`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.parabola_eccentricity_one`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.focus_directrix_quadratic`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.eccentricity_discriminant`
- **2:§7.5 Eccentricity of ellipse and hyperbola p.612**, p.612 [CA-418]: For an ellipse or hyperbola with semi-axis a and focal distance c, the eccentricity is $e=c/a$ (below 1 for the ellipse since c<a, above 1 for the hyperbola since c>a); a parabola has e=1. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.conic_eccentricity`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ellipse_directrix`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hyperbola_directrix`
- **2:Example 7.22 p.612**, p.612 [CA-418]: For an ellipse or hyperbola with semi-axis a and focal distance c, the eccentricity is $e=c/a$ (below 1 for the ellipse since c<a, above 1 for the hyperbola since c>a); a parabola has e=1. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.conic_eccentricity`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ellipse_directrix`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hyperbola_directrix`
- **2:Table 7.1 p.613**, p.613 [CA-417]: Eccentricity e is the ratio of the distance to a focus to the distance to the nearest directrix (e<1 ellipse, e=1 parabola, e>1 hyperbola); focal parameter p is the focus-directrix distance. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C1.eccentricity_focus_directrix`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.parabola_eccentricity_one`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.focus_directrix_quadratic`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.eccentricity_discriminant`
- **2:Theorem 7.11 p.613**, p.613 [CA-419]: A conic with a focus at the pole, eccentricity e and focal parameter p has polar equation $r=\dfrac{ep}{1\pm e\cos\theta}$ (horizontal axis) or $r=\dfrac{ep}{1\pm e\sin\theta}$ (vertical axis). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.polar_equation_of_conic`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_conic_cos_plus`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_conic_cos_minus`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_conic_sin_plus`
- **2:§7.5 General equations of degree two (discriminant) p.614**, p.614 [CA-420]: The graph of $Ax^2+Bxy+Cy^2+Dx+Ey+F=0$ is a conic: an ellipse if the discriminant $B^2-4AC<0$, a parabola if it equals 0, a hyperbola if it is >0. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.general_quadratic_discriminant`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.rotation_discriminant`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.discriminant_classification`
- **2:§7.5 Rotation of axes for a conic p.615**, p.615 [CA-421]: When B is nonzero, rotating the axes by an angle theta with $\cot2\theta=(A-C)/B$ removes the cross term, with explicit new coefficients A', B', C', D', E', F'. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.conic_rotation_of_axes`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.rotation_equation`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.rotB_eq`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.rotB_eq_zero_of_cot`
- **2:Example 7.24 p.615**, p.615 [CA-421]: When B is nonzero, rotating the axes by an angle theta with $\cot2\theta=(A-C)/B$ removes the cross term, with explicit new coefficients A', B', C', D', E', F'. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.conic_rotation_of_axes`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.rotation_equation`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.rotB_eq`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.rotB_eq_zero_of_cot`

- **3:§1.3 definition of polar coordinates p.47**, p.47 [CA-399]: A point is located in polar coordinates (r,theta) by its radial coordinate r (directed distance from the pole) and angular coordinate theta (angle from the polar axis, counterclockwise positive); the pole is the origin and the polar axis is the positive x-axis. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.polar_to_rectangular_point`, `polarCoord`, `Complex.polarCoord`, `Complex.arg`
- **3:Theorem 1.4 p.47**, p.47 [CA-400]: Polar and rectangular coordinates are related by $x=r\cos\theta$, $y=r\sin\theta$ and $r^2=x^2+y^2$, $\tan\theta=y/x$ (for x nonzero). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.rectangular_x_eq_r_cos`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.rectangular_y_eq_r_sin`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.polar_radius_sq`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.tan_polar_angle`
- **3:Theorem 1.5 p.58**, p.58 [CA-406]: Symmetry tests for polar curves: about the polar axis if (r,theta) in graph implies (r,-theta) (or (-r,pi-theta)); about the pole if (r,theta) implies (-r,theta) (or (r,theta+pi)); about the vertical line theta=pi/2 if (r,theta) implies (r,pi-theta) (or (-r,-theta)). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.polar_symmetry_tests`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_symm_axis`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_symm_pole`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_symm_vertical`
- **3:Theorem 1.6 p.65**, p.65 [CA-407]: If f is continuous and nonnegative on [alpha,beta] (beta-alpha<=2*pi), the region between the origin and $r=f(\theta)$ has area $\tfrac12\int_\alpha^\beta f(\theta)^2d\theta$; the area between two polar curves is the difference of such integrals over the common angular range (check the pole as an extra intersection point). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.polar_region_area_formula`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_region_area`, `integral_comp_polarCoord_symm`, `volume_regionBetween_eq_integral`
- **3:Theorem 1.7 p.68**, p.68 [CA-408]: For a polar curve $r=f(\theta)$ with f' continuous on [alpha,beta], the arc length is $\int_\alpha^\beta\sqrt{f(\theta)^2+f'(\theta)^2}\,d\theta$. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.polar_arc_length_formula`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_arc_length_integrand`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_arc_length`

#### Section 1.5

- **3:§1.5 Definition (parabola) p.72**, p.72 [CA-411]: A parabola is the set of points equidistant from a focus and directrix; with vertex (h,k) and focal distance p its standard equation is (y-k)^2=4p(x-h) or (x-h)^2=4p(y-k). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C1.parabola_standard_equation`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.parabola_horizontal`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.parabola_vertical`
- **3:Theorem 1.8 p.73**, p.73 [CA-411]: A parabola is the set of points equidistant from a focus and directrix; with vertex (h,k) and focal distance p its standard equation is (y-k)^2=4p(x-h) or (x-h)^2=4p(y-k). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C1.parabola_standard_equation`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.parabola_horizontal`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.parabola_vertical`
- **3:§1.5 Definition (ellipse) p.76**, p.76 [CA-414]: An ellipse (constant sum of distances to two foci) has standard form (x-h)^2/a^2+(y-k)^2/b^2=1 with foci at distance c, c^2=a^2-b^2. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C1.ellipse_standard_equation`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ellipse_standard`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ellipse_standard_vertical`
- **3:Theorem 1.9 p.78**, p.78 [CA-414]: An ellipse (constant sum of distances to two foci) has standard form (x-h)^2/a^2+(y-k)^2/b^2=1 with foci at distance c, c^2=a^2-b^2. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C1.ellipse_standard_equation`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ellipse_standard`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ellipse_standard_vertical`
- **3:§1.5 Definition (hyperbola) p.80**, p.80 [CA-416]: A hyperbola (constant difference of distances to two foci) has standard form (x-h)^2/a^2-(y-k)^2/b^2=1 with c^2=a^2+b^2, asymptotes y-k=±(b/a)(x-h). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C1.hyperbola_standard_equation`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hyperbola_standard`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hyperbola_asymptote`
- **3:Theorem 1.10 p.81**, p.81 [CA-416]: A hyperbola (constant difference of distances to two foci) has standard form (x-h)^2/a^2-(y-k)^2/b^2=1 with c^2=a^2+b^2, asymptotes y-k=±(b/a)(x-h). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C1.hyperbola_standard_equation`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hyperbola_standard`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hyperbola_asymptote`
- **3:§1.5 Definition (eccentricity) p.83**, p.83 [CA-417]: Eccentricity e is the ratio of the distance to a focus to the distance to the nearest directrix (e<1 ellipse, e=1 parabola, e>1 hyperbola); focal parameter p is the focus-directrix distance. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C1.eccentricity_focus_directrix`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.parabola_eccentricity_one`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.focus_directrix_quadratic`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.eccentricity_discriminant`
- **3:§1.5 Definition (focal parameter) p.84**, p.84 [CA-417]: Eccentricity e is the ratio of the distance to a focus to the distance to the nearest directrix (e<1 ellipse, e=1 parabola, e>1 hyperbola); focal parameter p is the focus-directrix distance. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C1.eccentricity_focus_directrix`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.parabola_eccentricity_one`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.focus_directrix_quadratic`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.eccentricity_discriminant`
- **3:§1.5 Table 1.1 p.85**, p.85 [CA-417]: Eccentricity e is the ratio of the distance to a focus to the distance to the nearest directrix (e<1 ellipse, e=1 parabola, e>1 hyperbola); focal parameter p is the focus-directrix distance. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C1.eccentricity_focus_directrix`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.parabola_eccentricity_one`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.focus_directrix_quadratic`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.eccentricity_discriminant`
- **3:Theorem 1.11 p.85**, p.85 [CA-419]: A conic with a focus at the pole, eccentricity e and focal parameter p has polar equation $r=\dfrac{ep}{1\pm e\cos\theta}$ (horizontal axis) or $r=\dfrac{ep}{1\pm e\sin\theta}$ (vertical axis). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.polar_equation_of_conic`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_conic_cos_plus`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_conic_cos_minus`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_conic_sin_plus`
- **3:§1.5 discriminant classification p.86**, p.86 [CA-420]: The graph of $Ax^2+Bxy+Cy^2+Dx+Ey+F=0$ is a conic: an ellipse if the discriminant $B^2-4AC<0$, a parabola if it equals 0, a hyperbola if it is >0. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.general_quadratic_discriminant`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.rotation_discriminant`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.discriminant_classification`
- **3:§1.5 rotation of axes formulas p.87**, p.87 [CA-421]: When B is nonzero, rotating the axes by an angle theta with $\cot2\theta=(A-C)/B$ removes the cross term, with explicit new coefficients A', B', C', D', E', F'. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.conic_rotation_of_axes`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.rotation_equation`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.rotB_eq`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.rotB_eq_zero_of_cot`

- **3:§2.1 Definition (vector) p.98**, p.98 [CA-422]: A vector is a quantity with magnitude and direction, drawn as a directed segment with initial and terminal point; the zero vector has no direction; equivalent vectors (same magnitude and direction) are identified. — *not a result* — –
- **3:§2.1 Definition (equivalent vectors) p.98**, p.98 [CA-422]: A vector is a quantity with magnitude and direction, drawn as a directed segment with initial and terminal point; the zero vector has no direction; equivalent vectors (same magnitude and direction) are identified. — *not a result* — –
