- **3:§2.4 text (cross product orthogonal to both factors) p.157**, p.157 [CA-447]: The cross product u cross v is orthogonal to both u and v, so it is orthogonal to the plane spanned by them. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.cross_product_orthogonal`, `dot_self_cross`, `dot_cross_self`
- **3:§2.4 text (right-hand rule) p.158**, p.158 [CA-448]: The direction of u cross v is given by the right-hand rule (u, v, u cross v is positively oriented). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.right_hand_rule`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.det_self_cross`
- **3:Example 2.31 p.158**, p.158 [CA-446]: The cross product of two vectors in R^3 has components (a2 b3 - a3 b2, a3 b1 - a1 b3, a1 b2 - a2 b1). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.cross_product_components`, `crossProduct`, `cross_apply`
- **3:Example 2.32 p.159**, p.159 [CA-450]: The cross product is anticommutative, bilinear (distributes over sums, scalars factor out), u x 0 = 0, v x v = 0, and u dot (v x w) = (u x v) dot w. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.cross_product_properties`, `cross_anticomm`, `crossProduct`, `map_add`
- **3:§2.4 text (cross products of standard unit vectors) p.160**, p.160 [CA-449]: The cross products of standard unit vectors: i x j = k, j x k = i, k x i = j, reverse order gives negatives, and each unit vector crossed with itself is zero. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.cross_products_of_standard_unit_vectors`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.cross_unit_vectors`
- **3:Example 2.33 p.160**, p.160 [CA-449]: The cross products of standard unit vectors: i x j = k, j x k = i, k x i = j, reverse order gives negatives, and each unit vector crossed with itself is zero. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.cross_products_of_standard_unit_vectors`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.cross_unit_vectors`
- **3:Theorem 2.6 p.161**, p.161 [CA-450]: The cross product is anticommutative, bilinear (distributes over sums, scalars factor out), u x 0 = 0, v x v = 0, and u dot (v x w) = (u x v) dot w. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.cross_product_properties`, `cross_anticomm`, `crossProduct`, `map_add`
- **3:Theorem 2.7 p.162**, p.162 [CA-451]: The magnitude of u cross v equals \|u\|\|v\| sin theta, where theta is the angle between u and v. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.cross_product_norm`, `InnerProductGeometry.norm_ofLp_crossProduct`, `InnerProductGeometry.norm_toLp_symm_crossProduct`
- **3:Example 2.34 p.162**, p.162 [CA-450]: The cross product is anticommutative, bilinear (distributes over sums, scalars factor out), u x 0 = 0, v x v = 0, and u dot (v x w) = (u x v) dot w. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.cross_product_properties`, `cross_anticomm`, `crossProduct`, `map_add`
- **3:Example 2.35 p.162**, p.162 [CA-451]: The magnitude of u cross v equals \|u\|\|v\| sin theta, where theta is the angle between u and v. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.cross_product_norm`, `InnerProductGeometry.norm_ofLp_crossProduct`, `InnerProductGeometry.norm_toLp_symm_crossProduct`
- **3:§2.4 text (determinants, Eq 2.10) p.163**, p.163 [CA-452]: A 2x2 determinant is ad - bc and a 3x3 determinant expands along its first row as a signed sum of 2x2 minors. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.det_two_by_two`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.det_three_by_three`, `Matrix.det_fin_two`, `Matrix.det_fin_three`
- **3:Example 2.36 p.163**, p.163 [CA-452]: A 2x2 determinant is ad - bc and a 3x3 determinant expands along its first row as a signed sum of 2x2 minors. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.det_two_by_two`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.det_three_by_three`, `Matrix.det_fin_two`, `Matrix.det_fin_three`
- **3:§2.4 Rule: Cross Product Calculated by a Determinant p.164**, p.164 [CA-453]: The cross product is computed as the formal determinant with i, j, k in the first row and the components of the factors in the second and third rows. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.cross_product_by_determinant`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.cross_apply_eq_det`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.cross_eq_cofactor`
- **3:Example 2.37 p.164**, p.164 [CA-453]: The cross product is computed as the formal determinant with i, j, k in the first row and the components of the factors in the second and third rows. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.cross_product_by_determinant`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.cross_apply_eq_det`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.cross_eq_cofactor`
- **3:Example 2.38 p.164**, p.164 [CA-447]: The cross product u cross v is orthogonal to both u and v, so it is orthogonal to the plane spanned by them. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.cross_product_orthogonal`, `dot_self_cross`, `dot_cross_self`
- **3:Theorem 2.8 p.165**, p.165 [CA-454]: The area of the parallelogram with adjacent sides u and v is \|u x v\|; a triangle on them has half that area. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.area_of_parallelogram`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.parallelogram_area`, `InnerProductGeometry.norm_ofLp_crossProduct`
- **3:Example 2.39 p.165**, p.165 [CA-454]: The area of the parallelogram with adjacent sides u and v is \|u x v\|; a triangle on them has half that area. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.area_of_parallelogram`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.parallelogram_area`, `InnerProductGeometry.norm_ofLp_crossProduct`
- **3:§2.4 Definition (triple scalar product) p.166**, p.166 [CA-455]: The triple scalar product u dot (v x w) equals the determinant of the 3x3 matrix with rows u, v, w. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.triple_scalar_product_eq_det`, `triple_product_eq_det`
- **3:Theorem 2.9 p.166**, p.166 [CA-455]: The triple scalar product u dot (v x w) equals the determinant of the 3x3 matrix with rows u, v, w. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.triple_scalar_product_eq_det`, `triple_product_eq_det`
- **3:Example 2.40 p.167**, p.167 [CA-455]: The triple scalar product u dot (v x w) equals the determinant of the 3x3 matrix with rows u, v, w. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.triple_scalar_product_eq_det`, `triple_product_eq_det`
- **3:§2.4 text (identities for permuting a triple scalar product) p.168**, p.168 [CA-456]: The triple scalar product is unchanged by cyclic permutation of its three vectors and changes sign when two are swapped. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.triple_scalar_product_permutations`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.triple_cyclic`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.triple_swap`, `triple_product_permutation`
- **3:Theorem 2.10 p.168**, p.168 [CA-457]: The volume of the parallelepiped with edge vectors u, v, w is the absolute value of the triple scalar product. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.volume_of_parallelepiped`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.volume_parallelepiped_triple`, `MeasureTheory.Measure.addHaar_parallelepiped`, `triple_product_eq_det`
- **3:Example 2.41 p.169**, p.169 [CA-457]: The volume of the parallelepiped with edge vectors u, v, w is the absolute value of the triple scalar product. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.volume_of_parallelepiped`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.volume_parallelepiped_triple`, `MeasureTheory.Measure.addHaar_parallelepiped`, `triple_product_eq_det`
- **3:Example 2.42 p.169**, p.169 [CA-458]: Three vectors are coplanar if and only if their triple scalar product is zero. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.coplanar_vectors_triple_product_zero`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.coplanar_iff_triple_eq_zero`, `triple_product_eq_det`
- **3:§2.4 Definition (torque) p.170**, p.170 [CA-459]: Torque of a force F applied at position vector r from the axis is r cross F, of magnitude \|r\|\|F\| sin theta. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.torque_cross_product_magnitude`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.torque_magnitude`
- **3:Example 2.43 p.170**, p.170 [CA-447]: The cross product u cross v is orthogonal to both u and v, so it is orthogonal to the plane spanned by them. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.cross_product_orthogonal`, `dot_self_cross`, `dot_cross_self`
- **3:Example 2.44 p.171**, p.171 [CA-459]: Torque of a force F applied at position vector r from the axis is r cross F, of magnitude \|r\|\|F\| sin theta. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.torque_cross_product_magnitude`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.torque_magnitude`

#### Section 2.5

- **3:§2.5 text (parallel iff scalar multiple) p.177**, p.177 [CA-423]: Two nonzero vectors are parallel (same or opposite direction) exactly when one is a scalar multiple of the other; the zero vector counts as parallel to all. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.parallel_vectors_iff_angle`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.parallel_iff_angle`, `collinear_iff_of_mem`
- **3:§2.5 text (vector equation of a line, Eq 2.12) p.178**, p.178 [CA-460]: The line through P0 with direction vector v has vector equation r = r0 + t v, parametric equations x=x0+ta, y=y0+tb, z=z0+tc and (all of a,b,c nonzero) symmetric equations. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.line_parametric_and_symmetric`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.line_forms`, `mem_affineSpan_pair_iff_exists_lineMap_eq`, `AffineMap.lineMap_apply`
- **3:Theorem 2.11 p.179**, p.179 [CA-460]: The line through P0 with direction vector v has vector equation r = r0 + t v, parametric equations x=x0+ta, y=y0+tb, z=z0+tc and (all of a,b,c nonzero) symmetric equations. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.line_parametric_and_symmetric`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.line_forms`, `mem_affineSpan_pair_iff_exists_lineMap_eq`, `AffineMap.lineMap_apply`
- **3:Example 2.45 p.179**, p.179 [CA-460]: The line through P0 with direction vector v has vector equation r = r0 + t v, parametric equations x=x0+ta, y=y0+tb, z=z0+tc and (all of a,b,c nonzero) symmetric equations. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.line_parametric_and_symmetric`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.line_forms`, `mem_affineSpan_pair_iff_exists_lineMap_eq`, `AffineMap.lineMap_apply`
- **3:§2.5 text (line segment vector equation, Eq 2.15) p.180**, p.180 [CA-461]: The segment between points P and Q is r = (1-t) r_P + t r_Q for t in [0,1], with the corresponding componentwise parametric equations. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.segment_vector_equation`, `segment_eq_image_lineMap`, `AffineMap.lineMap_apply`
- **3:§2.5 text (parametric equations of a segment, Eq 2.16) p.180**, p.180 [CA-461]: The segment between points P and Q is r = (1-t) r_P + t r_Q for t in [0,1], with the corresponding componentwise parametric equations. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.segment_vector_equation`, `segment_eq_image_lineMap`, `AffineMap.lineMap_apply`
- **3:§2.5 text (distance from a point to a line, definition) p.180**, p.180 [CA-462]: The distance from a point Q to the line through P with direction v is \|PQ x v\|/\|v\|; distance is the length of the perpendicular segment. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.distance_from_point_to_line`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.distance_point_line`
- **3:Example 2.46 p.180**, p.180 [CA-461]: The segment between points P and Q is r = (1-t) r_P + t r_Q for t in [0,1], with the corresponding componentwise parametric equations. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.segment_vector_equation`, `segment_eq_image_lineMap`, `AffineMap.lineMap_apply`
- **3:Theorem 2.12 p.181**, p.181 [CA-462]: The distance from a point Q to the line through P with direction v is \|PQ x v\|/\|v\|; distance is the length of the perpendicular segment. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.distance_from_point_to_line`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.distance_point_line`
- **3:Example 2.47 p.181**, p.181 [CA-462]: The distance from a point Q to the line through P with direction v is \|PQ x v\|/\|v\|; distance is the length of the perpendicular segment. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.distance_from_point_to_line`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.distance_point_line`
- **3:§2.5 text (skew lines and relationships between lines) p.182**, p.182 [CA-463]: Two lines in space are equal, parallel and distinct, intersecting in a point, or skew (not parallel and not meeting), decided by direction vectors and a common point. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.lines_in_space_relationships`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.lines_classification`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.lines_classification_exclusive`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.lines_skew_iff`
- **3:Example 2.48 p.183**, p.183 [CA-463]: Two lines in space are equal, parallel and distinct, intersecting in a point, or skew (not parallel and not meeting), decided by direction vectors and a common point. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.lines_in_space_relationships`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.lines_classification`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.lines_classification_exclusive`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.lines_skew_iff`
- **3:§2.5 Definition (vector and scalar equation of a plane, Eq 2.17, 2.18) p.186**, p.186 [CA-464]: The plane through P0 with normal n is the set of points P with n dot (P - P0) = 0, i.e. a(x-x0)+b(y-y0)+c(z-z0)=0, or ax+by+cz+d=0 in general form. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.plane_equation_forms`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.plane_forms`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.planeGen_eq_plane`, `AffineSubspace.mk'`
- **3:Theorem 2.13 p.188**, p.188 [CA-465]: The distance from a point to the plane with normal n through Q is \|n dot QP\|/\|n\|, which is \|a x0+b y0+c z0+d\|/sqrt(a^2+b^2+c^2) for the plane ax+by+cz+d=0; parallel planes have constant distance given by this formula. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.distance_from_point_to_plane`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.distance_point_plane`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.distance_point_plane_pointNormal`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.distance_parallel_planes`
- **3:Example 2.51 p.188**, p.188 [CA-465]: The distance from a point to the plane with normal n through Q is \|n dot QP\|/\|n\|, which is \|a x0+b y0+c z0+d\|/sqrt(a^2+b^2+c^2) for the plane ax+by+cz+d=0; parallel planes have constant distance given by this formula. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.distance_from_point_to_plane`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.distance_point_plane`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.distance_point_plane_pointNormal`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.distance_parallel_planes`
- **3:§2.5 text (parallel and intersecting planes) p.189**, p.189 [CA-466]: Two distinct planes in space are either parallel (parallel normals) or meet in a line; the line of intersection is found by solving the two plane equations. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.planes_parallel_or_meet_in_line`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.planes_parallel_or_line`
- **3:Example 2.52 p.189**, p.189 [CA-466]: Two distinct planes in space are either parallel (parallel normals) or meet in a line; the line of intersection is found by solving the two plane equations. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.planes_parallel_or_meet_in_line`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.planes_parallel_or_line`
- **3:§2.5 text (angle between two planes) p.190**, p.190 [CA-467]: The angle between two planes equals the angle between their normals: cos theta = \|n1 dot n2\|/(\|n1\|\|n2\|); planes are parallel iff normals are parallel and orthogonal iff normals are orthogonal. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.angle_between_planes`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.planeAngle_spec`
- **3:Example 2.53 p.190**, p.190 [CA-467]: The angle between two planes equals the angle between their normals: cos theta = \|n1 dot n2\|/(\|n1\|\|n2\|); planes are parallel iff normals are parallel and orthogonal iff normals are orthogonal. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.angle_between_planes`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.planeAngle_spec`
- **3:Theorem 2.14 p.191**, p.191 [CA-465]: The distance from a point to the plane with normal n through Q is \|n dot QP\|/\|n\|, which is \|a x0+b y0+c z0+d\|/sqrt(a^2+b^2+c^2) for the plane ax+by+cz+d=0; parallel planes have constant distance given by this formula. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.distance_from_point_to_plane`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.distance_point_plane`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.distance_point_plane_pointNormal`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.distance_parallel_planes`
- **3:§2.5 Student Project (distance between two skew lines) p.192**, p.192 [CA-468]: The distance between two skew lines with direction vectors a and b through points P and Q is \|(a x b) dot PQ\|/\|a x b\|. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.distance_between_skew_lines`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.distance_skew_lines`
- **3:Example 2.54 p.192**, p.192 [CA-465]: The distance from a point to the plane with normal n through Q is \|n dot QP\|/\|n\|, which is \|a x0+b y0+c z0+d\|/sqrt(a^2+b^2+c^2) for the plane ax+by+cz+d=0; parallel planes have constant distance given by this formula. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.distance_from_point_to_plane`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.distance_point_plane`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.distance_point_plane_pointNormal`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.distance_parallel_planes`

#### Section 2.6

- **3:§2.6 Definition (cylinder, rulings) p.201**, p.201 [CA-469]: A cylinder (cylindrical surface) is the union of parallel lines (rulings) through a given curve. — *not a result* — –
- **3:Example 2.55 p.201**, p.201 [CA-469]: A cylinder (cylindrical surface) is the union of parallel lines (rulings) through a given curve. — *not a result* — –
- **3:§2.6 Definition (traces) p.203**, p.203 [CA-470]: A trace of a surface is its intersection with a plane parallel to a coordinate plane. — *not a result* — –
- **3:§2.6 Definition (quadric surfaces) p.204**, p.204 [CA-471]: A quadric surface is the graph of a second-degree equation in x, y, z (general quadratic with cross terms and linear terms). — *not a result* — –
- **3:§2.6 text (ellipsoid and its traces) p.204**, p.204 [CA-472]: The ellipsoid x^2/a^2+y^2/b^2+z^2/c^2=1 has ellipses as coordinate traces, and is a sphere when a=b=c. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.ellipsoid_traces`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ellipsoid_trace_z`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ellipsoid_trace_coordinate`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ellipsoid_eq_sphere`
- **3:Example 2.56 p.204**, p.204 [CA-472]: The ellipsoid x^2/a^2+y^2/b^2+z^2/c^2=1 has ellipses as coordinate traces, and is a sphere when a=b=c. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.ellipsoid_traces`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ellipsoid_trace_z`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ellipsoid_trace_coordinate`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ellipsoid_eq_sphere`
- **3:§2.6 text (elliptic paraboloid) p.205**, p.205 [CA-473]: The elliptic paraboloid z = x^2/a^2+y^2/b^2 has ellipses as horizontal traces and parabolas as traces in vertical coordinate planes. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.elliptic_paraboloid_traces`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.paraboloid_trace_horizontal`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.paraboloid_trace_vertical`
- **3:Example 2.57 p.206**, p.206 [CA-473]: The elliptic paraboloid z = x^2/a^2+y^2/b^2 has ellipses as horizontal traces and parabolas as traces in vertical coordinate planes. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.elliptic_paraboloid_traces`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.paraboloid_trace_horizontal`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.paraboloid_trace_vertical`
- **3:§2.6 text (hyperboloid of one sheet) p.207**, p.207 [CA-474]: Catalogue of the standard quadric surfaces (hyperboloids of one and two sheets, elliptic cone, hyperbolic paraboloid, with ellipsoid and paraboloid) with their equations and traces. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.quadric_surfaces_traces`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hyperboloidOne_trace`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hyperboloidTwo_trace`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.cone_trace`
- **3:§2.6 table (standard quadric surfaces, Figures 2.87 and 2.88) p.209**, p.209 [CA-474]: Catalogue of the standard quadric surfaces (hyperboloids of one and two sheets, elliptic cone, hyperbolic paraboloid, with ellipsoid and paraboloid) with their equations and traces. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.quadric_surfaces_traces`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hyperboloidOne_trace`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hyperboloidTwo_trace`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.cone_trace`

#### Section 2.7

- **3:§2.7 Definition (cylindrical coordinates) p.218**, p.218 [CA-475]: Cylindrical coordinates (r, theta, z) describe a point by the polar coordinates of its projection to the xy-plane and its height z. — *not a result* — –
- **3:Theorem 2.15 p.218**, p.218 [CA-476]: x = r cos theta, y = r sin theta, z = z, and conversely r^2 = x^2+y^2, tan theta = y/x, z = z. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.cylindrical_cartesian_conversion`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.cylToCart_spec`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.cartesian_to_cylindrical`, `polarCoord`
- **3:Example 2.60 p.219**, p.219 [CA-476]: x = r cos theta, y = r sin theta, z = z, and conversely r^2 = x^2+y^2, tan theta = y/x, z = z. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.cylindrical_cartesian_conversion`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.cylToCart_spec`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.cartesian_to_cylindrical`, `polarCoord`
- **3:Example 2.61 p.220**, p.220 [CA-476]: x = r cos theta, y = r sin theta, z = z, and conversely r^2 = x^2+y^2, tan theta = y/x, z = z. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.cylindrical_cartesian_conversion`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.cylToCart_spec`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.cartesian_to_cylindrical`, `polarCoord`
- **3:§2.7 Definition (spherical coordinates) p.223**, p.223 [CA-477]: Spherical coordinates (rho, theta, phi): distance from the origin, the same angle theta as cylindrical, and the angle phi from the positive z-axis. — *not a result* — –
- **3:Theorem 2.16 p.223**, p.223 [CA-478]: x = rho sin phi cos theta, y = rho sin phi sin theta, z = rho cos phi; rho^2 = x^2+y^2+z^2; and the relations r = rho sin phi, z = rho cos phi with cylindrical coordinates. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.spherical_cartesian_conversion`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.sphToCart_spec`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.cartesian_to_spherical`
- **3:Example 2.63 p.225**, p.225 [CA-478]: x = rho sin phi cos theta, y = rho sin phi sin theta, z = rho cos phi; rho^2 = x^2+y^2+z^2; and the relations r = rho sin phi, z = rho cos phi with cylindrical coordinates. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.spherical_cartesian_conversion`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.sphToCart_spec`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.cartesian_to_spherical`
- **3:Example 2.64 p.226**, p.226 [CA-478]: x = rho sin phi cos theta, y = rho sin phi sin theta, z = rho cos phi; rho^2 = x^2+y^2+z^2; and the relations r = rho sin phi, z = rho cos phi with cylindrical coordinates. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.spherical_cartesian_conversion`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.sphToCart_spec`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.cartesian_to_spherical`

## Volume 3, Chapter 3

28 items; 25 with a Lean name; 18 proved proved here; 7 in Mathlib; 3 not a result.


#### Section 3.1

- **3:§3.1 Definition (vector-valued function) p.244**, p.244 [CA-479]: A vector-valued function r(t) of a real parameter has component functions (f,g) in the plane or (f,g,h) in space; its graph is a plane or space curve. — *not a result* — –
- **3:§3.1 helix example p.247**, p.247 [CA-480]: The helix is the space curve (cos t, sin t, t) (generalised with radius and pitch constants). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C3.helix_space_curve`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.helix3`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.curvature_helix3`
- **3:Theorem 3.1 p.249**, p.249 [CA-481]: The limit of a vector-valued function exists exactly when each component's limit exists, and equals the vector of the component limits. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C3.limit_vector_function_iff_components`, `tendsto_pi_nhds`
- **3:§3.1 Definition (continuity of vector-valued function) p.249**, p.249 [CA-482]: A vector-valued function is continuous at a point when each component is, i.e. the limit exists and equals the value. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C3.continuousAt_vector_function_iff_components`, `continuousAt_pi`

#### Section 3.2

- **3:§3.2 Definition (derivative of a vector-valued function) p.252**, p.252 [CA-483]: The derivative of r at t is the limit of (r(t+h)-r(t))/h as h goes to 0, when it exists. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C3.derivative_vector_function_iff_limit`, `hasDerivAt_iff_tendsto_slope`
- **3:Theorem 3.2 p.253**, p.253 [CA-484]: A vector-valued function is differentiable exactly when its components are, and r' is the vector of component derivatives. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C3.derivative_vector_function_iff_components`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C3.deriv_vector_function_components`, `hasDerivAt_pi`, `deriv_pi`
- **3:Theorem 3.3 (parts i-iv, vi) p.254**, p.254 [CA-485]: Derivative rules: constant multiple, sum/difference, scalar-function times vector, dot product, and chain rule r(f(t)). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C3.deriv_rule_const_mul`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C3.deriv_rule_add`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C3.deriv_rule_sub`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C3.deriv_rule_scalar_mul`
- **3:Theorem 3.3 (part v) p.254**, p.254 [CA-486]: Product rule for the cross product: (r x u)' = r' x u + r x u'. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C3.deriv_rule_cross_product`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_cross3`
- **3:Theorem 3.3 (part vii) p.254**, p.254 [CA-487]: If \|\|r(t)\|\| is constant then r(t) and r'(t) are orthogonal. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C3.deriv_orthogonal_of_constant_norm`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.dot_deriv_eq_zero_of_norm_const`, `HasDerivAt.norm_sq`
- **3:§3.2 Definition (tangent and unit tangent vector) p.256**, p.256 [CA-488]: For a smooth curve r(t), r'(t) is a tangent vector and T(t)=r'(t)/\|\|r'(t)\|\| is the unit tangent vector. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C3.unit_tangent_vector`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.unitTangent3`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.vnorm_unitTangent3`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.deriv_eq_speed_smul_unitTangent3`
- **3:§3.2 Definition (integral of vector-valued function) p.257**, p.257 [CA-489]: The indefinite and definite integrals of a vector-valued function are computed componentwise. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C3.integral_vector_function_components`, `ContinuousLinearMap.intervalIntegral_comp_comm`
- **3:§3.2 FTC remark p.257**, p.257 [CA-490]: Fundamental theorem of calculus for vector-valued functions: the integral of r' over [a,b] is r(b)-r(a). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C3.integral_vector_function_ftc`, `intervalIntegral.integral_eq_sub_of_hasDerivAt`
- **3:Theorem 3.4 p.266**, p.266 [CA-491]: The length of a smooth space curve r(t), a<=t<=b, is the integral of \|\|r'(t)\|\| dt (for plane curves sqrt(f'^2+g'^2)). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C3.arc_length_space_curve`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.arcLength3`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.arcLength3_plane`

#### Section 3.3

- **3:§3.3 arc-length parameterization p.268**, p.268 [CA-492]: The arc-length function s(t) is the integral of \|\|r'\|\| from a to t, with s'(t)=\|\|r'(t)\|\|; if \|\|r'\|\|=1 then t is arc length, and curves can be reparametrised by arc length. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C3.arc_length_function`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_arcLength3`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.arcLength3_unit_speed`
- **3:Theorem 3.5 p.268**, p.268 [CA-492]: The arc-length function s(t) is the integral of \|\|r'\|\| from a to t, with s'(t)=\|\|r'(t)\|\|; if \|\|r'\|\|=1 then t is arc length, and curves can be reparametrised by arc length. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C3.arc_length_function`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_arcLength3`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.arcLength3_unit_speed`
- **3:§3.3 Definition (curvature) p.270**, p.270 [CA-493]: The curvature of a smooth curve is kappa = \|\|dT/ds\|\|, the rate of turning of the unit tangent with respect to arc length (equivalently that of the inscribed circle). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C3.curvature_definition`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.curvature3`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.curvature3_unit_speed`
- **3:Theorem 3.6 p.270**, p.270 [CA-494]: Curvature equals \|\|T'(t)\|\|/\|\|r'(t)\|\|, equals \|\|r' x r''\|\|/\|\|r'\|\|^3 in space, and equals \|f''(x)\|/(1+f'(x)^2)^(3/2) for a graph y=f(x). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C3.curvature_formulas`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.curvature3_eq_cross`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.curvature_graph`
- **3:§3.3 Definition (principal unit normal, binormal) p.274**, p.274 [CA-495]: The principal unit normal is N=T'/\|\|T'\|\| and the binormal is B=T x N; T, N, B are mutually orthogonal unit vectors. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C3.principal_normal_and_binormal`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.normalN3`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.binormal3`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.frame3_orthonormal`
- **3:§3.3 osculating circle p.278**, p.278 [CA-496]: The osculating circle at a point lies on the concave side, is tangent to the curve, and has radius 1/kappa. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C3.osculating_circle`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.osculating_center_graph`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.osculating_circle_graph`

#### Section 3.4

- **3:§3.4 Definition (velocity, acceleration, speed) p.283**, p.283 [CA-497]: For position r(t), velocity is r', acceleration is r'', and speed is \|\|r'\|\|. — *not a result* — –
- **3:§3.4 Table 3.4 p.284**, p.284 [CA-497]: For position r(t), velocity is r', acceleration is r'', and speed is \|\|r'\|\|. — *not a result* — –
- **3:Theorem 3.7 p.286**, p.286 [CA-498]: Acceleration lies in the plane of T and N: a = (d speed/dt) T + kappa (speed)^2 N. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C3.acceleration_in_tangent_normal_plane`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.acceleration_decomposition`
- **3:Theorem 3.8 p.287**, p.287 [CA-499]: Tangential component a_T = a.T = r'.r''/\|\|r'\|\| and normal component a_N = a.N = \|\|r' x r''\|\|/\|\|r'\|\|, with a_T^2+a_N^2=\|\|a\|\|^2. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C3.tangential_and_normal_components`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.tangential_component`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.normal_component`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.tangential_normal_sq`
- **3:§3.4 projectile motion equations p.289**, p.289 [CA-500]: Under gravity only, with initial position r0 and initial velocity v0, r(t) = -(1/2) g t^2 j + v0 t + r0; launched at angle theta with speed v0 from the origin it is (v0 cos theta t, v0 sin theta t - g t^2/2). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C3.projectile_motion_equations`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.projectile_position`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.projectile_launch`
- **3:§3.4 launch-angle form p.290**, p.290 [CA-500]: Under gravity only, with initial position r0 and initial velocity v0, r(t) = -(1/2) g t^2 j + v0 t + r0; launched at angle theta with speed v0 from the origin it is (v0 cos theta t, v0 sin theta t - g t^2/2). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C3.projectile_motion_equations`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.projectile_position`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.projectile_launch`
- **3:§3.4 range remark p.293**, p.293 [CA-501]: Without air resistance the horizontal range at fixed speed is maximised by a 45 degree launch angle. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C3.projectile_range_maximal`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.projectile_range_formula`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.range_max_at_pi_div_four`
- **3:Theorem 3.9 p.293**, p.293 [CA-502]: Kepler: planetary orbits are ellipses with the Sun at a focus; equal areas are swept in equal times; the squares of periods are proportional to the cubes of semimajor axes. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C3.kepler_laws`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.kepler_polar_equation`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.kepler_LRL_const`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.kepler_orbit_bounded`
- **3:§3.4 Equation 3.30 p.295**, p.295 [CA-503]: Newton's form of Kepler's third law: P^2 = 4 pi^2 a^3 / (G (M+m)) for a body of mass m orbiting mass M. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C3.keplers_third_law`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.kepler_third_law`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ellipse_focal_polar_area`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.integral_inv_one_add_mul_cos_sq`

## Volume 3, Chapter 4

60 items; 53 with a Lean name; 18 proved proved here; 35 in Mathlib; 7 not a result.


#### Section 4.1

- **3:§4.1 Definition (function of two variables) p.310**, p.310 [CA-504]: A function of two variables assigns to each pair in a subset D of the plane exactly one real number; D is its domain and the set of attained values is its range. — *not a result* — –
- **3:§4.1 Definition (graph of a function of two variables, surface) p.312**, p.312 [CA-505]: The graph of a function of two variables is the set of triples (x,y,f(x,y)) in space, called a surface. — *not a result* — –
- **3:§4.1 Definition (level curve, contour map) p.316**, p.316 [CA-506]: The level curve of f at height c is the set of points (x,y) with f(x,y)=c; a collection of level curves is a contour map. — *not a result* — –
- **3:§4.1 Definition (vertical trace) p.318**, p.318 [CA-507]: A vertical trace of f is the curve obtained by fixing x=a or y=b in the graph of z=f(x,y). — *not a result* — –
- **3:§4.1 Definition (level surface) p.321**, p.321 [CA-508]: The level surface of a function of three variables at value c is the set of points (x,y,z) with f(x,y,z)=c. — *not a result* — –

#### Section 4.2

- **3:§4.2 Definition (disk) p.325**, p.325 [CA-509]: The open disk (plane) or open ball (space, higher dimensions) of radius delta about a point is the set of points at distance less than delta from it. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.disk_eq_dist_lt`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.ball_eq_dist_lt`, `Metric.ball`
- **3:§4.2 Definition (limit of a function of two variables) p.325**, p.325 [CA-510]: The limit of f at an interior point (a,b) of the domain equals L if for every epsilon there is a delta so that points in the delta-disk around (a,b), other than (a,b), have f within epsilon of L; same definition with balls for three or more variables. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.limit_two_variables_iff`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.limit_three_variables_iff`, `Metric.tendsto_nhds`, `Filter.Tendsto`
- **3:Theorem 4.1 p.326**, p.326 [CA-512]: Limit laws for functions of two variables: constant, identity, sum, difference, constant multiple, product, quotient (nonzero limit of denominator), positive-integer power, and root laws; they also hold for three or more variables. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.limit_laws`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.limit_const_and_coordinate`, `Filter.Tendsto.mul`, `Filter.Tendsto.const_mul`
- **3:§4.2 Principle (limit fails if path-dependent) p.328**, p.328 [CA-513]: If f has limit L at a point, then its restriction along any path or line approaching the point has the same limit, so two paths giving different values show the limit does not exist. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.limit_fails_if_path_dependent`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.not_limit_of_two_paths`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.no_limit_xy_over_sq_sum`, `Filter.Tendsto.comp`
- **3:Example 4.9 p.328**, p.328 [CA-513]: If f has limit L at a point, then its restriction along any path or line approaching the point has the same limit, so two paths giving different values show the limit does not exist. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.limit_fails_if_path_dependent`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.not_limit_of_two_paths`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.no_limit_xy_over_sq_sum`, `Filter.Tendsto.comp`
- **3:§4.2 Definition (interior point, boundary point) p.330**, p.330 [CA-514]: A point of S is an interior point if some disk about it lies in S, and a boundary point if every disk about it meets both S and its complement. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.interior_point_iff`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.boundary_point_iff`, `interior`, `frontier`
- **3:§4.2 Definition (limit at a boundary point) p.331**, p.331 [CA-511]: The limit of f at a boundary point of the domain is defined with epsilon-delta using only the domain points in a small disk around the point. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.limit_at_boundary_point_iff`, `Metric.tendsto_nhdsWithin_nhds`
- **3:§4.2 Definition (open set, closed set) p.331**, p.331 [CA-515]: A set is open if every point is an interior point and closed if it contains all its boundary points. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.open_set_iff`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.closed_set_iff`, `IsOpen`, `IsClosed`
- **3:§4.2 Definition (connected set, region) p.331**, p.331 [CA-516]: An open set is connected if it is not a union of two disjoint nonempty open sets; a region is an open, connected, nonempty set. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.region_iff`, `IsConnected`, `IsPreconnected`, `isPreconnected_iff_subset_of_disjoint`
- **3:§4.2 Definition (continuity of a function of two variables) p.333**, p.333 [CA-517]: A function of two (or more) variables is continuous at a point of its domain if the limit exists there, the function value exists, and they agree; equivalently by epsilon-delta. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.continuousAt_iff_epsilon_delta`, `ContinuousAt`, `Metric.continuousAt_iff`
- **3:Theorem 4.2 p.333**, p.333 [CA-518]: The sum of functions continuous at a point is continuous at that point. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.continuousAt_add`
- **3:Theorem 4.3 p.334**, p.334 [CA-519]: The product of functions continuous at a point is continuous at that point. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.continuousAt_mul`, `ContinuousAt.mul`
- **3:Theorem 4.4 p.334**, p.334 [CA-520]: If g is continuous at a point and h of one variable is continuous at g of that point, then h composed with g is continuous there. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.continuousAt_comp`, `ContinuousAt.comp`
- **3:§4.2 Definition (ball in three or more dimensions) p.335**, p.335 [CA-509]: The open disk (plane) or open ball (space, higher dimensions) of radius delta about a point is the set of points at distance less than delta from it. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.disk_eq_dist_lt`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.ball_eq_dist_lt`, `Metric.ball`

#### Section 4.3

- **3:§4.3 Definition (partial derivatives of a function of two variables) p.338**, p.338 [CA-521]: The partial derivatives of f(x,y) are the limits of the difference quotients in x with y fixed and in y with x fixed; they are computed by treating the other variable as constant and using the one-variable rules. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.partial_derivatives_two_variables_iff`, `HasLineDerivAt`, `lineDeriv`, `HasDerivAt`
- **3:§4.3 Definition (partial derivatives of a function of three variables) p.343**, p.343 [CA-522]: The partial derivatives of f(x,y,z) in x, y, z are the one-variable derivative limits holding the other two variables fixed. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.partial_derivatives_three_variables_iff`, `HasLineDerivAt`, `lineDeriv`
- **3:§4.3 Definition (higher-order and mixed partial derivatives) p.345**, p.345 [CA-523]: Higher-order partial derivatives are partials of partials; mixed partials differentiate with respect to different variables. — *not a result* — –
- **3:Theorem 4.5 p.346**, p.346 [CA-524]: If the mixed second partial derivatives f_xy and f_yx are continuous on a disk around a point then they are equal there (Clairaut). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.clairaut`, `ContDiffAt.isSymmSndFDerivAt`, `second_derivative_symmetric_of_eventually`, `IsSymmSndFDerivAt`
- **3:§4.3 Definition (partial differential equation) p.347**, p.347 [CA-525]: A partial differential equation is an equation involving an unknown function of several variables and some of its partial derivatives. — *not a result* — –

- **3:Equation 4.18 p.347**, p.347 [CA-526]: Named examples of PDEs: the two-dimensional heat equation, the two-dimensional wave equation and Laplace's equation. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.classical_pde_examples`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.PDEExamples.HeatEq2D`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.PDEExamples.WaveEq2D`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.PDEExamples.LaplaceEq2D`
- **3:Equation 4.19 p.347**, p.347 [CA-526]: Named examples of PDEs: the two-dimensional heat equation, the two-dimensional wave equation and Laplace's equation. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.classical_pde_examples`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.PDEExamples.HeatEq2D`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.PDEExamples.WaveEq2D`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.PDEExamples.LaplaceEq2D`

#### Section 4.4

- **3:§4.4 Definition (tangent plane) p.355**, p.355 [CA-527]: The tangent plane to a surface at a point is the plane containing the tangent lines of all curves through the point lying in the surface, when they are coplanar. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.tangent_lines_lie_in_tangent_plane`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.curve_tangent_in_tangent_plane`
- **3:§4.4 Definition (equation of the tangent plane) p.356**, p.356 [CA-528]: For a differentiable f the tangent plane to z=f(x,y) at (a,b) is z = f(a,b) + f_x(a,b)(x-a) + f_y(a,b)(y-b). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.tangent_plane_equation`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.tangentPlane`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.tangent_plane_unique`
- **3:§4.4 Definition (linear approximation) p.359**, p.359 [CA-529]: Near (a,b), f(x,y) is approximated by the tangent-plane function f(a,b)+f_x(a,b)(x-a)+f_y(a,b)(y-b). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.linear_approximation_two_variables`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.linear_approximation`, `HasFDerivAt`, `hasFDerivAt_iff_isLittleO_nhds_zero`
- **3:§4.4 Definition (differentiable, two variables) p.361**, p.361 [CA-530]: f is differentiable at (a,b) (resp. (a,b,c)) if f(x,y)-f(a,b) equals f_x(a,b)(x-a)+f_y(a,b)(y-b) plus an error term that is small compared to the distance to the point. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.differentiable_two_variables_iff`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.differentiable_three_variables_iff`, `DifferentiableAt`, `HasFDerivAt`
- **3:Theorem 4.6 p.363**, p.363 [CA-531]: A function of two (or three) variables differentiable at a point is continuous at that point. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.continuousAt_of_differentiableAt_two`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.continuousAt_of_differentiableAt_three`, `DifferentiableAt.continuousAt`, `HasFDerivAt.continuousAt`
- **3:Theorem 4.7 p.363**, p.363 [CA-532]: If the first partial derivatives of f exist near a point and are continuous at it, then f is differentiable there (also for three variables). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.differentiableAt_of_continuous_partials`, `hasStrictFDerivAt_uncurry_coprod`, `HasStrictFDerivAt.hasFDerivAt`
- **3:§4.4 Definition (differentials, total differential) p.364**, p.364 [CA-533]: The total differential is dz = f_x(a,b)dx + f_y(a,b)dy and approximates the change in f for small increments dx, dy. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.total_differential_approximates_increment`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.totalDifferential`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.total_differential_approx`, `fderiv`
- **3:§4.4 Definition (differentiable, three variables) p.366**, p.366 [CA-530]: f is differentiable at (a,b) (resp. (a,b,c)) if f(x,y)-f(a,b) equals f_x(a,b)(x-a)+f_y(a,b)(y-b) plus an error term that is small compared to the distance to the point. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.differentiable_two_variables_iff`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.differentiable_three_variables_iff`, `DifferentiableAt`, `HasFDerivAt`
- **3:§4.4 Fact (differentiable implies continuous, three variables) p.366**, p.366 [CA-531]: A function of two (or three) variables differentiable at a point is continuous at that point. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.continuousAt_of_differentiableAt_two`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.continuousAt_of_differentiableAt_three`, `DifferentiableAt.continuousAt`, `HasFDerivAt.continuousAt`
- **3:§4.4 Fact (continuous first partials imply differentiable, three variables) p.366**, p.366 [CA-532]: If the first partial derivatives of f exist near a point and are continuous at it, then f is differentiable there (also for three variables). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.differentiableAt_of_continuous_partials`, `hasStrictFDerivAt_uncurry_coprod`, `HasStrictFDerivAt.hasFDerivAt`
- **3:Theorem 4.8 p.369**, p.369 [CA-534]: If x(t), y(t) are differentiable and z=f(x,y) is differentiable, then dz/dt = f_x dx/dt + f_y dy/dt. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.chain_rule_one_parameter`, `HasFDerivAt.comp_hasDerivAt`
- **3:Theorem 4.9 p.373**, p.373 [CA-535]: If x(u,v), y(u,v) are differentiable and z=f(x,y) is differentiable then z_u = f_x x_u + f_y y_u and z_v = f_x x_v + f_y y_v. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.chain_rule_two_parameters`, `HasFDerivAt.comp`
- **3:Theorem 4.10 p.375**, p.375 [CA-536]: Generalized chain rule: for f of n variables each depending on m variables, the partial derivative with respect to a variable t_j is the sum over i of f_{x_i} times ∂x_i/∂t_j. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.generalized_chain_rule`, `HasFDerivAt.comp`
- **3:Theorem 4.11 p.377**, p.377 [CA-537]: If F(x,y)=0 defines y implicitly as a function of x then dy/dx = -F_x/F_y (F_y nonzero); if F(x,y,z)=0 defines z then z_x = -F_x/F_z and z_y = -F_y/F_z. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.implicit_derivative_two_variables`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.implicit_derivative_three_variables_x`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.implicit_derivative_three_variables_y`, `hasStrictFDerivAt_implicitFunctionOfBivariate`

#### Section 4.6

- **3:§4.6 Definition (directional derivative, two variables) p.383**, p.383 [CA-538]: The directional derivative of f at a point in the direction of a unit vector u is the limit as t→0 of (f(point+tu)-f(point))/t; the same for three variables (unit vector components are the direction cosines). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.directional_derivative_iff`, `HasLineDerivAt`, `lineDeriv`
- **3:Theorem 4.12 p.384**, p.384 [CA-540]: For differentiable f the directional derivative in the unit direction u equals the gradient dotted with u (f_x cos θ + f_y sin θ in the plane). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.directional_derivative_eq_inner_gradient`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.directional_derivative_plane`, `HasFDerivAt.hasLineDerivAt`, `inner_gradient_left`
- **3:§4.6 Definition (gradient, two variables) p.385**, p.385 [CA-539]: The gradient of f is the vector of its partial derivatives (f_x,f_y) (or (f_x,f_y,f_z)). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.gradient_component_eq_partial`, `gradient`, `HasGradientAt`, `inner_gradient_left`
- **3:Theorem 4.13 p.386**, p.386 [CA-541]: If the gradient is zero all directional derivatives are zero; otherwise the directional derivative is largest (value \|∇f\|) in the gradient direction and smallest (value -\|∇f\|) in the opposite direction. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.directional_derivative_extremes`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.directionalDeriv_abs_le`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.directionalDeriv_eq_zero_of_grad_zero`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.directionalDeriv_max_iff`
- **3:Theorem 4.14 p.389**, p.389 [CA-542]: If f has continuous first partials near a point and nonzero gradient there, the gradient is perpendicular to the level curve of f through the point. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.gradient_perpendicular_to_level_curve`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.gradient_orthogonal_level_curve`
- **3:§4.6 Definition (gradient, three variables) p.390**, p.390 [CA-539]: The gradient of f is the vector of its partial derivatives (f_x,f_y) (or (f_x,f_y,f_z)). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.gradient_component_eq_partial`, `gradient`, `HasGradientAt`, `inner_gradient_left`
- **3:§4.6 Definition (directional derivative, three variables) p.391**, p.391 [CA-538]: The directional derivative of f at a point in the direction of a unit vector u is the limit as t→0 of (f(point+tu)-f(point))/t; the same for three variables (unit vector components are the direction cosines). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.directional_derivative_iff`, `HasLineDerivAt`, `lineDeriv`

#### Section 4.7

- **3:§4.7 Definition (critical point) p.395**, p.395 [CA-543]: A point is critical for f if both first partial derivatives vanish there or at least one does not exist. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.critical_point_characterization`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsCriticalPoint`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.isCriticalPoint_iff`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.isCriticalPoint_of_isLocalExtr`
- **3:§4.7 Definition (local and global extrema) p.397**, p.397 [CA-544]: f has a local maximum (minimum) at a point if f is at most (at least) its value there on a disk about the point; global when this holds on the whole domain. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.local_max_iff_disk`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.local_min_iff_disk`, `IsLocalMax`, `IsLocalMin`
- **3:Theorem 4.16 p.397**, p.397 [CA-545]: If f has a local extremum at a point where the partials exist, that point is a critical point (the partials vanish). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.local_extremum_partials_zero`, `IsLocalExtr.fderiv_eq_zero`, `IsLocalMin.fderiv_eq_zero`, `IsLocalMax.fderiv_eq_zero`
- **3:§4.7 Definition (saddle point) p.398**, p.398 [CA-546]: A saddle point is a critical point with vanishing first partials that is not a local extremum. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.saddle_point_characterization`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsSaddlePoint`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.isSaddlePoint_iff`
- **3:Theorem 4.17 p.398**, p.398 [CA-547]: With D = f_xx f_yy - (f_xy)^2 at a critical point: D>0 and f_xx>0 gives a local minimum, D>0 and f_xx<0 a local maximum, D<0 a saddle point, D=0 inconclusive. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.second_derivative_test`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.second_derivative_test_min`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.second_derivative_test_max`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.second_derivative_test_saddle`
- **3:§4.7 Definition (bounded set) p.401**, p.401 [CA-548]: A set is bounded if it is contained in a disk (ball) of finite radius. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.bounded_iff_subset_disk`, `Bornology.IsBounded`, `Metric.isBounded_iff_subset_closedBall`
- **3:Theorem 4.18 p.402**, p.402 [CA-549]: A continuous function on a closed bounded set in the plane attains an absolute maximum and an absolute minimum. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.extreme_value_closed_bounded`, `IsCompact.exists_isMinOn`, `IsCompact.exists_isMaxOn`, `Metric.isCompact_of_isClosed_isBounded`
- **3:Theorem 4.19 p.402**, p.402 [CA-550]: For a differentiable f on a closed bounded set, the absolute extreme values are among the values at interior critical points and on the boundary. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.absolute_extrema_closed_bounded`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.absolute_extrema_on_compact`, `IsCompact.exists_isMinOn`, `IsMinOn.isLocalMin`
- **3:Theorem 4.20 p.412**, p.412 [CA-551]: If f restricted to the curve g(x,y)=c has a local extremum at a point where the gradient of g is nonzero, then the gradient of f there equals lambda times the gradient of g; same method for three variables. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.lagrange_multipliers_one_constraint`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.lagrange_multiplier`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.lagrange_multiplier_two_vars`, `IsLocalExtrOn.exists_multipliers_of_hasStrictFDerivAt_1d`

#### Section 4.8

- **3:§4.8 Method (Lagrange multipliers, three variables) p.415**, p.415 [CA-551]: If f restricted to the curve g(x,y)=c has a local extremum at a point where the gradient of g is nonzero, then the gradient of f there equals lambda times the gradient of g; same method for three variables. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.lagrange_multipliers_one_constraint`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.lagrange_multiplier`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.lagrange_multiplier_two_vars`, `IsLocalExtrOn.exists_multipliers_of_hasStrictFDerivAt_1d`

- **3:Example 5.1 p.430**, p.430 [CA-553]: The double integral of f over a rectangle R=[a,b]x[c,d] is the limit of the double Riemann sums sum f(x_ij*,y_ij*) dA as the grid is refined (m,n to infinity); for f>=0 it is the volume under the graph. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.double_integral_rectangle_riemann_sums`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.tendsto_doubleSum`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.doubleSum_error_le`, `BoxIntegral.HasIntegral`

- **3:Theorem 5.8 p.477**, p.477 [CA-574]: For f continuous on the polar region alpha<=theta<=beta, h1(theta)<=r<=h2(theta), the double integral equals the iterated integral of f(r cos t, r sin t) r dr dtheta (dr inside). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.double_integral_polar_region`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.setIntegral_polarRegionBetween`, `integral_comp_polarCoord_symm`, `MeasureTheory.setIntegral_prod`
- **3:Example 5.28 p.477**, p.477 [CA-574]: For f continuous on the polar region alpha<=theta<=beta, h1(theta)<=r<=h2(theta), the double integral equals the iterated integral of f(r cos t, r sin t) r dr dtheta (dr inside). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.double_integral_polar_region`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.setIntegral_polarRegionBetween`, `integral_comp_polarCoord_symm`, `MeasureTheory.setIntegral_prod`
- **3:Example 5.32 Analysis p.480**, p.480 [CA-576]: The volume of a right circular cone of radius R and height h is (1/3) pi R^2 h. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.volume_right_circular_cone`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.volume_cone`
- **3:Section 5.3 exercise on Gaussian integral p.483**, p.483 [CA-575]: The integral of exp(-(x^2+y^2)) over the whole plane is pi, and hence the integral of exp(-x^2) over the real line is sqrt(pi). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.gaussian_integral_plane`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.gaussian_integral_line`, `integral_gaussian`, `MeasureTheory.integral_prod_mul`

#### Section 5.4

- **3:§5.4 text on integrability (continuity sufficient) p.494**, p.494 [CA-578]: A continuous function (more generally bounded and continuous except on the boundary) is integrable over a box in R^3. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.integrable_of_continuousOn_box`, `BoxIntegral.integrable_of_continuousOn`, `BoxIntegral.integrable_of_bounded_and_ae_continuousWithinAt`, `ContinuousOn.integrableOn_compact`
- **3:Theorem 5.9 p.494**, p.494 [CA-579]: Fubini for triple integrals: for f continuous on a box the triple integral equals the iterated integral, and any of the six orders of integration gives the same value. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.fubini_box_dz_dy_dx`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.fubini_box_dy_dz_dx`, `MeasureTheory.integral_prod`, `MeasureTheory.integral_prod_symm`
- **3:Definition (triple integral over a box, eq. 5.10) p.494**, p.494 [CA-577]: The triple integral of f over a box is the limit of triple Riemann sums over subboxes as the subdivision is refined. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.triple_integral_box_riemann_sums`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.tendsto_tripleSum`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.tripleSum_error_le`, `BoxIntegral.HasIntegral`
- **3:Example 5.36 p.494**, p.494 [CA-577]: The triple integral of f over a box is the limit of triple Riemann sums over subboxes as the subdivision is refined. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.triple_integral_box_riemann_sums`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.tendsto_tripleSum`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.tripleSum_error_le`, `BoxIntegral.HasIntegral`
- **3:Example 5.37 p.495**, p.495 [CA-579]: Fubini for triple integrals: for f continuous on a box the triple integral equals the iterated integral, and any of the six orders of integration gives the same value. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.fubini_box_dz_dy_dx`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.fubini_box_dy_dz_dx`, `MeasureTheory.integral_prod`, `MeasureTheory.integral_prod_symm`
- **3:Theorem 5.10 p.496**, p.496 [CA-580]: For a solid between two surfaces z=u1(x,y) and z=u2(x,y) over a planar region D (and analogously for projections to other planes), the triple integral equals the double integral over D of the integral in z; for Type I or II shadows it becomes a triple iterated integral. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.triple_integral_over_solid`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.setIntegral_solid`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.setIntegral_solid_typeI`, `MeasureTheory.setIntegral_prod`
- **3:Example 5.38 p.498**, p.498 [CA-580]: For a solid between two surfaces z=u1(x,y) and z=u2(x,y) over a planar region D (and analogously for projections to other planes), the triple integral equals the double integral over D of the integral in z; for Type I or II shadows it becomes a triple iterated integral. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.triple_integral_over_solid`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.setIntegral_solid`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.setIntegral_solid_typeI`, `MeasureTheory.setIntegral_prod`
- **3:§5.4 text, volume as triple integral of 1 p.499**, p.499 [CA-581]: The volume of a solid region is the triple integral of the constant 1 over it. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.volume_eq_triple_integral_one`, `MeasureTheory.setIntegral_const`
- **3:Example 5.39 p.499**, p.499 [CA-581]: The volume of a solid region is the triple integral of the constant 1 over it. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.volume_eq_triple_integral_one`, `MeasureTheory.setIntegral_const`
- **3:Example 5.40 p.500**, p.500 [CA-580]: For a solid between two surfaces z=u1(x,y) and z=u2(x,y) over a planar region D (and analogously for projections to other planes), the triple integral equals the double integral over D of the integral in z; for Type I or II shadows it becomes a triple iterated integral. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.triple_integral_over_solid`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.setIntegral_solid`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.setIntegral_solid_typeI`, `MeasureTheory.setIntegral_prod`
- **3:Theorem 5.11 p.503**, p.503 [CA-582]: The average value of f over a solid of positive volume is the triple integral of f divided by the volume. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.average_value_solid`, `MeasureTheory.setAverage_eq`
- **3:Example 5.42 p.503**, p.503 [CA-582]: The average value of f over a solid of positive volume is the triple integral of f divided by the volume. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.average_value_solid`, `MeasureTheory.setAverage_eq`
- **3:Definition (triple integral in cylindrical coordinates, eq. 5.11) p.513**, p.513 [CA-583]: A triple integral in cylindrical coordinates over a box in (r,theta,z) is a limit of Riemann sums with volume element r dz dr dtheta, and equals the rectangular triple integral of f(r cos t, r sin t, z): dV = r dz dr dtheta. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.triple_integral_cylindrical_coordinates`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.setIntegral_cylindricalCoord_image`, `integral_comp_polarCoord_symm`, `MeasureTheory.integral_prod`
- **3:Theorem 5.12 p.513**, p.513 [CA-584]: Fubini in cylindrical coordinates: for f continuous on a cylindrical box the integral equals the iterated integral of f times r over dr dtheta dz in any of the six orders. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.fubini_cylindrical_coordinates`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.fubini_cylindrical`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.setIntegral_cylindricalCoord_image`, `integral_comp_polarCoord_symm`
- **3:Example 5.43 p.514**, p.514 [CA-583]: A triple integral in cylindrical coordinates over a box in (r,theta,z) is a limit of Riemann sums with volume element r dz dr dtheta, and equals the rectangular triple integral of f(r cos t, r sin t, z): dV = r dz dr dtheta. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.triple_integral_cylindrical_coordinates`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.setIntegral_cylindricalCoord_image`, `integral_comp_polarCoord_symm`, `MeasureTheory.integral_prod`
- **3:Example 5.44 p.514**, p.514 [CA-584]: Fubini in cylindrical coordinates: for f continuous on a cylindrical box the integral equals the iterated integral of f times r over dr dtheta dz in any of the six orders. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.fubini_cylindrical_coordinates`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.fubini_cylindrical`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.setIntegral_cylindricalCoord_image`, `integral_comp_polarCoord_symm`
- **3:Example 5.45 p.515**, p.515 [CA-584]: Fubini in cylindrical coordinates: for f continuous on a cylindrical box the integral equals the iterated integral of f times r over dr dtheta dz in any of the six orders. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.fubini_cylindrical_coordinates`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.fubini_cylindrical`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.setIntegral_cylindricalCoord_image`, `integral_comp_polarCoord_symm`
- **3:Example 5.46 p.516**, p.516 [CA-584]: Fubini in cylindrical coordinates: for f continuous on a cylindrical box the integral equals the iterated integral of f times r over dr dtheta dz in any of the six orders. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.fubini_cylindrical_coordinates`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.fubini_cylindrical`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.setIntegral_cylindricalCoord_image`, `integral_comp_polarCoord_symm`
- **3:Definition (triple integral in spherical coordinates) p.519**, p.519 [CA-585]: A triple integral in spherical coordinates (rho,theta,phi) has volume element rho^2 sin(phi) drho dphi dtheta and equals the rectangular triple integral; the Jacobian of the spherical coordinate map is rho^2 sin(phi); over a general solid the integral is an iterated integral with variable limits. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.triple_integral_spherical_coordinates`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.det_sphericalJacobianMatrix`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.det_sphericalFDeriv`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.setIntegral_sphericalCoord_image`

#### Section 5.6

- **3:§5.6 definition of center of mass and centroid p.536**, p.536 [CA-591]: The center of mass of a lamina is (M_y/m, M_x/m); for constant density it is the centroid (integral of x,y over D divided by the area). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.lamina_center_of_mass`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.MassMoments.laminaCenterOfMass_const`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.MassMoments.laminaCenterOfMass_fst_eq_zero_of_symm`
- **3:§5.6 mass of a lamina (eq. 5.13) p.537**, p.537 [CA-589]: The mass of a lamina occupying a region D with density rho(x,y) is the double integral of rho over D. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.lamina_mass`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.MassMoments.laminaMass_const`
- **3:Example 5.55 p.537**, p.537 [CA-589]: The mass of a lamina occupying a region D with density rho(x,y) is the double integral of rho over D. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.lamina_mass`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.MassMoments.laminaMass_const`
- **3:§5.6 moments M_x, M_y of a lamina p.538**, p.538 [CA-590]: The moments of a lamina about the x-axis and y-axis are the double integrals of y rho and x rho respectively. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.lamina_moments`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.MassMoments.laminaMomentY_eq_zero_of_symm`
- **3:Example 5.56 p.538**, p.538 [CA-590]: The moments of a lamina about the x-axis and y-axis are the double integrals of y rho and x rho respectively. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.lamina_moments`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.MassMoments.laminaMomentY_eq_zero_of_symm`
- **3:Example 5.57 p.539**, p.539 [CA-591]: The center of mass of a lamina is (M_y/m, M_x/m); for constant density it is the centroid (integral of x,y over D divided by the area). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.lamina_center_of_mass`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.MassMoments.laminaCenterOfMass_const`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.MassMoments.laminaCenterOfMass_fst_eq_zero_of_symm`
- **3:Example 5.58 p.540**, p.540 [CA-591]: The center of mass of a lamina is (M_y/m, M_x/m); for constant density it is the centroid (integral of x,y over D divided by the area). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.lamina_center_of_mass`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.MassMoments.laminaCenterOfMass_const`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.MassMoments.laminaCenterOfMass_fst_eq_zero_of_symm`
- **3:Example 5.59 p.542**, p.542 [CA-591]: The center of mass of a lamina is (M_y/m, M_x/m); for constant density it is the centroid (integral of x,y over D divided by the area). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.lamina_center_of_mass`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.MassMoments.laminaCenterOfMass_const`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.MassMoments.laminaCenterOfMass_fst_eq_zero_of_symm`
- **3:§5.6 moments of inertia I_x, I_y, I_0 p.543**, p.543 [CA-592]: The moments of inertia of a lamina about the x-axis, y-axis and origin are the double integrals of y^2 rho, x^2 rho, and (x^2+y^2) rho (the polar moment is the sum of the other two). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.lamina_moments_of_inertia`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.MassMoments.laminaInertiaO_eq`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.MassMoments.laminaInertiaVLine_parallel_axis`
- **3:§5.6 radii of gyration p.543**, p.543 [CA-593]: The radii of gyration are the square roots of the moments of inertia divided by the mass (about the x-axis, y-axis, and origin). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.lamina_radii_of_gyration`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.MassMoments.laminaRadiusGyrationX_sq`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.MassMoments.laminaRadiusGyrationY_sq`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.MassMoments.laminaRadiusGyrationO_sq`
- **3:Example 5.60 p.543**, p.543 [CA-592]: The moments of inertia of a lamina about the x-axis, y-axis and origin are the double integrals of y^2 rho, x^2 rho, and (x^2+y^2) rho (the polar moment is the sum of the other two). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.lamina_moments_of_inertia`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.MassMoments.laminaInertiaO_eq`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.MassMoments.laminaInertiaVLine_parallel_axis`
- **3:Example 5.61 p.544**, p.544 [CA-593]: The radii of gyration are the square roots of the moments of inertia divided by the mass (about the x-axis, y-axis, and origin). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.lamina_radii_of_gyration`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.MassMoments.laminaRadiusGyrationX_sq`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.MassMoments.laminaRadiusGyrationY_sq`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.MassMoments.laminaRadiusGyrationO_sq`
- **3:§5.6 definition (mass of a solid, 3D) p.545**, p.545 [CA-594]: The mass of a solid with density rho(x,y,z) is the triple integral of rho over the solid. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.solid_mass`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.MassMoments.solidMass_const`
- **3:§5.6 definition (moments of a solid) p.545**, p.545 [CA-595]: The moments of a solid about the coordinate planes are the triple integrals of x rho, y rho, z rho (appropriately paired with the planes). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.solid_moments`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.MassMoments.solidCenterOfMass_const`
- **3:§5.6 definition (center of mass of a solid) p.545**, p.545 [CA-596]: The center of mass of a solid is (moment about yz-plane, xz-plane, xy-plane) divided by the mass; with constant density this is the centroid. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.solid_center_of_mass`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.MassMoments.solidCenterOfMass_const`
- **3:§5.6 definition (moments of inertia of a solid) p.545**, p.545 [CA-597]: The moments of inertia of a solid about the coordinate axes are the triple integrals of (y^2+z^2) rho, (x^2+z^2) rho, (x^2+y^2) rho. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.solid_moments_of_inertia`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.MassMoments.solidInertia_add`
- **3:Example 5.62 p.545**, p.545 [CA-594]: The mass of a solid with density rho(x,y,z) is the triple integral of rho over the solid. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.solid_mass`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.MassMoments.solidMass_const`
- **3:Example 5.63 p.546**, p.546 [CA-596]: The center of mass of a solid is (moment about yz-plane, xz-plane, xy-plane) divided by the mass; with constant density this is the centroid. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.solid_center_of_mass`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.MassMoments.solidCenterOfMass_const`
- **3:Example 5.64 p.547**, p.547 [CA-597]: The moments of inertia of a solid about the coordinate axes are the triple integrals of (y^2+z^2) rho, (x^2+z^2) rho, (x^2+y^2) rho. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.solid_moments_of_inertia`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.MassMoments.solidInertia_add`

#### Section 5.7

- **3:§5.7 text, planar transformation p.554**, p.554 [CA-598]: A transformation (change of variables) T(u,v)=(x(u,v),y(u,v)) maps a region in the uv-plane to a region in the xy-plane; it is C^1 if the component functions have continuous first partial derivatives. — *not a result* — –
- **3:Example 5.65 p.554**, p.554 [CA-599]: A transformation is one-to-one if no two distinct points map to the same image point. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.one_to_one_transformation`, `Set.InjOn`

- **3:Example 5.66 p.555**, p.555 [CA-598]: A transformation (change of variables) T(u,v)=(x(u,v),y(u,v)) maps a region in the uv-plane to a region in the xy-plane; it is C^1 if the component functions have continuous first partial derivatives. — *not a result* — –
- **3:§5.7 text, C^1 transformation p.556**, p.556 [CA-598]: A transformation (change of variables) T(u,v)=(x(u,v),y(u,v)) maps a region in the uv-plane to a region in the xy-plane; it is C^1 if the component functions have continuous first partial derivatives. — *not a result* — –

## Volume 3, Chapter 6

138 items; 124 with a Lean name; 117 proved proved here; 7 in Mathlib; 14 not a result.


#### Section 6.1

- **3:§6.1 Definition (vector field) p.585**, p.585 [CA-605]: A vector field on a subset D of the plane or space assigns a 2- or 3-component vector to each point of D; it is continuous when its component functions are. — *not a result* — –
- **3:Equation 6.1 and 6.2 p.586**, p.586 [CA-605]: A vector field on a subset D of the plane or space assigns a 2- or 3-component vector to each point of D; it is continuous when its component functions are. — *not a result* — –
- **3:Example 6.6 p.592**, p.592 [CA-606]: Normalizing a nonvanishing vector field F gives the unit vector field F/\|F\|, in which every vector has length 1. — *not a result* — –
- **3:§6.1 Definition (gradient field) p.595**, p.595 [CA-607]: A vector field F is a gradient (conservative) field if F = grad f for some scalar function f; f is called a potential function of F. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.gradient_field_definition`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsConservativeOn`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsConservative2`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsConservative3`
- **3:§6.1 Definition (conservative field, potential function) p.596**, p.596 [CA-607]: A vector field F is a gradient (conservative) field if F = grad f for some scalar function f; f is called a potential function of F. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.gradient_field_definition`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsConservativeOn`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsConservative2`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsConservative3`
- **3:Theorem 6.1 p.597**, p.597 [CA-608]: On an open connected domain, two potential functions of the same conservative field differ by a constant. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.potential_unique_up_to_constant`, `IsOpen.exists_eq_add_of_fderiv_eq`
- **3:Theorem 6.2 p.598**, p.598 [CA-609]: If F=(P,Q) (or (P,Q,R)) is conservative with C^1 components then the mixed partials agree: P_y=Q_x (and P_z=R_x, Q_z=R_y). — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.mixed_partials_of_potential`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.mixed_partials_plane`, `second_derivative_symmetric`, `ContDiffAt.isSymmSndFDerivAt`

- **3:§6.2 Definition (scalar line integral) p.602**, p.602 [CA-610]: The scalar line integral of f along a smooth curve C is the limit of Riemann sums sum f(P_i*) Delta s_i over a partition of C into arc-length pieces. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.scalar_line_integral_riemann_sums`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.LineIntegralLimits.line_integral_riemann_sums`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.LineIntegralLimits.line_integral_riemann_sums_plane`

#### Section 6.3

- **3:§6.3 Definition (closed and simple curves) p.624**, p.624 [CA-623]: A curve is closed if it has a parameterization (traversing it once) with r(a)=r(b), and simple if it does not cross itself (a one-to-one parameterization, except possibly at the endpoints). — *not a result* — –
- **3:Example 6.27 p.625**, p.625 [CA-623]: A curve is closed if it has a parameterization (traversing it once) with r(a)=r(b), and simple if it does not cross itself (a one-to-one parameterization, except possibly at the endpoints). — *not a result* — –
- **3:§6.3 Definition (connected, simply connected regions) p.626**, p.626 [CA-624]: A region is connected if any two points are joined by a path in it, and simply connected if moreover every simple closed curve in it can be shrunk continuously to a point inside it. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.simply_connected_region_iff`, `PathConnectedSpace`, `SimplyConnectedSpace`, `simply_connected_iff_loops_nullhomotopic`
