- **3:Example 6.34 p.637**, p.637 [CA-630]: On an open simply connected region, a C^1 field F=(P,Q) with P_y=Q_x (resp. all three cross partials equal in space) is conservative. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.cross_partials_imply_conservative`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.isConservative2_of_pdy_eq_pdx`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.isConservative3_iff_curl3_eq_zero`, `Convex.exists_forall_hasFDerivAt_of_fderiv_symmetric`
- **3:Example 6.35 p.637**, p.637 [CA-630]: On an open simply connected region, a C^1 field F=(P,Q) with P_y=Q_x (resp. all three cross partials equal in space) is conservative. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.cross_partials_imply_conservative`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.isConservative2_of_pdy_eq_pdx`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.isConservative3_iff_curl3_eq_zero`, `Convex.exists_forall_hasFDerivAt_of_fderiv_symmetric`
- **3:Example 6.36 p.638**, p.638 [CA-625]: Fundamental Theorem for Line Integrals: for a piecewise smooth curve r:[a,b] and f with continuous partials on C, the line integral of grad f along C equals f(r(b)) - f(r(a)). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.fundamental_theorem_line_integrals`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.lineIntegral_ftli`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.lineIntegral2_ftli_partials`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.lineIntegral3_ftli_partials`
- **3:Theorem 6.12 p.644**, p.644 [CA-632]: Green's theorem (circulation form): for a positively oriented piecewise smooth simple closed curve C bounding D, the circulation of F=(P,Q) equals the double integral over D of Q_x - P_y. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.green_theorem_circulation_glued_region`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.green_theorem_circulation_param_region`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.green_glued`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.green_cells`
- **3:Equation 6.13 p.644**, p.644 [CA-632]: Green's theorem (circulation form): for a positively oriented piecewise smooth simple closed curve C bounding D, the circulation of F=(P,Q) equals the double integral over D of Q_x - P_y. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.green_theorem_circulation_glued_region`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.green_theorem_circulation_param_region`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.green_glued`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.green_cells`
- **3:Example 6.38 p.646**, p.646 [CA-632]: Green's theorem (circulation form): for a positively oriented piecewise smooth simple closed curve C bounding D, the circulation of F=(P,Q) equals the double integral over D of Q_x - P_y. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.green_theorem_circulation_glued_region`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.green_theorem_circulation_param_region`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.green_glued`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.green_cells`
- **3:Example 6.39 p.647**, p.647 [CA-632]: Green's theorem (circulation form): for a positively oriented piecewise smooth simple closed curve C bounding D, the circulation of F=(P,Q) equals the double integral over D of Q_x - P_y. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.green_theorem_circulation_glued_region`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.green_theorem_circulation_param_region`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.green_glued`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.green_cells`
- **3:Example 6.40 p.648**, p.648 [CA-633]: Area of a region D bounded by a positively oriented simple closed curve C is the integral over C of x dy, equivalently of -y dx, equivalently one half of the integral of x dy - y dx (e.g. area of an ellipse x^2/a^2+y^2/b^2=1 is pi a b). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.area_glued_region_line_integrals`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.area_param_region_line_integrals`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.area_glued`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.area_param_image`
- **3:Theorem 6.13 p.650**, p.650 [CA-634]: Green's theorem (flux form): the flux of F=(P,Q) across the positively oriented boundary C of D equals the double integral over D of P_x + Q_y. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.green_theorem_flux_glued_region`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.green_theorem_flux_param_region`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.green_flux_glued`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.green_flux_param_image`
- **3:Equation 6.14 p.650**, p.650 [CA-633]: Area of a region D bounded by a positively oriented simple closed curve C is the integral over C of x dy, equivalently of -y dx, equivalently one half of the integral of x dy - y dx (e.g. area of an ellipse x^2/a^2+y^2/b^2=1 is pi a b). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.area_glued_region_line_integrals`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.area_param_region_line_integrals`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.area_glued`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.area_param_image`
- **3:Equation 6.15 p.650**, p.650 [CA-634]: Green's theorem (flux form): the flux of F=(P,Q) across the positively oriented boundary C of D equals the double integral over D of P_x + Q_y. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.green_theorem_flux_glued_region`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.green_theorem_flux_param_region`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.green_flux_glued`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.green_flux_param_image`
- **3:Example 6.41 p.651**, p.651 [CA-633]: Area of a region D bounded by a positively oriented simple closed curve C is the integral over C of x dy, equivalently of -y dx, equivalently one half of the integral of x dy - y dx (e.g. area of an ellipse x^2/a^2+y^2/b^2=1 is pi a b). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.area_glued_region_line_integrals`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.area_param_region_line_integrals`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.area_glued`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.area_param_image`
- **3:Example 6.42 p.652**, p.652 [CA-634]: Green's theorem (flux form): the flux of F=(P,Q) across the positively oriented boundary C of D equals the double integral over D of P_x + Q_y. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.green_theorem_flux_glued_region`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.green_theorem_flux_param_region`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.green_flux_glued`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.green_flux_param_image`

#### Section 6.4

- **3:§6.4 text (source free) p.653**, p.653 [CA-635]: A field is source free if its flux across every closed curve is zero; a stream function of F=(P,Q) is g with g_y=P, g_x=-Q. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.source_free_and_stream_function`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsStreamFunction`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsSourceFree2`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsStreamFunction.isSourceFree2`
- **3:§6.4 List (equivalent conditions for source free) p.653**, p.653 [CA-636]: On a simply connected domain these are equivalent for a field F: flux across any closed curve is zero; flux is path independent; F has a stream function; P_x+Q_y=0. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.source_free_equivalent_conditions`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.sourceFree_tfae`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsFluxPathIndependent2`
- **3:Example 6.43 p.653**, p.653 [CA-634]: Green's theorem (flux form): the flux of F=(P,Q) across the positively oriented boundary C of D equals the double integral over D of P_x + Q_y. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.green_theorem_flux_glued_region`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.green_theorem_flux_param_region`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.green_flux_glued`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.green_flux_param_image`
- **3:§6.4 Definition (stream function) p.654**, p.654 [CA-635]: A field is source free if its flux across every closed curve is zero; a stream function of F=(P,Q) is g with g_y=P, g_x=-Q. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.source_free_and_stream_function`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsStreamFunction`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsSourceFree2`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsStreamFunction.isSourceFree2`
- **3:Example 6.44 p.654**, p.654 [CA-635]: A field is source free if its flux across every closed curve is zero; a stream function of F=(P,Q) is g with g_y=P, g_x=-Q. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.source_free_and_stream_function`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsStreamFunction`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsSourceFree2`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsStreamFunction.isSourceFree2`
- **3:§6.4 Definition (harmonic function) p.655**, p.655 [CA-637]: A function satisfying Laplace's equation (sum of second partials equals 0) is called harmonic. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.harmonic_iff_laplace`, `InnerProductSpace.HarmonicAt`, `InnerProductSpace.HarmonicOnNhd`
- **3:§6.4 Fact (potentials of source-free conservative fields are harmonic) p.655**, p.655 [CA-638]: A potential function of a conservative source-free field (in the plane, simply connected domain) is harmonic. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.potential_of_source_free_field_harmonic`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.laplacian2`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.laplacian2_eq_zero_of_potential`
- **3:Example 6.45 p.655**, p.655 [CA-638]: A potential function of a conservative source-free field (in the plane, simply connected domain) is harmonic. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.potential_of_source_free_field_harmonic`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.laplacian2`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.laplacian2_eq_zero_of_potential`
- **3:§6.4 Theorem (Green's theorem on general regions) p.656**, p.656 [CA-639]: Green's theorem extends to regions with finitely many holes: the double integral over D equals the line integral over the whole boundary, outer curve counterclockwise and inner curves clockwise (both forms). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.green_theorem_glued_region_with_holes`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.green_theorem_region_between_curves`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.green_glued`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.green_cells`
- **3:Example 6.46 p.657**, p.657 [CA-639]: Green's theorem extends to regions with finitely many holes: the double integral over D equals the line integral over the whole boundary, outer curve counterclockwise and inner curves clockwise (both forms). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.green_theorem_glued_region_with_holes`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.green_theorem_region_between_curves`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.green_glued`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.green_cells`
- **3:Example 6.47 p.658**, p.658 [CA-640]: For F=(-y,x)/(x^2+y^2) and any positively oriented simple closed curve C missing the origin, the circulation is 0 if C does not enclose the origin and 2 pi if it does. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.swirl_circulation_winding_number`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.swirl_circulation_glued_enclosing`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.swirl_circulation_glued_not_enclosing`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.swirl_circulation_cases`

#### Section 6.5

- **3:§6.5 Definition (divergence) p.668**, p.668 [CA-641]: The divergence of a field F=(P,Q,R) is div F = P_x + Q_y + R_z (planar: P_x+Q_y), a scalar function. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.divergence_formula`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.div2`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.div3`
- **3:Equation 6.16 p.668**, p.668 [CA-641]: The divergence of a field F=(P,Q,R) is div F = P_x + Q_y + R_z (planar: P_x+Q_y), a scalar function. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.divergence_formula`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.div2`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.div3`
- **3:Example 6.48 p.670**, p.670 [CA-641]: The divergence of a field F=(P,Q,R) is div F = P_x + Q_y + R_z (planar: P_x+Q_y), a scalar function. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.divergence_formula`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.div2`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.div3`
- **3:Theorem 6.14 p.671**, p.671 [CA-642]: If F is a source-free continuous field with differentiable components then div F = 0. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.stream_function_field_divergence_free`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.div2_eq_zero_of_isSourceFree2`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsStreamFunction.div2_eq_zero`
- **3:Theorem 6.15 p.672**, p.672 [CA-643]: On a simply connected domain, a continuous field with differentiable components is source free if and only if div F = 0. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.source_free_iff_div_zero`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.isSourceFree2_iff_div2_eq_zero`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.div2_eq_zero_of_isSourceFree2`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.exists_isStreamFunction_of_div2_eq_zero`
- **3:Example 6.50 p.672**, p.672 [CA-643]: On a simply connected domain, a continuous field with differentiable components is source free if and only if div F = 0. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.source_free_iff_div_zero`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.isSourceFree2_iff_div2_eq_zero`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.div2_eq_zero_of_isSourceFree2`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.exists_isStreamFunction_of_div2_eq_zero`
- **3:§6.5 Definition (curl) p.674**, p.674 [CA-644]: The curl of F=(P,Q,R) is (R_y - Q_z, P_z - R_x, Q_x - P_y); for a planar field it is Q_x - P_y (scalar). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.curl_formula`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.curl2`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.curl3`
- **3:Equation 6.17 p.674**, p.674 [CA-644]: The curl of F=(P,Q,R) is (R_y - Q_z, P_z - R_x, Q_x - P_y); for a planar field it is Q_x - P_y (scalar). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.curl_formula`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.curl2`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.curl3`
- **3:Example 6.52 p.674**, p.674 [CA-644]: The curl of F=(P,Q,R) is (R_y - Q_z, P_z - R_x, Q_x - P_y); for a planar field it is Q_x - P_y (scalar). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.curl_formula`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.curl2`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.curl3`
- **3:Example 6.53 p.675**, p.675 [CA-644]: The curl of F=(P,Q,R) is (R_y - Q_z, P_z - R_x, Q_x - P_y); for a planar field it is Q_x - P_y (scalar). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.curl_formula`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.curl2`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.curl3`
- **3:Theorem 6.16 p.676**, p.676 [CA-646]: If F has C^2 components in space then div(curl F) = 0. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.divergence_of_curl_is_zero`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.div3_curl3`
- **3:Example 6.54 p.676**, p.676 [CA-645]: The inverse-square (gravitational) field r/\|r\|^3 has zero curl away from the origin. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.curl_inverse_square_field_zero`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.curl3_invSq`
- **3:Theorem 6.17 p.677**, p.677 [CA-647]: If F is conservative (the gradient of f) then curl F = 0, i.e. curl(grad f) = 0 (also in the plane). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.curl_of_gradient_is_zero`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.curl2_grad`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.curl3_grad`
- **3:Theorem 6.18 p.677**, p.677 [CA-648]: On a simply connected domain, a field with curl F = 0 is conservative; so F is conservative iff curl F = 0 (plane or space). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.conservative_iff_curl_zero`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.isConservative2_iff_curl2_eq_zero`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.isConservative3_iff_curl3_eq_zero`, `Convex.exists_forall_hasFDerivAt_of_fderiv_symmetric`
- **3:Example 6.55 p.677**, p.677 [CA-646]: If F has C^2 components in space then div(curl F) = 0. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.divergence_of_curl_is_zero`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.div3_curl3`
- **3:Example 6.56 p.677**, p.677 [CA-648]: On a simply connected domain, a field with curl F = 0 is conservative; so F is conservative iff curl F = 0 (plane or space). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.conservative_iff_curl_zero`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.isConservative2_iff_curl2_eq_zero`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.isConservative3_iff_curl3_eq_zero`, `Convex.exists_forall_hasFDerivAt_of_fderiv_symmetric`
- **3:Example 6.58 p.682**, p.682 [CA-649]: Standard parameterizations: cylinder x^2+y^2=r^2 by (r cos u, r sin u, v); cone z=k sqrt(x^2+y^2) by (u cos v, u sin v, ku); sphere by spherical coordinates (r sin phi cos theta, r sin phi sin theta, r cos phi); graph z=f(x,y) by (x,y,f(x,y)); surface of revolution (x, f(x) cos t, f(x) sin t). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.standard_surface_parameterizations`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.sphereParam`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.cylinderParam`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.coneParam`

#### Section 6.6

- **3:§6.6 Fact (sphere and graph parameterizations) p.685**, p.685 [CA-649]: Standard parameterizations: cylinder x^2+y^2=r^2 by (r cos u, r sin u, v); cone z=k sqrt(x^2+y^2) by (u cos v, u sin v, ku); sphere by spherical coordinates (r sin phi cos theta, r sin phi sin theta, r cos phi); graph z=f(x,y) by (x,y,f(x,y)); surface of revolution (x, f(x) cos t, f(x) sin t). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.standard_surface_parameterizations`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.sphereParam`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.cylinderParam`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.coneParam`
- **3:Example 6.60 p.685**, p.685 [CA-649]: Standard parameterizations: cylinder x^2+y^2=r^2 by (r cos u, r sin u, v); cone z=k sqrt(x^2+y^2) by (u cos v, u sin v, ku); sphere by spherical coordinates (r sin phi cos theta, r sin phi sin theta, r cos phi); graph z=f(x,y) by (x,y,f(x,y)); surface of revolution (x, f(x) cos t, f(x) sin t). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.standard_surface_parameterizations`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.sphereParam`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.cylinderParam`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.coneParam`
- **3:§6.6 Definition (regular parameterization) p.687**, p.687 [CA-650]: A surface parameterization r(u,v) is regular (smooth) if r_u x r_v is nonzero at every point of the parameter domain. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.regular_parameterization_definition`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsRegularParam`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.graphParam_isRegular`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.cylinderParam_isRegular`
- **3:§6.6 Definition (smooth surface parameterization) p.687**, p.687 [CA-650]: A surface parameterization r(u,v) is regular (smooth) if r_u x r_v is nonzero at every point of the parameter domain. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.regular_parameterization_definition`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsRegularParam`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.graphParam_isRegular`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.cylinderParam_isRegular`
- **3:Example 6.61 p.687**, p.687 [CA-650]: A surface parameterization r(u,v) is regular (smooth) if r_u x r_v is nonzero at every point of the parameter domain. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.regular_parameterization_definition`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsRegularParam`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.graphParam_isRegular`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.cylinderParam_isRegular`
- **3:§6.6 Definition (surface area) p.690**, p.690 [CA-651]: The area of a surface with smooth one-to-one parameterization r on D is the double integral over D of \|r_u x r_v\| du dv. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.surface_area_double_integral`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.paramSurfaceArea`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.paramSurfaceArea_graph`
- **3:Equation 6.18 p.690**, p.690 [CA-651]: The area of a surface with smooth one-to-one parameterization r on D is the double integral over D of \|r_u x r_v\| du dv. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.surface_area_double_integral`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.paramSurfaceArea`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.paramSurfaceArea_graph`
- **3:Example 6.62 p.690**, p.690 [CA-652]: Lateral area of a right circular cone of height h and radius r is pi r sqrt(r^2+h^2); area of a sphere of radius r is 4 pi r^2. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.cone_and_sphere_surface_area`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.paramSurfaceArea_sphere`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.paramSurfaceArea_cone`
- **3:Example 6.63 p.692**, p.692 [CA-652]: Lateral area of a right circular cone of height h and radius r is pi r sqrt(r^2+h^2); area of a sphere of radius r is 4 pi r^2. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.cone_and_sphere_surface_area`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.paramSurfaceArea_sphere`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.paramSurfaceArea_cone`
- **3:Example 6.64 p.693**, p.693 [CA-649]: Standard parameterizations: cylinder x^2+y^2=r^2 by (r cos u, r sin u, v); cone z=k sqrt(x^2+y^2) by (u cos v, u sin v, ku); sphere by spherical coordinates (r sin phi cos theta, r sin phi sin theta, r cos phi); graph z=f(x,y) by (x,y,f(x,y)); surface of revolution (x, f(x) cos t, f(x) sin t). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.standard_surface_parameterizations`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.sphereParam`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.cylinderParam`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.coneParam`
- **3:§6.6 Definition (surface integral of a scalar function) p.694**, p.694 [CA-653]: The surface integral of a scalar function f over a piecewise smooth parametrized surface S is the limit of Riemann sums sum f(P_ij*) Delta S_ij. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.scalar_surface_integral_definition`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.scalarSurfaceIntegral`
- **3:Equation 6.19 p.695**, p.695 [CA-654]: The scalar surface integral equals the double integral over the parameter domain of f(r(u,v)) \|r_u x r_v\| dA. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.scalar_surface_integral_formula`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.scalarSurfaceIntegral`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.scalarSurfaceIntegral_one`
- **3:Example 6.65 p.695**, p.695 [CA-654]: The scalar surface integral equals the double integral over the parameter domain of f(r(u,v)) \|r_u x r_v\| dA. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.scalar_surface_integral_formula`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.scalarSurfaceIntegral`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.scalarSurfaceIntegral_one`
- **3:§6.6 Text (oriented surface, positive orientation, nonorientable) p.699**, p.699 [CA-655]: An orientation of a surface is a continuous choice of unit normal N (for a parameterization N = r_u x r_v/\|r_u x r_v\|); a closed surface has positive (outward) orientation; surfaces such as the Moebius strip are nonorientable. — *not a result* — –
- **3:Example 6.69 p.700**, p.700 [CA-655]: An orientation of a surface is a continuous choice of unit normal N (for a parameterization N = r_u x r_v/\|r_u x r_v\|); a closed surface has positive (outward) orientation; surfaces such as the Moebius strip are nonorientable. — *not a result* — –
- **3:§6.6 Definition (surface integral of a vector field) p.703**, p.703 [CA-656]: The surface integral (flux) of F over an oriented surface S is the integral of F·N dS. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.surface_integral_vector_field`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.surfaceFlux`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.unitNormal`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.surfaceFlux_eq_unitNormal`
- **3:Equation 6.20 p.703**, p.703 [CA-656]: The surface integral (flux) of F over an oriented surface S is the integral of F·N dS. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.surface_integral_vector_field`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.surfaceFlux`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.unitNormal`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.surfaceFlux_eq_unitNormal`
- **3:Equation 6.21 p.703**, p.703 [CA-657]: With parameterization r, the flux of F across S equals the double integral over D of F(r(u,v))·(r_u x r_v) dA. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.flux_parameterized_surface`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.surfaceFlux`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.surfaceFlux_eq_unitNormal`
- **3:Example 6.70 p.704**, p.704 [CA-657]: With parameterization r, the flux of F across S equals the double integral over D of F(r(u,v))·(r_u x r_v) dA. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.flux_parameterized_surface`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.surfaceFlux`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.surfaceFlux_eq_unitNormal`
- **3:Theorem 6.19 p.714**, p.714 [CA-658]: Stokes' theorem: for a piecewise smooth oriented surface S whose boundary is a positively oriented simple closed curve C and a C^1 field F, the circulation of F along C equals the flux of curl F across S. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.stokes_theorem_glued_surface`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.stokes_theorem_param_surface`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.stokes_glued`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.stokes_patch`
- **3:Example 6.73 p.717**, p.717 [CA-658]: Stokes' theorem: for a piecewise smooth oriented surface S whose boundary is a positively oriented simple closed curve C and a C^1 field F, the circulation of F along C equals the flux of curl F across S. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.stokes_theorem_glued_surface`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.stokes_theorem_param_surface`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.stokes_glued`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.stokes_patch`
- **3:Example 6.74 p.718**, p.718 [CA-659]: Two oriented smooth surfaces with the same oriented boundary give the same flux of curl F (surface independence of curl fields). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.curl_flux_glued_surfaces_equal`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.curl_flux_same_boundary_equal`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.curl_flux_glued_independent`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.stokes_glued`
- **3:Equation 6.23 p.719**, p.719 [CA-659]: Two oriented smooth surfaces with the same oriented boundary give the same flux of curl F (surface independence of curl fields). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.curl_flux_glued_surfaces_equal`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.curl_flux_same_boundary_equal`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.curl_flux_glued_independent`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.stokes_glued`
- **3:Example 6.75 p.720**, p.720 [CA-658]: Stokes' theorem: for a piecewise smooth oriented surface S whose boundary is a positively oriented simple closed curve C and a C^1 field F, the circulation of F along C equals the flux of curl F across S. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.stokes_theorem_glued_surface`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.stokes_theorem_param_surface`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.stokes_glued`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.stokes_patch`

#### Section 6.7

- **3:§6.7 Fact (curl as circulation per unit area) p.721**, p.721 [CA-660]: For continuous F, the component of curl F along a unit normal N at P is the limit of circulation around small disks (normal N) divided by their area. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.curl_as_circulation_per_unit_area`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.circulation_per_area_tendsto`
- **3:Theorem 6.20 p.730**, p.730 [CA-661]: Divergence theorem: for a closed piecewise smooth outward-oriented surface S enclosing a solid E and a C^1 field F, the flux of F across S equals the triple integral of div F over E. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.divergence_theorem_glued_solid`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.divergence_theorem_param_solid`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.divergence_glued`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.divergence_cell`
- **3:Equation 6.24 p.730**, p.730 [CA-661]: Divergence theorem: for a closed piecewise smooth outward-oriented surface S enclosing a solid E and a C^1 field F, the flux of F across S equals the triple integral of div F over E. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.divergence_theorem_glued_solid`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.divergence_theorem_param_solid`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.divergence_glued`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.divergence_cell`
- **3:Example 6.77 p.731**, p.731 [CA-661]: Divergence theorem: for a closed piecewise smooth outward-oriented surface S enclosing a solid E and a C^1 field F, the flux of F across S equals the triple integral of div F over E. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.divergence_theorem_glued_solid`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.divergence_theorem_param_solid`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.divergence_glued`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.divergence_cell`
- **3:Example 6.78 p.734**, p.734 [CA-661]: Divergence theorem: for a closed piecewise smooth outward-oriented surface S enclosing a solid E and a C^1 field F, the flux of F across S equals the triple integral of div F over E. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.divergence_theorem_glued_solid`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.divergence_theorem_param_solid`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.divergence_glued`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.divergence_cell`
- **3:Example 6.79 p.734**, p.734 [CA-662]: The divergence of a continuous field at P is the limit of the flux through small spheres around P divided by the enclosed volume (net outflow rate). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.divergence_as_flux_per_unit_volume`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.flux_per_volume_tendsto`

#### Section 6.8

- **3:§6.8 Fact (divergence as flux per unit volume) p.737**, p.737 [CA-662]: The divergence of a continuous field at P is the limit of the flux through small spheres around P divided by the enclosed volume (net outflow rate). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.divergence_as_flux_per_unit_volume`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.flux_per_volume_tendsto`
- **3:Example 6.80 p.737**, p.737 [CA-663]: The inverse-square field r/\|r\|^3 has zero divergence away from the origin. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.divergence_inverse_square_field_zero`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.div3_invSq`
- **3:Theorem 6.21 p.738**, p.738 [CA-664]: The flux of r/\|r\|^3 across a closed piecewise smooth surface S is 0 if S does not enclose the origin and 4 pi if it does. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.flux_invSquare_glued_enclosing_or_not`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.flux_invSquare_enclosing_or_not`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.flux_invSquare_glued_shell`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.flux_invSquare_glued_zero`
- **3:§6.8 Gauss' law p.739**, p.739 [CA-665]: Gauss' law: the flux of the electrostatic field across a closed surface is the enclosed charge divided by the electric constant. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.gauss_law_glued_solid`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.gauss_law_enclosed_or_not`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.gauss_law_glued_shell`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.gauss_law_glued_no_charge`
- **3:Example 6.81 p.739**, p.739 [CA-665]: Gauss' law: the flux of the electrostatic field across a closed surface is the enclosed charge divided by the electric constant. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.gauss_law_glued_solid`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.gauss_law_enclosed_or_not`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.gauss_law_glued_shell`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.gauss_law_glued_no_charge`
- **3:Key Concepts 6.1 p.747**, p.747 [CA-607]: A vector field F is a gradient (conservative) field if F = grad f for some scalar function f; f is called a potential function of F. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.gradient_field_definition`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsConservativeOn`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsConservative2`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsConservative3`
- **3:Key Concepts 6.2 p.747**, p.747 [CA-613]: The scalar line integral of the constant 1 over C is the length of C, i.e. the integral of \|r'(t)\| dt (ds = \|r'\| dt). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.arc_length_as_line_integral`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.arcLength2`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.scalarLineIntegral2_one`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.scalarLineIntegral3_one`
- **3:Key Equations 6 p.747**, p.747 [CA-639]: Green's theorem extends to regions with finitely many holes: the double integral over D equals the line integral over the whole boundary, outer curve counterclockwise and inner curves clockwise (both forms). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.green_theorem_glued_region_with_holes`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.green_theorem_region_between_curves`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.green_glued`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.green_cells`
- **3:Key Equations 6 p.747**, p.747 [CA-646]: If F has C^2 components in space then div(curl F) = 0. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.divergence_of_curl_is_zero`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.div3_curl3`
- **3:Key Equations 6 p.747**, p.747 [CA-647]: If F is conservative (the gradient of f) then curl F = 0, i.e. curl(grad f) = 0 (also in the plane). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.curl_of_gradient_is_zero`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.curl2_grad`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.curl3_grad`
- **3:Key Equations 6 p.747**, p.747 [CA-654]: The scalar surface integral equals the double integral over the parameter domain of f(r(u,v)) \|r_u x r_v\| dA. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.scalar_surface_integral_formula`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.scalarSurfaceIntegral`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.scalarSurfaceIntegral_one`
- **3:Key Equations 6 p.747**, p.747 [CA-657]: With parameterization r, the flux of F across S equals the double integral over D of F(r(u,v))·(r_u x r_v) dA. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.flux_parameterized_surface`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.surfaceFlux`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.surfaceFlux_eq_unitNormal`
- **3:Key Equations 6 p.747**, p.747 [CA-658]: Stokes' theorem: for a piecewise smooth oriented surface S whose boundary is a positively oriented simple closed curve C and a C^1 field F, the circulation of F along C equals the flux of curl F across S. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.stokes_theorem_glued_surface`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.stokes_theorem_param_surface`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.stokes_glued`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.stokes_patch`
- **3:Key Equations 6 p.747**, p.747 [CA-661]: Divergence theorem: for a closed piecewise smooth outward-oriented surface S enclosing a solid E and a C^1 field F, the flux of F across S equals the triple integral of div F over E. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.divergence_theorem_glued_solid`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.divergence_theorem_param_solid`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.divergence_glued`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.divergence_cell`
- **3:Key Concepts 6.3 p.748**, p.748 [CA-627]: If F is conservative then its circulation along every closed curve is zero. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.conservative_field_closed_curve_zero`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsConservativeOn.lineIntegral_closed`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsConservative2.lineIntegral2_closed`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsConservative3.lineIntegral3_closed`
- **3:Key Concepts 6.3 p.748**, p.748 [CA-631]: On an open simply connected region, F is conservative if and only if its cross partials are equal (P_y=Q_x in the plane; in space also P_z=R_x and Q_z=R_y). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.conservative_iff_cross_partials_equal`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.isConservative2_iff_pdy_eq_pdx`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.pdy2_eq_pdx2_of_isConservative2`, `second_derivative_symmetric`
- **3:Key Concepts 6.4 p.748**, p.748 [CA-636]: On a simply connected domain these are equivalent for a field F: flux across any closed curve is zero; flux is path independent; F has a stream function; P_x+Q_y=0. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.source_free_equivalent_conditions`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.sourceFree_tfae`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.IsFluxPathIndependent2`
- **3:Key Concepts 6.5 p.749**, p.749 [CA-643]: On a simply connected domain, a continuous field with differentiable components is source free if and only if div F = 0. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.source_free_iff_div_zero`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.isSourceFree2_iff_div2_eq_zero`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.div2_eq_zero_of_isSourceFree2`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.exists_isStreamFunction_of_div2_eq_zero`
- **3:Key Concepts 6.5 p.749**, p.749 [CA-648]: On a simply connected domain, a field with curl F = 0 is conservative; so F is conservative iff curl F = 0 (plane or space). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.conservative_iff_curl_zero`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.isConservative2_iff_curl2_eq_zero`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.isConservative3_iff_curl3_eq_zero`, `Convex.exists_forall_hasFDerivAt_of_fderiv_symmetric`
- **3:Key Concepts 6.8 p.749**, p.749 [CA-665]: Gauss' law: the flux of the electrostatic field across a closed surface is the enclosed charge divided by the electric constant. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.gauss_law_glued_solid`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.gauss_law_enclosed_or_not`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.gauss_law_glued_shell`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.gauss_law_glued_no_charge`

## Volume 3, Chapter 7

46 items; 44 with a Lean name; 40 proved proved here; 4 in Mathlib; 2 not a result.


#### Section 7.1

- **3:§7.1 Definition (linear, homogeneous, nonhomogeneous second-order equation) p.752**, p.752 [CA-666]: A second-order equation is linear when it has the form y'' + p(x)y' + q(x)y = f(x); it is homogeneous when f is identically zero and nonhomogeneous otherwise. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.linear_second_order_equation_definition`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.LinSol`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ConstSol`
- **3:Theorem 7.1 p.754**, p.754 [CA-667]: Any linear combination c1 y1 + c2 y2 of two solutions of a linear homogeneous equation is again a solution. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.superposition_principle`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.superposition`
- **3:§7.1 Definition (linear dependence of functions) p.755**, p.755 [CA-668]: Functions f1..fn are linearly dependent on an interval if some nontrivial combination vanishes identically there; otherwise they are linearly independent. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.linear_dependence_of_functions`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.LinDepOn`, `LinearIndependent`
- **3:Theorem 7.2 p.755**, p.755 [CA-669]: Two functions are linearly dependent exactly when one is identically zero or one is a constant multiple of the other. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.linearly_dependent_iff_constant_multiple`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.linDepOn_iff`, `LinearIndependent.pair_iff'`
- **3:Theorem 7.3 p.756**, p.756 [CA-670]: If y1, y2 are linearly independent solutions of a second-order linear homogeneous equation, then c1 y1 + c2 y2 is the general solution (every solution has this form). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.general_solution_linearly_independent`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.general_solution_of_independent`
- **3:§7.1 Definition (characteristic equation) p.757**, p.757 [CA-671]: For a y'' + b y' + c y = 0 with constant coefficients, the characteristic equation is a r^2 + b r + c = 0 (from trying y = e^(r x)). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.characteristic_equation`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.exp_solves_iff_characteristic`
- **3:§7.1 Distinct Real Roots (unnumbered) p.757**, p.757 [CA-672]: If the characteristic equation has distinct real roots r1, r2, the general solution is c1 e^(r1 x) + c2 e^(r2 x). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.solution_distinct_real_roots`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.general_solution_distinct_real_roots`
- **3:§7.1 Single Repeated Real Root (unnumbered) p.757**, p.757 [CA-673]: If the characteristic equation has a repeated real root r, the general solution is c1 e^(r x) + c2 x e^(r x). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.solution_repeated_real_root`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.general_solution_repeated_root`
- **3:§7.1 Euler's formula (recalled from earlier chapter) p.758**, p.758 [CA-387]: Euler's formula: e^(i x) = cos x + i sin x for real x. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.euler_formula`, `Complex.exp_mul_I`
- **3:§7.1 Complex Conjugate Roots (unnumbered) p.758**, p.758 [CA-674]: If the characteristic roots are complex conjugates a plus or minus b i, the general real solution is e^(a x)(c1 cos b x + c2 sin b x). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.solution_complex_conjugate_roots`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.general_solution_complex_roots`
- **3:Table 7.1 p.759**, p.759 [CA-672]: If the characteristic equation has distinct real roots r1, r2, the general solution is c1 e^(r1 x) + c2 e^(r2 x). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.solution_distinct_real_roots`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.general_solution_distinct_real_roots`
- **3:Table 7.1 p.759**, p.759 [CA-673]: If the characteristic equation has a repeated real root r, the general solution is c1 e^(r x) + c2 x e^(r x). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.solution_repeated_real_root`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.general_solution_repeated_root`
- **3:Table 7.1 p.759**, p.759 [CA-674]: If the characteristic roots are complex conjugates a plus or minus b i, the general real solution is e^(a x)(c1 cos b x + c2 sin b x). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.solution_complex_conjugate_roots`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.general_solution_complex_roots`
- **3:Example 7.6 p.759**, p.759 [CA-674]: If the characteristic roots are complex conjugates a plus or minus b i, the general real solution is e^(a x)(c1 cos b x + c2 sin b x). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.solution_complex_conjugate_roots`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.general_solution_complex_roots`
- **3:§7.1 boundary conditions / boundary-value problem (unnumbered) p.760**, p.760 [CA-675]: A boundary-value problem is a differential equation together with conditions on the solution at two different values of the variable. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.boundary_value_problem_examples`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ODEExtras.IsBVPSolution`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ODEExtras.bvp_sin_infinitely_many`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ODEExtras.bvp_sin_unique`

#### Section 7.2

- **3:§7.2 Definition (complementary equation, Eq. 7.3) p.764**, p.764 [CA-676]: For a nonhomogeneous linear equation, the associated homogeneous equation with right-hand side zero is called the complementary equation. — *not a result* — –
- **3:§7.2 Definition (particular solution) p.765**, p.765 [CA-677]: A particular solution is a solution containing no arbitrary constants. — *not a result* — –
- **3:Theorem 7.4 p.765**, p.765 [CA-678]: The general solution of y'' + p y' + q y = f is a particular solution plus the general solution of the complementary equation. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.general_solution_particular_plus_complementary`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.general_eq_particular_add_complementary`
- **3:Example 7.11 p.765**, p.765 [CA-678]: The general solution of y'' + p y' + q y = f is a particular solution plus the general solution of the complementary equation. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.general_solution_particular_plus_complementary`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.general_eq_particular_add_complementary`

- **3:§7.2 Strategy: Undetermined Coefficients p.768**, p.768 [CA-679]: Method of undetermined coefficients: when f is built from polynomials, exponentials, sines and cosines, guess a particular solution of the same form (table of guesses), multiplying by x as needed to avoid overlap with complementary solutions, then solve for the coefficients. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.undetermined_coefficients_guesses`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ODEExtras.undetermined_exp`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ODEExtras.undetermined_exp_resonant`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ODEExtras.undetermined_trig`

- **3:Example 7.15 p.771**, p.771 [CA-680]: Cramer's rule: a 2 by 2 linear system with nonzero determinant has solutions given by ratios of determinants. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.cramers_rule_two_by_two`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.cramers_rule_matrix`, `Matrix.cramer`, `Matrix.cramer_apply`
- **3:§7.2 Strategy: Variation of Parameters p.772**, p.772 [CA-681]: Variation of parameters: with independent complementary solutions y1, y2, a particular solution is u1 y1 + u2 y2 where u1' y1 + u2' y2 = 0 and u1' y1' + u2' y2' = f, solved by Cramer's rule and integration. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.variation_of_parameters_method`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.variation_of_parameters`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.variation_of_parameters_cramer`

#### Section 7.3

- **3:§7.3 Equations 7.5-7.6 (simple harmonic motion) p.776**, p.776 [CA-682]: An undamped spring-mass system m x'' + k x = 0 gives simple harmonic motion x = c1 cos(w t) + c2 sin(w t) with w = sqrt(k/m). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.simple_harmonic_motion_solutions`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.simple_harmonic_motion`
- **3:Example 7.17 p.777**, p.777 [CA-682]: An undamped spring-mass system m x'' + k x = 0 gives simple harmonic motion x = c1 cos(w t) + c2 sin(w t) with w = sqrt(k/m). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.simple_harmonic_motion_solutions`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.simple_harmonic_motion`
- **3:Theorem 7.5 p.778**, p.778 [CA-683]: c1 cos(w t) + c2 sin(w t) can be rewritten as A sin(w t + phi), with A = sqrt(c1^2 + c2^2) and phi determined by tan phi = c1/c2 in the correct quadrant. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.amplitude_phase_form`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.amplitude_phase`
- **3:Example 7.18 p.779**, p.779 [CA-683]: c1 cos(w t) + c2 sin(w t) can be rewritten as A sin(w t + phi), with A = sqrt(c1^2 + c2^2) and phi determined by tan phi = c1/c2 in the correct quadrant. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.amplitude_phase_form`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.amplitude_phase`
- **3:§7.3 Case 1 overdamped p.780**, p.780 [CA-684]: For m x'' + b x' + k x = 0 with b^2 > 4 m k (overdamped), the solution is a combination of two decaying real exponentials with no oscillation. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.overdamped_spring`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.overdamped`
- **3:Example 7.19 p.781**, p.781 [CA-684]: For m x'' + b x' + k x = 0 with b^2 > 4 m k (overdamped), the solution is a combination of two decaying real exponentials with no oscillation. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.overdamped_spring`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.overdamped`
- **3:§7.3 Case 2 critically damped p.782**, p.782 [CA-685]: For b^2 = 4 m k (critically damped) the solution is e^(r t)(c1 + c2 t) with r < 0, non-oscillating. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.critically_damped_spring`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.critically_damped`
- **3:§7.3 Case 3 underdamped p.782**, p.782 [CA-686]: For b^2 < 4 m k (underdamped) the solution is a decaying exponential times a combination of sine and cosine, so it oscillates with decreasing amplitude. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.underdamped_spring`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.underdamped`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.spring_solution_tendsto_zero`
- **3:Example 7.20 p.782**, p.782 [CA-685]: For b^2 = 4 m k (critically damped) the solution is e^(r t)(c1 + c2 t) with r < 0, non-oscillating. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.critically_damped_spring`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.critically_damped`
- **3:Example 7.21 p.783**, p.783 [CA-686]: For b^2 < 4 m k (underdamped) the solution is a decaying exponential times a combination of sine and cosine, so it oscillates with decreasing amplitude. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.underdamped_spring`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.underdamped`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.spring_solution_tendsto_zero`
- **3:§7.3 Forced vibrations (Eq. 7.8, transient and steady-state solutions) p.787**, p.787 [CA-687]: For a forced damped system m x'' + b x' + k x = f(t), the solution is a transient part (complementary solution, tending to 0) plus a steady-state part (a particular solution) governing long-term behavior; the natural frequency of the undamped system is w/(2 pi). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.forced_vibration_transient_steady_state`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.forced_vibration_transient`
- **3:Example 7.23 p.787**, p.787 [CA-687]: For a forced damped system m x'' + b x' + k x = f(t), the solution is a transient part (complementary solution, tending to 0) plus a steady-state part (a particular solution) governing long-term behavior; the natural frequency of the undamped system is w/(2 pi). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.forced_vibration_transient_steady_state`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.forced_vibration_transient`
- **3:§7.3 Equation 7.9 (RLC series circuit) p.789**, p.789 [CA-688]: The charge q on the capacitor of a series RLC circuit satisfies L q'' + R q' + q/C = E(t). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.rlc_series_circuit`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ODEExtras.rlc_iff_forcedSpring`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ODEExtras.rlc_solutions_differ_by_transient`
- **3:Example 7.24 p.790**, p.790 [CA-688]: The charge q on the capacitor of a series RLC circuit satisfies L q'' + R q' + q/C = E(t). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.rlc_series_circuit`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ODEExtras.rlc_iff_forcedSpring`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ODEExtras.rlc_solutions_differ_by_transient`

#### Section 7.4

- **3:§7.4 (uniqueness of power series, used in Example 7.25) p.793**, p.793 [CA-388]: If two power series agree as functions near a point, their coefficients are equal; hence in a power-series solution each coefficient of x^n must vanish separately. — *in Mathlib* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.power_series_coefficients_unique`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.power_series_zero_coefficients`, `HasFPowerSeriesAt.eq_formalMultilinearSeries`, `HasFPowerSeriesAt.eq_zero`
- **3:§7.4 Strategy: Finding Power Series Solutions p.793**, p.793 [CA-689]: Power series method: assume y = sum a_n x^n, differentiate term by term, substitute, re-index, equate coefficients of each power to get recursion for the a_n. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.power_series_solution_method`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ODEExtras.power_series_method`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ODEExtras.power_series_oscillator`, `HasFPowerSeriesAt.hasDerivAt`
- **3:Example 7.25 p.793**, p.793 [CA-689]: Power series method: assume y = sum a_n x^n, differentiate term by term, substitute, re-index, equate coefficients of each power to get recursion for the a_n. — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.power_series_solution_method`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ODEExtras.power_series_method`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.ODEExtras.power_series_oscillator`, `HasFPowerSeriesAt.hasDerivAt`
- **3:Example 7.26 p.797**, p.797 [CA-690]: The Bessel equation of order 0, x^2 y'' + x y' + x^2 y = 0, has a power series solution sum (-1)^n x^(2n) / (4^n (n!)^2) (up to constant multiple). — *proved proved here* — `UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.bessel_equation_order_zero_series_solution`, `UndergradKB.Calculus.J0_bessel_equation`, `Complex.besselJ`
